### [1] [LLM Agents Factory: Retrieval of Domain-Specific LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09934)
*Vitalii Belov,Artyom Sosedka,Andrey Sakhovskiy,Elizaveta Kovtun,Artyom Boyarskikh,Semen Budennyy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes LLM Agents Factory, a retrieval-based framework that builds domain-specific LLM agents from a large repository of predefined profiles, achieving higher accuracy and lower cost than dynamic agent generation or non-agent baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents improve performance via specialized roles but are costly and unstable when designed on-the-fly for each request, making them hard to deploy in industrial settings. The authors want a more efficient, controllable, and practical way to get the benefits of agents without expensive dynamic construction.

Method: They construct a repository of 20K+ predefined, Wikipedia-grounded, domain-specific agent profiles. Given a task, their framework either (1) retrieves the most relevant profile via semantic search, or (2) uses a distilled compact model fine-tuned to directly generate appropriate agent profiles. These agents are then used in a single-agent setting to solve benchmark tasks.

Result: On MMLU, BIG-bench, and BIG-bench Hard, the retrieval-based agent construction outperforms non-agent baselines in accuracy and matches the performance of an AutoGen-style system using a 120B-parameter backbone, but with substantially lower inference cost. This demonstrates that static retrieval-based agents can maintain high quality while being more efficient.

Conclusion: A structured repository of predefined agents, combined with retrieval or distilled agent generation, offers a cost-efficient, accurate, and controllable alternative to dynamic, per-request agent construction. This approach better meets industrial requirements for stability and efficiency, and the authors release both code and the agent base for public use.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents improve task performance by decomposing problems into role-specialized behaviors. However, their practical deployment is often limited by the computational cost and instability associated with the on-the-fly agent design for each user request. To address this, we present LLM Agents Factory, a retrieval-based framework that constructs domain-specific and Wikipedia-grounded agents on demand using a base of over 20K predetermined agent profiles. Our framework supports two modes: (1) agent profile retrieval via semantic search and (2) distillation into a compact model fine-tuned for direct agent generation. Experiments on MMLU, BIG-bench, and BIG-bench Hard in a single-agent scenario demonstrate that our retrieval-based agent construction surpasses non-agent baselines in accuracy while matching AutoGen generation quality with a 120B backbone at a substantially lower inference cost. Our work reveals that retrieval from a structured agent repository provides a cost-efficient, accurate, and controllable alternative to dynamic agent generation, responding to the strict demands of industrial applications. We provide the implementation code and the agent base in https://huggingface.co/frontier-ai/llm-agent-factory.

</details>


### [2] [Conflict or Strategy? Asymmetric Role Framing of La France insoumise and Rassemblement National in French News Headlines, 2022-2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09936)
*Amr Sobhy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses a large LLM-annotated corpus of French news headlines about two populist parties to show that media mainly differ in how they assign political roles (conflict vs strategic actors) rather than in simple positive/negative tone, and proposes a reliability-aware framework for using LLM pipelines in framing analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work often treats left- and right-populist actors as either symmetric ‘extremes’ or as qualitatively different, but evidence from media coverage is mixed and usually based on small or hand-coded datasets. Framing research also tends to collapse multiple dimensions into coarse valence categories and lacks clear reliability standards when using LLMs as annotators. The authors want to clarify whether French media portray LFI and RN symmetrically or asymmetrically, disentangle role-based framing from simple valence, and provide a methodologically robust way to use LLMs for large-scale framing analysis.

Method: They collect 28,592 headlines about LFI and RN from 25 French-language outlets (2022–2025). They design a three-model LLM annotation pipeline that labels headlines along several dimensions: conflict framing, strategic-game framing, actor roles (e.g., aggressor), and normative judgments (legitimacy, blame, victimhood). The pipeline’s outputs are validated against a stratified human audit. They examine stability across models, time, and outlets, and use bootstrapping and permutation tests to assess robustness. They then analyze how often each party appears under different frames, roles, and moral-accounting patterns, and evaluate reliability separately for each construct.

Result: They find strong role asymmetry: LFI is more often framed in a conflict register, while RN is more often framed in a strategic-electoral register. This direction of role difference is stable across all three LLM models, across years, and across outlet families, and is statistically robust to bootstrapping and permutation tests. Conflict and strategic-game frames show higher cross-model stability and human validation than actor-role labels, which in turn are more reliable than normative-judgment constructs like legitimacy and blame. Moral-accounting patterns (who is blamed or legitimized) vary more by outlet than by party, producing aggregate null results that mask highly polarized behavior in specific outlets.

Conclusion: French headlines do not simply treat LFI and RN as symmetric extreme actors; instead, they assign different political roles, with LFI more frequently in conflict frames and RN more in strategic-electoral frames. Valence alone misses this systematic role asymmetry. Methodologically, certain framing constructs (conflict/strategic-game) are much more reliably captured by LLM annotation than normative judgments, so researchers should adopt construct-stratified reliability standards and treat some labels as corroborating rather than primary evidence. The paper positions political-role assignment as a key target for computational framing research and provides a calibrated framework for using majority-vote LLM pipelines in political text analysis.

Abstract: Do French news headlines frame left- and right-populist challengers as symmetric ``extremes,'' or as fundamentally different political adversaries? We examine 28,592 headlines about La France insoumise (LFI) and Rassemblement National (RN) published by 25 French-language outlets between 2022 and 2025, annotated through a three-model LLM pipeline validated against a stratified human audit. The clearest finding is role asymmetry rather than valence asymmetry: conflict framing and strategic-game framing are more robust across models and time than delegitimization, with AGGRESSOR serving as corroborating role syntax. LFI appears in headlines more often through a conflict register and RN through a strategic-electoral register. This role gap is direction-stable across all three annotation models, survives bootstrapping and permutation tests, and persists across outlet families and most of 2022-2025. A secondary moral-accounting layer (who is blamed, legitimized, or cast as a victim) is structured by outlet rather than party, producing aggregate nulls that conceal some of the corpus's most polarized patterns. Methodologically, the annotation pipeline reveals a two-tier reliability profile: conflict and strategic-game framing achieve the strongest human validation and cross-model stability; actor role is direction-stable but treated as corroborating because its audit reliability is lower; normative-judgment constructs (legitimacy, blame) are weaker. The paper contributes political-role assignment as a target for computational framing research that decomposes what valence-based measures conflate, and establishes a construct-stratified reliability framework for calibrating majority-vote LLM annotation pipelines in political text tasks.

</details>


### [3] [Carefully Considering Culture: Analyzing LLM Alignment in Single- and Multi-Cultural Settings using Cultural Consensus Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09937)
*Krishna Pothugunta,John P. Lalor*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses cultural consensus theory to evaluate how well large language models capture nuanced cultural norms, showing that models often misrepresent within-country cultural variation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on cultural knowledge in large language models usually treats cultures as homogeneous distributions within countries, overlooking internal diversity, group consensus levels, and multicultural subgroups. The authors want a principled way to capture and evaluate this nuance so that we can distinguish between genuine reflection of human diversity and artifacts of model behavior.

Method: They adopt cultural consensus theory (CCT) from cultural anthropology and apply it to responses from the World Values Survey (WVS), covering 12 value domains across 10 countries. Using CCT, they model consensus and intra-group variance and then compare these structures to the cultural representations inferred from large language models, analyzing when models show coherent consensus, lack it, or over-regularize it.

Result: The analysis shows that large language models often fail to match the true cultural structures observed in human survey data. In some cases, models do not form a cohesive consensus where one exists among people; in other cases, they impose an overly uniform consensus, ignoring substantial human intra-group variation.

Conclusion: Cultural consensus theory enables more precise, multidimensional evaluation of cultural knowledge in large language models. By explicitly representing intra-group variance, CCT yields diagnostics that clarify whether model outputs correspond to real human diversity or to algorithmic homogenization, and it highlights systematic misrepresentations of cultural norms by current models.

Abstract: Recent work in NLP has probed large language models for their understanding of cultural norms across countries. However, this work typically considers distributional patterns, ignoring group consensus or possible multicultural environments within a country. In this work, we leverage cultural consensus theory (CCT) from cultural anthropology to model such multidimensional nuance. Applying CCT to the World Values Survey (WVS) across 10 countries and 12 domains, we demonstrate that models frequently misrepresent cultural structures by either failing to form cohesive consensus or severely over-regularizing consensus. Through explicit representation of intra-group variance, CCT provides actionable diagnostics to evaluate when models reflect true human diversity versus algorithmic homogenization.

</details>


### [4] [The Multilingual Quantization Tax: Structural Collapse and Typological Fragility in Edge SLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09941)
*Mohammad Wathiq Soualhi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how 4-bit weight quantization affects multilingual performance of small language models, revealing that quantization amplifies existing cross-lingual inequalities and behaves very differently across languages, domains, and architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most quantization studies focus on English and overlook how compression affects other languages, especially low-resource or non-Latin-script languages, even though small language models are intended for global, edge-device deployment. The authors want to understand the “quantization tax” in a multilingual setting and uncover whether parameter truncation disproportionately harms certain languages or domains.

Method: They perform a zero-shot multilingual evaluation of 4-bit weight quantization on two popular SLM families (Gemma 4 and Qwen 3.5). Using the MMLU ProX Lite and GlobalPIQA benchmarks across eight typologically diverse languages, they compare pre- and post-quantization performance. They examine architecture-specific behavior and analyze patterns of degradation to characterize different failure modes and robustness regimes under quantization.

Result: They find that 4-bit quantization exposes and amplifies pre-existing inequalities from pre-training. Several phenomena emerge: (1) low-resource and some non-Latin-script languages can suffer severe representational collapse, sometimes failing to produce valid logits at all, with patterns that depend on the model architecture; (2) even languages that are central to a model’s pre-training (“home” languages) are not strongly protected from precision loss; (3) tasks involving multi-step, cross-lingual reasoning degrade more strongly than associative recall in soft-science domains; and (4) some saturated, well-aligned language–domain pairs show notable resistance to degradation, and any observed post-quantization gains in these cases are within the range of statistical noise rather than true improvements.

Conclusion: 4-bit weight quantization is not linguistically neutral: it can cause severe, architecture-dependent failures in low-resource and certain script families, and it differentially harms complex cross-lingual reasoning compared to simpler associative recall. The supposed robustness of “home” languages is limited, and apparent quantization gains in well-represented domains are likely illusory. Therefore, multilingual and typologically-aware evaluation is essential when deploying quantized small language models, particularly for equitable performance across languages.

Abstract: While 4-bit weight quantization is critical for deploying Small Language Models (SLMs) on edge devices, evaluations of the resulting performance degradation-the quantization tax-remain overwhelmingly English-centric. We present a zero-shot multilingual evaluation of 4-bit quantization across the Gemma 4 and Qwen 3.5 architectures. Evaluating on eight typo-logically diverse languages using MMLU ProX Lite and GlobalPIQA, we show parameter truncation exposes deep pre-training inequalities. We identify four phenomena: (1) Typological Fragility: low-resource and specific non-Latin scripts suffer representational collapse via architecture-specific double dissociations, failing to generate valid task logits; (2) Home Language Fragility Paradox: foundational pre-training pathways provide limited precision loss protection; (3) Domain-Specific Forgetting: multi-step cross-lingual routing degrades while associative soft-science recall remains robust; and (4) Quantization Resistance: highly saturated, typologically aligned domains resist deterministic degradation, with post-quantization performance gains bounded by statistical noise.

</details>


### [5] [When Chain-of-Thought Helps and When It Hurts: An Empirical Investigation of the Serial-Depth Bottleneck in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09942)
*Tughanbulut Kurtulush*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests when chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting actually helps LLM reasoning and finds it mainly helps on tasks that require deep serial computation, acting as a bandwidth bypass rather than a universal booster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many believe CoT prompting broadly improves reasoning in large language models, but this assumption lacked a principled, architecture-grounded analysis that distinguishes when CoT is necessary, helpful, or redundant.

Method: Using the H_dp bandwidth bound as a conceptual lens, the authors study how model performance changes with the serial depth of reasoning required by different benchmark tasks, comparing single-pass (no-CoT) vs CoT prompting across three instruction-tuned models (Qwen-2.5-7B/32B, Llama-3.1-8B) and five benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, MMLU, ARC, HumanEval), and run statistical tests including Spearman correlation and McNemar tests; the study was pre-registered on OSF.

Result: They find a within-benchmark serial-depth gradient where no-CoT accuracy drops as serial depth increases, while CoT performance stays roughly constant; CoT yields large gains on deep P-complete tasks like GSM8K and MATH (+54 to +68 percentage points), is mostly redundant on shallow TC^0 tasks like MMLU and ARC (0 to +4.6 points), and shows mixed, size-dependent effects on intermediate L-class tasks like HumanEval; overall depth-recovery correlation is Spearman rho = 0.661 with many benchmark-level results statistically significant after correction.

Conclusion: CoT prompting is not a universal enhancer of reasoning in LLMs but functions as a bandwidth bypass for serial computations that exceed a transformer’s single-pass capacity, providing substantial benefits on high-depth tasks and little to no benefit on tasks whose computational depth already fits within that capacity.

Abstract: It is widely assumed that chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting universally improves LLM reasoning. We investigate this through the conceptual framework of the H_dp bandwidth bound (Chen et al., 2024): although the formal bound binds only asymptotically (at astronomically large prompt lengths), it identifies a real architectural bottleneck -- serial computation exceeding a transformer's single-pass capacity must be externalised, which is what CoT does. Our central finding is a within-benchmark serial-depth gradient: single-pass (no-CoT) accuracy degrades monotonically with per-item serial depth, while CoT is approximately depth-invariant. We measure CoT effects across three instruction-tuned models (Qwen-2.5-7B/32B, Llama-3.1-8B) and five standard NLP benchmarks at practical context lengths. On high-depth P-complete tasks (GSM8K, MATH), CoT gives a +54 to +68 pp recovery gap across all models. On shallow TC^0 tasks (MMLU, ARC), CoT is structurally redundant (Delta in [0.0, +4.6] pp, no significant negative effect) -- though high no-CoT baselines (up to 95% on ARC) may reflect contamination, so this null is not a clean architectural test. The intermediate class L (HumanEval) shows a model-size-dependent transition: +23.2 pp (32B), +9.1 pp (8B), -28.7 pp (7B). The cross-benchmark depth-recovery correlation is Spearman rho = 0.661 (p = 0.007, n = 15); 9 of 15 benchmark-level McNemar tests are significant after Bonferroni correction. Pre-registered on OSF, our results indicate that CoT is not a universal reasoning enhancer but acts as a bandwidth bypass: it helps serial computation that strains single-pass capacity and is redundant for tasks that already fit.

</details>


### [6] [Position Encoding in Transformers: From Absolute and Relative Methods to Rotary Position Embeddings and Long-Context Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10021)
*Jiguo Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper is a technical survey of positional encoding methods in Transformers, focusing on how they model token order, how they scale to long contexts, and how they behave beyond training sequence lengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers rely on self-attention, which is order-agnostic by default. To be useful for sequences like text, they need mechanisms to encode token positions. Many positional encoding methods and long-context extensions have been proposed, but their relationships, trade-offs, and practical implications for long-context generalization are scattered. The paper aims to provide a unified conceptual and mathematical treatment to clarify how these methods work and how they compare in practice.

Method: The authors conceptually and mathematically analyze a wide range of positional encoding schemes and long-context extensions. They derive the mechanics of each approach (especially RoPE), compare design dimensions such as where position is injected, computational cost, KV-cache compatibility, and length extrapolation, and review implementation details and evaluation protocols used in large language models. They specifically examine scaling methods for RoPE and related techniques via an NTK (neural tangent kernel)-informed lens.

Result: The survey systematizes positional encoding techniques (absolute, relative, and rotary) and long-context adaptations (e.g., Position Interpolation, various RoPE scaling schemes). It clarifies how RoPE converts absolute indices into relative phase differences in attention, outlines trade-offs across methods, and distills common patterns in frequency allocation, rescaling strategies, and practical deployment. It also identifies gaps between theoretical extrapolation ability and empirically verified long-context performance.

Conclusion: Positional encodings that mathematically support extrapolation beyond training lengths do not guarantee robust long-context generalization. Reliable long-context behavior requires careful method choice, implementation, and training, and must be empirically validated with targeted evaluations such as short-context retention, position-wise perplexity, retrieval, reasoning, and long-context code tasks. The survey provides guidance on these choices and highlights that good positional features are necessary but not sufficient for strong long-context LLM performance.

Abstract: Self-attention models content-dependent interactions between tokens but does not by itself encode token order. Position encoding addresses this limitation by introducing absolute coordinates, relative distances, or position-dependent rotations into Transformer representations and attention scores. This technical survey develops a unified account of sinusoidal and learned absolute position embeddings, Shaw-style relative position representations, Transformer-XL, T5 relative position bias, ALiBi, and Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE). We derive how RoPE converts absolute position indices into relative phase differences in Query-Key inner products and compare these methods in terms of where position is injected, computational cost, compatibility with KV caching, and length extrapolation. We then examine long-context extensions, including Position Interpolation, RoPE scaling laws, NTK-aware scaling, Dynamic NTK, NTK-by-parts, YaRN, LongRoPE, and LongRoPE2, with emphasis on frequency allocation, attention rescaling, training length, and target context length. We also summarize implementation considerations, evaluation protocols, and position-encoding choices in representative large language models. A central conclusion is that the ability to compute positional features beyond the training length does not imply reliable long-context generalization; context extension must be evaluated through short-context retention, position-wise perplexity, retrieval, reasoning, and long-context code tasks.

</details>


### [7] [PERCEPT: A Corpus for POS Tagging and Analysis of Persian-English Code-Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10109)
*Ghazal Kalhor,Zahra Jafari,Amirarsalan Shahbazi,Behnam Bahrak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PERCEPT, the first large-scale Persian-English code-mixed social media corpus annotated with Universal Dependencies POS tags, built with an LLM-assisted annotation framework and used for linguistic analysis of code-mixing behavior across platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persian-English code-mixing in social media is common but underexplored compared to other language pairs. Existing Persian resources lack UD POS-annotated data for code-mixed content, which hinders linguistic research and the development of syntax-aware NLP models capable of handling mixed-language input.

Method: The authors collect 6,800 posts from multiple platforms (X, Instagram, Digikala) and construct PERCEPT, a code-mixed corpus with UD POS annotations specifically for code-mixed words. They design an LLM-assisted pipeline that automatically assigns POS tags and document-level topics, then validate annotation quality via human evaluation against gold-standard labels. Using the resulting corpus, they perform quantitative analyses of POS category distributions, positional patterns of code-mixed tokens, and triggering effects across platforms.

Result: PERCEPT provides high-quality UD POS annotations for Persian-English code-mixed words, with human evaluators showing high agreement with the LLM-assisted annotations. The analysis shows that nouns dominate among code-mixed words, that other POS categories differ in frequency across platforms, that the positions where code-mixed words appear are highly consistent across platforms, and that the triggering effect of code-mixing is notably stronger in Digikala than in other platforms.

Conclusion: PERCEPT fills a key resource gap for Persian-English code-mixed NLP by offering a large, publicly available, UD-annotated corpus. The LLM-assisted annotation framework is reliable and scalable, and the initial analyses reveal systematic patterns in how and where code-mixing occurs across social media platforms, enabling future linguistic and modeling advances.

Abstract: Social media has become a major venue for multilingual communication, where users frequently mix multiple languages within a single utterance. Although code-mixed corpora have been developed for several language pairs, Persian-English code-mixing remains relatively underexplored. Existing Persian resources lack Universal Dependencies (UD) part-of-speech (POS) annotations for code-mixed words, limiting both linguistic analyses and the development of syntax-aware NLP models. To address this gap, we introduce PERCEPT, the first publicly available large-scale Persian-English code-mixed corpus annotated with Universal Dependencies POS tags for code-mixed words. The dataset comprises 6,800 posts collected from X, Instagram, and Digikala. We further present an LLM-assisted annotation framework that automatically assigns POS tags and document-level topics. Human evaluation demonstrates high agreement between the automatically generated annotations and gold annotations, confirming the reliability of the annotations. Using PERCEPT, we conduct the first comprehensive linguistic analysis of Persian-English code-mixing across multiple social media platforms. Our analyses reveal that nouns are the predominant category for code-mixed words, while the distributions of other POS categories vary across platforms. We further find that the positional distribution of code-mixed words is remarkably consistent across platforms, whereas the triggering effect is substantially more pronounced in Digikala. PERCEPT is publicly available at https://github.com/kalhorghazal/PERCEPT.

</details>


### [8] [The Parser Already Knows: Lightweight Bias Correction in Constrained Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10137)
*Işıl Özgü,Yaoxuan Wu,Guy Van den Broeck,Miryung Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They improve grammar-constrained decoding for language models by correcting masked logits using parser/lexer states, restoring the original probability distribution without expensive online resampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard grammar-constrained decoding enforces syntactic validity by masking invalid tokens, but this masking distorts the language model’s probability distribution and can hurt output quality. Online sampling can recover the true distribution but is slow due to repeated resampling. There is a need for a method that enforces grammar constraints while preserving the LM’s probabilistic behavior and keeping inference efficient.

Method: They observe that incremental parsing already maintains internal parser and lexer states that encode which future tokens are grammatically valid. They train a lightweight offline logit-correction model that takes as input these syntactic/lexical states and candidate next tokens, and outputs corrections to the masked logits. The base LM is frozen; the correction layer is conditioned on parsing state and optionally only on the candidate token itself in a lighter variant. This is used at inference to adjust the masked distribution so it better matches the LM’s original distribution, without iterative resampling.

Result: Across multiple grammars, the corrected logits significantly reduce the divergence between the grammar-masked distribution and the LM’s original unconstrained distribution. The method consistently yields higher-quality outputs than both plain masking and online sampling, while maintaining low overhead. Even the simplest variant that only conditions on the candidate token (without full parser state) performs on par with or better than the baselines.

Conclusion: Using parser and lexer state for logit correction effectively restores probability mass removed by grammar masking, reconciling strict grammar conformance with the language model’s original probabilistic preferences. This offers a practical way to get both high-quality and syntactically valid generations without incurring the latency costs of online resampling, and shows that even minimal token-level conditioning can provide useful lookahead-like signals for resolving grammatical ambiguity.

Abstract: Grammar Constrained Decoding (GCD) forces Language Models (LMs) to produce syntactically valid outputs by masking out non-conforming tokens at each step. However, rigid masking distorts the model's underlying probability distribution, often biasing generation toward valid but suboptimal outputs. While online sampling restores this distribution, it requires computationally expensive iterative resampling. As a result, existing methods force a compromise between output quality and inference latency. Our key insight is that the internal parser and lexer states inherently maintained during incremental parsing already encode future grammatical validity -- exactly the information required to restore the LM's true distribution. We propose a lightweight, offline-trained logit correction conditioned on this syntactic and lexical state together with candidate next tokens. Because these states are already computed as a necessary part of incremental parsing for masking, extracting them adds negligible overhead while leaving the base LM's weights completely untouched. Across several grammars, this correction substantially closes the gap between the masked distribution and the LM's true distribution, consistently outperforming both masking and online sampling. Even its lightest variant, which relies on the candidate next token alone, still matches or exceeds both baselines: the next token itself carries an implicit lookahead, much like how parsers commonly use a lookahead token to resolve ambiguous decisions. By restoring the probability mass that masking removes, it reconciles the LM's probabilistic integrity with grammar conformance.

</details>


### [9] [Multimodal Item Parameter Estimation using Simulated Response Probabilitie](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10154)
*Christopher Ormerod,YoungKoung Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses a fine-tuned multimodal LLM to reconstruct item response theory (IRT) curves (3PL and multiple-choice model) from observed student option choices, enabling direct estimation of item difficulty from model-predicted choice probabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard IRT models like 3PL and MCM require explicit psychometric modeling and parameter estimation from large-scale student response data. The authors aim to see whether a modern multimodal LLM, trained on rich item content and labeled ability levels, can implicitly learn and reproduce these psychometric response curves, thereby offering an alternative and potentially more flexible way to estimate item characteristics such as difficulty without manual IRT calibration.

Method: They fine-tune a Qwen3.5-based multimodal LLM on a large corpus of multiple-choice questions that may include both text and image stimuli. Training examples include item content, the response options, and a labeled discrete ability level for the student. The model is trained (via prompting and fine-tuning) to output the full probability distribution over options for each ability level, effectively learning systematic error patterns across abilities. From these predicted choice probabilities, they reconstruct the underlying response curves corresponding to the 3PL and multiple-choice models, and then evaluate how well these reconstructions match traditional IRT-based estimates on a held-out test set.

Result: The fine-tuned LLM successfully reproduces the response probability patterns characteristic of 3PL and MCM across a discrete set of ability levels. Its predicted option probabilities on unseen items allow accurate approximation of item difficulty, as validated on a held-out test set. This indicates that the LLM has implicitly learned the psychometric structure encoded by traditional IRT models from student response data.

Conclusion: A multimodal LLM fine-tuned on student response patterns can implicitly encode and reconstruct classic IRT response curves (3PL and MCM). This capability enables direct estimation of item difficulty from model-predicted choice probabilities, suggesting that LLMs can serve as powerful, flexible psychometric engines that approximate or complement traditional IRT calibration, especially for new items involving rich text and image content.

Abstract: We present results from reconstructing multiple-choice model (MCM) and three-parameter logistic (3PL) model curves using a fine-tuned multimodal large language model (LLM) based on Qwen3.5. The model is prompted and fine-tuned to replicate choice probabilities across a large training corpus of multiple-choice items containing both image and text stimuli, conditioned on a labeled set of student ability levels. By learning to reproduce the systematic error patterns of students across a discrete range of abilities, the LLM implicitly captures the underlying response probabilities encoded in the 3PL and MCM curves. This allows us to accurately approximate item difficulty on a held-out test set directly from the model's predicted option probabilities.

</details>


### [10] [Similarity Gates Approve Reversals: A Validity Audit of Embedding-Cosine Thresholds in Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10216)
*Scott E. Frias*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that using embedding cosine similarity as a fixed-threshold ‘meaning preservation’ gate is systematically flawed: it mostly measures wording change, not semantic change, and can invert safety and evaluation decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many agent and LLM systems use embedding cosine similarity to decide if two texts mean the same thing—for safety drift guards, deduplication, semantic caches, and graders. These gates are widely deployed but treated as if they reliably detect semantic equivalence, without a careful audit of what they actually measure and how they behave on meaning-breaking edits, especially in safety-critical contexts.

Method: The authors audit cosine-similarity-based gates as measurement instruments. They test real production drift guards and multiple shipped operating points across configurations, thresholds, and tasks. They construct adversarial and controlled corpora that distinguish small wording changes from true meaning reversals, including single-word instruction flips like ‘withhold’ vs ‘administer’. They evaluate detection of meaning-breaking mutations and compute metrics such as balanced accuracy and AUROC under both naive corpora and a balanced 2x2 design. They also try fixes: swapping encoders, overlap-conditioned gating, and substituting NLI models, and analyze overlap-matched reversal vs paraphrase discrimination.

Result: The audited production drift guard failed to detect any of 56 meaning-breaking mutations and even approved a critical reversal (‘withhold the study drug’ → ‘administer the study drug’) at a very high cosine similarity (0.9608). Across five observed operating points and 90 configuration–threshold–task cells, balanced accuracy never exceeded 0.700 and had a median of 0.525, barely above chance. Naively constructed evaluation corpora inherit the same confounder, sometimes giving fully inverted performance (decision AUROC 0.000 in 13/18 cells, ≤0.040 in all 18) while a properly balanced 2x2 design for the same setups yields AUROC 0.440–0.815. Attempts at obvious fixes—encoder swaps, overlap-conditioned gates (0.750 in-sample, 0.533 held-out), and NLI drop-ins—failed to generalize beyond chance on separately authored held-out data. However, when controlling for textual overlap, the best two of nine configurations could separate reversals from paraphrases with AUROC 0.79–0.90.

Conclusion: Embedding cosine similarity gates, as commonly deployed, measure wording changes rather than semantic preservation and can systematically fire in the wrong direction, undermining both safety mechanisms and evaluations. Naive corpus construction further corrupts assessment, even to the point of inverted AUROC. Simple fixes like swapping encoders or adding overlap conditions are insufficient, and NLI substitutions do not readily solve the problem. Nonetheless, embeddings retain potential: with careful, overlap-matched audits and design, it appears possible to build a valid instrument. The paper releases its corpus, harness, and frozen results, and argues that future systems must explicitly validate what their similarity-based gates are actually measuring before deployment.

Abstract: Agent frameworks ship quality gates that compare text blocks by embedding-cosine similarity and decide at a fixed cutoff. Deduplication filters, semantic caches, drift guards, and answer grader gates deploy to answer the question: "Does this text still mean the same thing?" But the score answers a different question: "How much did the wording change?" We audit this gate class as a measurement instrument. In the cases these gates exist to catch, the two can run in opposite ways. Many times, reversing an instruction is a single word edit, while agreement often rephrases a sentence. The consequence is a safety check that fires backwards. The production drift guard we audited caught 0 of 56 meaning-breaking mutations, and one approved item, "withhold the study drug" -> "administer the study drug", came in at cosine 0.9608. We observed five shipped operating points, and balanced accuracy across 90 configuration-threshold-task cells never exceeded 0.700 (median 0.525). The same confounder also corrupted evaluations. A naively built corpus inherits this confounder and can return an inverted verdict, with a decision AUROC exactly 0.000 in 13 of 18 configuration-task cells (at most 0.040 in all 18) against 0.440-0.815 for the same nine configurations under a balanced 2x2 design. Twice in the effort it captured our own headline claims. Obvious repairs fail: an encoder swap and an overlap-conditioned gate (0.750 in-sample, 0.533 held-out) land at chance on separately authored held-out data, and an NLI drop-in did no better. Embeddings do still bear hope here, as the strongest two of nine configurations separated reversal from paraphrase at matched overlap (AUROC 0.79-0.90), but only a matched-pair audit reveals the deployment regime. We release the corpus method, harness, and frozen results, and contend that scores gated this way measure the wrong thing. We believe a valid instrument is buildable.

</details>


### [11] [Off-Axis, On Purpose: Where a Transformer Computes Concepts and Why it Does So](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10251)
*Mark Oskin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how transformer representations relate geometrically to the unembedding/read-out direction and shows that off-read-out subspaces form a functional ‘concept workspace’ mostly orthogonal to the output axis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability work often focuses on components aligned with the read-out (unembedding) direction, implicitly treating off-axis directions as noise or obstacles. The authors want to understand whether these off-axis components are actually functionally important, how transformers organize their internal computation geometrically, and how flexible that geometry is without hurting model quality.

Method: Analyze a 12-layer transformer’s internal activations and attention value vectors relative to the unembedding/read-out direction. Measure angles between attention/value subspaces and the read-out, and test functional importance by rotating or forcing projections onto the read-out axis and comparing performance drop, especially separating cross-token mixing from vocabulary interaction. Modify training to press every layer closer to the read-out (early-exit-style training), or explicitly prescribe geometric constraints such as nulling read-out projections or inserting fixed/sparse rotations at a phase boundary. Run multiple seeds and different fixed rotations, including random bases, and evaluate on perplexity, LAMBADA, and BLiMP.

Result: They find a clear two-phase computation. In the first phase (early to mid layers), attention value subspaces stay nearly orthogonal (75–96°) to the read-out direction, forming a ‘concept-phase’ workspace insulated from direct vocabulary interference; rotating values onto the read-out axis is 64–84× more harmful than a comparable random rotation, and the damage arises from disrupted cross-token mixing. The internal ‘frame’ of this workspace rotates rigidly with depth beneath this insulating subspace. In the second phase, the final answer appears on-axis (aligned with read-out) late and by additive read-out-aligned contributions rather than by gradually rotating the concept content. Forcing all layers towards the read-out (as in early-exit training) preserves perplexity and benchmark performance while shrinking the concept-phase effective dimensionality from ~25 to ~14, a change unseen by standard metrics. Directly prescribing read-out-null geometry via loss is unstable (6/8 runs collapse by discarding dimensions), but inserting a single fixed rotation at the phase boundary or using a sparse rotation that surrounding weights can absorb yields baseline-quality models with improved convergence across seeds. Performance is insensitive to the specific rotation: many different fixed rotations and seeds reach similar quality, and independently trained baselines use near-orthogonal concept frames but share similar read-outs. A randomly drawn basis prescribed before training is stably adopted, again without hurting quality.

Conclusion: Transformer computation is strongly organized by geometry: there is a robust, near-orthogonal ‘concept workspace’ separated from the read-out direction where cross-token composition occurs, followed by a late, additive projection onto the read-out axis. This off-read-out subspace is functional, not incidental. The particular basis of the concept phase is largely arbitrary: many distinct, even random, rotations work equally well and can be imposed a priori, showing substantial geometric freedom that standard benchmarks do not distinguish. Explicitly constraining geometry via losses can cause degenerate solutions, whereas implementing geometry through structural rotations is stable. Overall, internal representational geometry—especially the separation and rotation of subspaces—offers a powerful and flexible handle on how transformers compute, with implications for interpretability and for designing models whose internal spaces are more controllable.

Abstract: A transformer's answer lives on one axis: the direction its unembedding reads. Its intermediate states largely do not, and that off-axis position is usually treated as an obstacle to interpretation. We show it is functional. A 12-layer model computes in two phases. Through the first, every sublayer writes into a subspace held near-orthogonal to the read-out, attention 75 to 96 degrees off it at every depth. Moving attention's values onto the read-out is 64 to 84 times more damaging than a matched random rotation, and the damage is entirely in cross-token mixing: the subspace insulates composition from the vocabulary. Beneath it the frame itself turns rigidly with depth. In the second phase the answer arrives on-axis, late, and by addition rather than by turning accumulated content onto the read-out.
  Pressing every layer onto the read-out instead, as training for early exit does, matches the baseline on perplexity, LAMBADA and BLiMP while cutting the concept-phase workspace from about twenty-five effective dimensions to fourteen, a change none of those benchmarks register. The geometry can also be imposed, though not by asking for it. Prescribing it through the loss is a lottery: six of eight seeds collapse, because a model told to null its read-out projection obeys most cheaply by discarding dimensions. Inserting one fixed rotation at the phase boundary lands it instead, at baseline quality. A sparse rotation the surrounding weights can absorb converges on all nine seeds, against five of nine for ordinary training. Which rotation is immaterial: twenty-five runs across thirteen distinct ones reach the same quality, and two baselines from different seeds hold their concepts in near-orthogonal frames while agreeing on their read-outs. That freedom is usable: a basis drawn at random and prescribed before training is adopted across the concept phase, with quality unchanged.

</details>


### [12] [TAF-MED: Multi-Turn Safety Refusal Collapse in LLMs Under Declared Self-Treatment Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10258)
*Waleed Jamil,Raphael Schmitt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TAF-MED, a benchmark to evaluate whether large language models remain safe over multi-turn medical conversations, showing that most models eventually give unsafe medication advice even after a safe first response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety benchmarks largely focus on single-turn interactions and do not test whether LLMs maintain safe behavior after users explicitly state an intent to self-treat and continue the dialogue. This leaves a gap in understanding real-world risk, where users often ask follow-up questions and push for medication guidance.

Method: The authors build TAF-MED, a physician-reviewed dataset of 500 three-turn conversational medication-safety scenarios, yielding 4,000 model conversations across eight LLMs. They use a rubric-based automated judge to categorize each model reply as SAFE, LEAKY, or UNSAFE, and have two physicians independently annotate a random, model-balanced subset of 400 conversations. They then analyze rates of unsafe guidance, safety “collapse” after initially safe answers, the stability of model rankings across metrics, and validate the automated judge against the physician reference.

Result: 71.6% of conversations contained at least one UNSAFE response. Among conversations that started with a strictly SAFE first reply, 61.4% eventually “collapsed” to an UNSAFE answer in later turns; collapse rates by model ranged from 24.4% to 96.2%. In comparing models, 4 of 28 model pairs flipped rank order when comparing first-turn unsafe rates versus collapse rates, indicating unstable safety rankings if only the initial turn is considered. The automated judge labels matched the adjudicated physician reference with 94.3% agreement (κ = 0.895).

Conclusion: Single-turn safety evaluations substantially underestimate medical risk because many LLMs that begin safely later provide unsafe medication advice as conversations continue. Robust assessment of medical safety must therefore examine full multi-turn trajectories and explicit self-treatment intent. The validated TAF-MED benchmark will be released publicly to enable reproducible, multi-turn safety evaluation of medical LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly provide conversational health information that may influence treatment decisions, yet existing benchmarks do not isolate whether medication-safety boundaries persist across follow-ups after explicit self-treatment intent. We introduce TAF-MED, a physician-reviewed benchmark of 500 fixed three-turn scenarios, and evaluate eight LLMs across 4,000 conversations. A rubric-based automated judge labelled responses as SAFE, LEAKY, or UNSAFE, and two physicians independently annotated a model-balanced random subset of 400 conversations. We assessed unsafe guidance, collapse after a strictly SAFE initial response, and model-ranking stability. Overall, 71.6% of conversations contained an UNSAFE response, and 61.4% of those beginning with a strictly SAFE response later collapsed to UNSAFE; model-level collapse rates ranged from 24.4% to 96.2%. Four of 28 model pairs reversed order between initial unsafe and collapse rates. Automated labels achieved 94.3% agreement with the adjudicated physician reference ($κ= 0.895$). These findings show that first-turn safety is an incomplete proxy for conversational safety persistence and motivate evaluation across complete dialogue trajectories. We will release TAF-MED on Hugging Face to support reproducible research on multi-turn medical safety.

</details>


### [13] [Locally Deployable Small Language Models for Emergency Department Decision Support: A Systematic Benchmark of Fine-Tuning Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10273)
*Qingfeng Zhang,Yuanxiong Guo,Yanmin Gong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well open-source small language models, fine-tuned with different strategies, can support emergency department decision-making compared with commercial LLMs, showing that lightweight LoRA fine-tuning can outperform closed-source models on key tasks while preserving privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergency departments need decision support tools to help with triage, referrals, and diagnosis, but sending patient data to closed-source LLMs poses privacy risks, and it is unclear which fine-tuning strategies make locally deployable open-source models clinically competitive. The paper aims to fill this gap with a systematic benchmark.

Method: The authors benchmark eight open-source small language models on three ED tasks (triage level prediction, specialist referral recommendation, diagnosis prediction) using four approaches: zero-shot prompting, prefix tuning, LoRA, and full fine-tuning. They use 2,083 MIMIC-IV-ED clinical cases and compare performance against Claude Haiku 4.5 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 as commercial baselines, including confusion matrix analysis to study error patterns, especially for high-severity cases.

Result: LoRA-fine-tuned open-source SLMs outperform the commercial Claude baselines on triage level prediction and specialist referral recommendation, but still lag behind on diagnosis prediction. Error analysis via confusion matrices reveals that the fine-tuned SLMs can correctly identify some of the highest-severity patients that the commercial models miss.

Conclusion: With appropriate parameter-efficient fine-tuning (e.g., LoRA), locally deployable open-source small language models can match or exceed commercial LLMs on important emergency department decision-support tasks while keeping data on-premise. However, diagnosis prediction remains a harder problem, indicating room for further methodological and modeling advances.

Abstract: Deploying large language models (LLMs) for decision support in emergency departments (EDs) faces two major challenges: privacy risks of transmitting patient data to closed-source commercial LLMs and the lack of systematic evaluation of fine-tuning strategies for locally deployable open-source small language models (SLMs). We benchmarked eight open-source SLMs using zero-shot prompting, prefix tuning, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), and full fine-tuning on three ED tasks: triage level prediction, specialist referral recommendation, and diagnosis prediction. Using 2,083 MIMIC-IV-ED cases and Claude Haiku 4.5 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 as baselines, we found that LoRA fine-tuned open-source SLMs outperform commercial baselines on triage level prediction and specialist referral recommendation, while diagnosis prediction remains challenging for open-source SLMs. Confusion matrix analysis further shows that fine-tuned open-source SLMs can detect highest-severity patients missed by the commercial baselines. These results demonstrate that locally deployable SLMs can achieve clinically competitive performance for ED decision support.

</details>


### [14] [Cracks in the Foundation: Seemingly Minor Architectural Choices Impact Long Context Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10296)
*Amanda Bertsch,Luca Soldaini,Matthew R. Gormley,Graham Neubig,Hannaneh Hajishirzi,Kyle Lo,Dirk Groeneveld*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that seemingly small architectural choices in dense Transformers can significantly harm or improve long-context performance, even when short-context metrics look similar, and provides a systematic ablation study plus a public model suite (OlmPool) to analyze these effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context capability is increasingly important for real-world LLM applications, yet models with similar sizes and short-context performance often differ greatly in how well they extend to long contexts. Existing work does not clearly explain which architectural factors drive these differences, nor how to diagnose long-context issues early in pretraining. The paper aims to identify which small design choices within the standard dense Transformer architecture materially affect long-context extensibility and to provide a controlled, comparable set of models for studying this phenomenon.

Method: The authors perform controlled ablation studies over a family of 7B dense Transformer models. They keep data, tokenizer, and the long-context extension recipe fixed while systematically varying four architectural factors: normalization strategy, grouped-query attention (GQA) vs standard attention, pretraining context length, and use of sliding-window attention. They apply context extension early in pretraining, evaluate long-context downstream performance, and analyze attention patterns, including attention sinks and attention distributions across the sequence. The result is OlmPool, a pool of 26 7B models, each trained for substantial GPU hours with checkpoints before and after long-context extension, enabling direct comparisons across architectures.

Result: The study finds that each of the four architectural choices alone only slightly degrades long-context performance, but combinations—especially three or more—can reduce downstream long-context performance by as much as 47%. Crucially, these performance degradations are not visible in short-context loss or standard validation metrics. The authors show that much of the apparent variability in long-context ability across popular model families (e.g., Olmo, Llama, Qwen) can be attributed to these architectural decisions. They also provide a set of 26 trained 7B models (OlmPool) with standardized conditions, some of which outperform the Llama 3 architecture in terms of long-context extensibility.

Conclusion: Minor-looking architectural decisions in dense Transformer models have large, compounding effects on long-context extensibility, despite leaving short-context metrics nearly unchanged. Because these effects can be detected when context extension is applied early in pretraining, practitioners should account for and evaluate these architectural factors explicitly rather than relying solely on short-context validation. The released OlmPool model suite offers a controlled testbed for the community to study long-context behavior and shows that some alternative architectures surpass the Llama 3 design for long-context use cases. The analysis of attention sinks and attention distributions links specific attention pathologies to particular architectural choices, offering guidance for better long-context model design.

Abstract: One might imagine that architectural variations within the dense transformer paradigm have a limited effect on accuracy. However, we demonstrate that this is not the case in the long context setting. Specifically, we show that a set of four minor architectural decisions --- all made by at least one of the Olmo, Llama, and Qwen dense model families --- have a compoundingly negative effect on long context extensibility. Any one of these choices alone has a minor impact on long context performance, but combining three or more can drop the performance downstream by up to 47%. Furthermore, these differences are not detectable from short-context loss or validation datasets. We show that much of the variation in long context ability across model families is driven by these architectural features and detectable from applying context extension early in pretraining. We demonstrate this with controlled ablations that hold data, tokenizer, and extension recipe fixed while varying normalization, GQA, pretraining context length, and sliding window attention. After over 170,000 GPU hours of training, we release the resulting set of models as OlmPool, a set of 26 comparable 7B models with checkpoints before and after long-context extension. This pool includes several architectures that outperform the Llama 3 architecture on long context extensibility. In an analysis of our ablation models, we identify patterns in attention sink behavior and attention distributions across context that are attributable to specific architectural differences.

</details>


### [15] [Co-Evolution in Agentic Systems: Toward Self-Directed Evolution Beyond Human Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10299)
*Qing Zong,Jiayu Liu,Junhao Shen,Zecong Tang,Linsi Wu,Yuxuan Liu,Rui Wang,Zhaowei Wang,Weiqi Wang,Cheng Qian,Xiusi Chen,Yangqiu Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper surveys co-evolution in multi-agent, agentic systems, proposing a three-stage taxonomy (Agent–Agent, Agent–Environment, and Meta Co-Evolution) and discussing challenges in evaluation, scaling, and safety for open-ended, self-improving systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic systems are expected to self-improve after deployment but are usually limited by static learning setups, such as fixed tasks and feedback. There is a need to understand and organize how more open-ended, multi-component systems—where agents and environments evolve together—can be designed and studied. A unified framework is missing to connect disparate works in this emerging area.

Method: The authors conduct a survey of existing literature on agentic systems that exhibit co-evolution. They introduce a progressive, three-stage taxonomy that categorizes prior work based on how much human-engineered constraints are relaxed: (1) Agent–Agent Co-Evolution, (2) Agent–Environment Co-Evolution, and (3) Meta Co-Evolution. For each stage, they group works by the type of adaptive pressure and interaction and analyze their properties and implications.

Result: The survey identifies and systematizes prior work into three main co-evolution categories: (1) Agent–Agent Co-Evolution, where agents adapt to dynamic peers (adversarial, collaborative, organizational); (2) Agent–Environment Co-Evolution, where tasks, feedback, and interaction spaces also adapt; and (3) Meta Co-Evolution, where even the mechanisms of evolution become subject to change. It highlights how systems progressively move away from fixed, human-designed constraints and surfaces patterns and gaps in the literature.

Conclusion: Co-evolution offers a path to more robust, open-ended improvement in agentic systems beyond static learning contexts. By framing the field with a three-stage taxonomy, the survey provides a conceptual foundation for designing and analyzing such systems. The authors emphasize unresolved challenges in evaluation, scaling across many components, and ensuring safety and controllability as autonomy and evolutionary complexity increase.

Abstract: Agentic systems are increasingly expected to improve after deployment, yet single-entity self-evolution is often bounded by a static learning context, such as fixed tasks and feedback. This survey focuses on co-evolution in agentic systems, a multi-component form of self-evolution in which multiple agents and their environment impose adaptive pressure on one another. To organize existing papers, we propose a progressive three-stage taxonomy that traces how the system gradually sheds human-engineered constraints. Agent--Agent Co-Evolution studies how agents adapt through dynamic peers, including adversarial, collaborative, and organizational adaptation. Agent--Environment Co-Evolution extends this loop to adaptive tasks, feedback, and interaction spaces that change with the agents. Meta Co-Evolution further explores the possibility of making the evolution mechanism itself evolvable. We also discuss open challenges in evaluating such systems, scaling them across multiple components, and keeping increasingly autonomous evolutionary processes safe and controllable. This survey provides a unified foundation for building robust and open-ended agentic systems that can improve beyond fixed human-designed paths.

</details>


### [16] [Is This Your Final Answer? Cross-Contextual Consistency as a Measure of LLM Credibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10315)
*Siyang Wu,Yibo Jiang,Bryon Aragam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose and measure a new behavioral property of LLMs—cross-contextual consistency—and show that models whose answers change less under neutral prompt perturbations tend to be more correct and factual.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are black-box systems, so it is unclear whether their outputs reflect stable internal beliefs or just superficial pattern matching. Existing evaluations often rely on single-prompt performance, which cannot distinguish between robust knowledge and fragile behavior, and many benchmarks are considered saturated, obscuring where models truly remain unreliable.

Method: They define Cross-Contextual Consistency (C3) as the stability of a model’s answer when the same task is embedded in different, topic-aligned but content-neutral contexts. Practically, they perturb prompts in controlled ways and compare model generations on the original versus perturbed prompts, quantifying the “cross-contextual shift.” They compute this metric across 26 LLMs on six benchmarks covering reasoning, factuality, and code generation.

Result: Across all evaluated models and tasks, responses that exhibit smaller cross-contextual shifts are more likely to be correct or factual. C3 behaves as an additional evaluation axis distinct from raw accuracy and reveals variation in reliability that standard scores obscure, even on benchmarks where aggregate performance is already very high.

Conclusion: Cross-contextual consistency is a useful behavioral signal for assessing LLM reliability. Measuring C3 helps identify which answers and which benchmark regions remain informative and non-saturated, providing a diagnostic tool for understanding and improving the practical usefulness of LLM benchmarks and model evaluations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are powerful black-box systems, making it difficult to discern whether their answers reflect stable internal beliefs or superficial pattern matching. We identify cross-contextual consistency as an underutilized behavioral property of LLMs: a credible answer should remain stable when the same task is placed under topic-aligned, content-neutral contextual variation. Building on this intuition, we operationalize Cross-Contextual Consistency (C3) by comparing model generations under original and perturbed prompts. Across 26 models and six benchmarks spanning reasoning, factuality, and code generation, we find that answers with smaller cross-contextual shifts are more likely to be correct or factual. We demonstrate that C3 provides a complementary axis of evaluation and can serve as a benchmark usefulness diagnostic, identifying which portions of a benchmark remain informative even when aggregated scores are widely considered "saturate".

</details>


### [17] [VisEditBench: Can Vision-Language Models Edit Visualization Code from Multimodal Feedback?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10408)
*Mizanur Rahman,Arshia Azimlu,Shadikur Rahman,Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar,Amran Bhuiyan,Shafiq Joty,Enamul Hoque Prince*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces VisEditBench, a benchmark for evaluating how well vision-language models can edit existing visualization code based on multimodal feedback, and proposes VisEditAgent, a render-grounded editing framework that significantly improves performance on this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most work and benchmarks on visualization with vision-language models focus on generating visualization code from scratch, but real-world visualization authoring is iterative: users repair buggy charts and restyle existing ones. There is a lack of systematic evaluation and methods for code editing based on multimodal feedback (text plus images of charts). The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a benchmark and assessing current models’ abilities in realistic editing scenarios.

Method: The authors construct VisEditBench, a dataset of 1,395 human-annotated visualization code-editing tasks reflecting realistic workflows and failure cases. It includes two settings: (1) feedback-guided repair, where models receive buggy or annotated charts plus textual feedback and must fix the visualization code; and (2) reference-guided restyling, where models modify code so the resulting chart matches a target chart image. They evaluate 20 state-of-the-art vision-language models on these tasks using pass rate as the main metric. They also design VisEditAgent, a render-grounded editing framework built on GPT-4o that iteratively proposes edits, runs the code to render charts, validates the output, and refines the edits based on the rendered feedback.

Result: Across 20 evaluated VLMs, visualization code editing is found to be challenging. The best-performing model, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, achieves an overall pass rate of 74.46%, while most open-source models score under 50%. Performance is especially weak on visually grounded style adaptation tasks; even Claude-4.6-Sonnet reaches only 55.71% there. VisEditAgent, the proposed render-grounded agent based on GPT-4o, boosts performance from an overall pass rate of 55.75% (single-pass baseline) to 67.99%, showing significant gains from iterative, render-aware editing.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current vision-language models are far from solving the problem of visualization code editing, particularly for style adaptation guided by visual references. VisEditBench provides a realistic and challenging benchmark to drive progress in this area. The success of VisEditAgent indicates that incorporating render-grounded, iterative feedback is key to more faithful and reliable visualization editing. The authors will release VisEditBench publicly to support further research and benchmarking.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong capabilities in generating visualization code from textual or visual specifications. However, real-world visualization authoring is inherently iterative: users frequently revise existing visualizations to repair flawed charts or adapt them to desired styles. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate generation from scratch, leaving visualization code editing from multimodal feedback largely unexplored. We introduce VisEditBench, a benchmark of 1,395 human-annotated visualization code-editing tasks grounded in realistic visualization workflows and failure cases. VisEditBench covers two practical settings: feedback-guided repair, where models revise visualization code using buggy or marked charts together with textual feedback, and reference-guided restyling, where models modify code to match a target chart image. Evaluating 20 state-of-the-art VLMs reveals that visualization code editing remains challenging: Claude-4.6-Sonnet achieves the best overall pass rate of 74.46%, while most open-source models remain below 50%. Performance is particularly weak on visually grounded style adaptation, where Claude-4.6-Sonnet achieves only 55.71%. To establish a strong baseline, we further propose VisEditAgent, a render-grounded editing framework that iteratively generates, executes, validates, and refines candidate edits. Built on GPT-4o, VisEditAgent improves overall pass rate from 55.75% to 67.99%, demonstrating the importance of render-grounded feedback for faithful visualization editing. We will release VisEditBench at https://github.com/vis-nlp/VisEditBench.

</details>


### [18] [How Robust Are LLMs to Vietnamese Dialects?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10414)
*Minh Tran,Trinh Chau,Thanh-Nhan Le,Nam Tran,Luan Thanh Nguyen,Cuong Dang,Duc Hoang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces VialectBench, a benchmark to test how robust large language models are to Vietnamese dialectal variation across multiple NLP tasks, showing that even strong models degrade noticeably under dialect inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM evaluations use standard written Vietnamese, even though real users speak and write in diverse dialects whose forms differ while preserving meaning. Prior work focuses on normalizing dialects into the standard form instead of directly measuring how models fail on dialectal inputs. This leaves a gap in understanding the robustness and reliability of LLMs for Vietnamese speakers across regions.

Method: The authors construct VialectBench, a controlled benchmark derived from 400 Standard Vietnamese instances and 2,400 human-written dialectal rewrites spanning six dialect groups. They cover four tasks: emotion recognition, natural language inference, question answering, and multiple-choice QA. They analyze likelihood shifts with a fixed reference model to confirm that dialectal versions preserve meaning while differing in surface form. Then they systematically evaluate ten instruction-tuned LLMs on both standard and dialectal versions, measuring accuracy drops, dialect group effects, and harmful-flip rates (cases where predictions change for the worse).

Result: Dialectal rewrites lead to measurable shifts in model-relative likelihood while keeping similar length to standard sentences, confirming controlled variation. Across ten instruction-tuned models, dialectal inputs reduce average performance by 2.82%, and none of the models is dialect-invariant. All four tasks show degradation, with QA suffering the most. Robustness varies strongly by dialect group: PNT3 and PNT2 cause the largest drops (6.17% and 4.73%), while PNB slightly improves performance by 0.42%. The Central dialect group (PNT1–PNT4) yields the highest harmful-flip rate at 6.54%.

Conclusion: High accuracy on Standard Vietnamese does not ensure reliable performance on regional dialects, even when meaning is preserved. LLMs exhibit systematic robustness gaps across dialects, especially in QA and for certain Central dialects. The VialectBench benchmark provides a controlled way to measure these failures and motivates future work on making Vietnamese-capable LLMs more dialect-robust rather than relying solely on normalization to the standard form.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are typically evaluated on standard written Vietnamese, yet everyday communication frequently involves regional dialects that preserve meaning but differ in surface form. Existing Vietnamese dialect work largely addresses this issue through dialect-to-standard normalization instead of measuring how the model fails under Vietnamese dialectal inputs. To address this gap, we present the first systematic evaluation of LLM robustness to Vietnamese dialect variation across multiple tasks, quantifying performance degradation and failure patterns. We introduce VialectBench (Vietnamese Dialects Benchmarking), a controlled benchmark for testing whether model decisions remain stable across six Vietnamese dialect groups. VialectBench contains 400 Standard Vietnamese source instances and 2,400 human-written dialectal rewrites spanning emotion recognition (ER), natural language inference (NLI), question answering (QA), and multiple-choice question answering (MCQA). Dataset evaluation with a fixed reference language model shows that the dialectal rewrites induce a measurable model-relative likelihood shift while remaining nearly equal in length to their Standard counterparts. Across ten instruction-tuned models, dialectal inputs reduce average performance by 2.82%, and no evaluated model is fully dialect-invariant. All four tasks are affected, with QA showing the largest average degradation. Robustness also varies substantially across dialect groups: PNT3 and PNT2 cause the largest average performance drops, at 6.17% and 4.73%, respectively, whereas PNB slightly improves average performance by 0.42%. The Central dialect group (PNT1-PNT4) also yields the highest average harmful-flip rate across all models, at 6.54%. These findings show that strong performance on Standard Vietnamese does not guarantee reliable behavior under meaning-preserving regional variation.

</details>


### [19] [From Reasoning Depth to Reasoning Breadth: Evaluating Multi-Point Associative Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10444)
*Si'an Xie,Jiaxun Liu,Biao Yang,Wei Yuan,Fan Yang,Tingting Gao,Ming Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MPAR-Bench, a bilingual benchmark designed to test reasoning breadth in LLMs via multi-point associative reasoning tasks, showing that current models’ strong depth does not translate into robust breadth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations mostly test reasoning depth—following long, linear chains of inference—while largely neglecting reasoning breadth, i.e., integrating multiple diverse clues in parallel. The authors argue that breadth is a distinct and practically important capability that current benchmarks do not isolate or measure well.

Method: They design MPAR-Bench, inspired by the party game Just One, where models must guess a hidden target word from several independently generated and semantically diverse clues. They create 1,000 bilingual (English–Chinese) items using a multi-agent pipeline to generate clues, apply embedding-based filtering to ensure diversity, and then perform human verification. They also define multiple evaluation metrics (accuracy, ANLS, embedding similarity, reasoning-trace verification) and introduce four perturbations to test robustness: masking some clues, shuffling clue order, adding distractor clues, and using multi-step clues.

Result: Across multiple LLMs, the perturbations substantially reduce accuracy (by 9–18 percentage points in English and 5–12 in Chinese). Switching models to a “thinking mode” (chain-of-thought or similar) improves overall accuracy, particularly for English, but does not reliably reduce their vulnerability to the perturbations; in some instances, extended reasoning even turns initially correct guesses into wrong ones.

Conclusion: Reasoning breadth is not a mere byproduct of reasoning depth: current LLMs that perform well on depth-oriented tasks can still struggle to integrate multiple, diverse clues robustly. Existing benchmarks under-cover this capability, so MPAR-Bench provides a needed tool to measure and analyze breadth-specific weaknesses and guide future model development.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress on reasoning tasks that require increasingly long and complex inferential chains. This progress primarily reflects reasoning depth. A complementary and comparatively unexamined capability is reasoning breadth: exploring multiple semantic directions in parallel and integrating the resulting clues into one coherent answer. We introduce MPAR-Bench, a bilingual English-Chinese benchmark that isolates reasoning breadth through multi-point associative reasoning. Inspired by the cooperative game Just One, each item asks a model to recover a hidden target from several independently generated, semantically diverse clues. We construct 1,000 items using a multi-agent clue-generation pipeline, embedding-based diversity filtering, and human verification. Only the answer space is drawn from public word lists, whereas every clue set is generated from scratch. Beyond exact-match accuracy, we evaluate models using accuracy, ANLS, embedding similarity, reasoning-trace verification, and four perturbations: clue masking, order shuffling, distractor injection, and multi-step clues. Across evaluated models, perturbations reduce accuracy by 9-18 percentage points in English and 5-12 percentage points in Chinese. Thinking mode improves standard-setting accuracy, especially in English, but does not consistently reduce sensitivity to perturbations. Case-level analysis also shows that extended reasoning can overturn an initially correct hypothesis. These results indicate that greater reasoning depth does not automatically confer robust reasoning breadth, and that reasoning breadth remains largely uncovered by current benchmarks.

</details>


### [20] [MD-ProTector: Positioning Multiple Data-Driven Prototypes for LLM-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10459)
*Jinmo Han,Jimin Hong,Chanyeong Moon,Ju Yeon Kang,Seonuk Kim,Nam Soo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MD-ProTector, a prototype-based encoder detector that improves large-scale detection of LLM-generated text across diverse conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-generated text detectors must work reliably at scale across diverse writing styles, domains, languages, and models, but typical input-only encoder-based detectors perform binary classification and provide only a single decision boundary per class. This ignores rich within-class variation (e.g., different types of human writing or different families of LLM outputs), limiting robustness, interpretability, and performance under domain and generator shifts.

Method: The authors design MD-ProTector, an encoder-based detection method that represents each class (human vs LLM-generated) with multiple trainable reference vectors, called prototypes, in the encoder embedding space. Each prototype effectively captures a subgroup of texts within its class, yielding multiple decision boundaries per class. To ensure that prototypes specialize in distinct within-class variations rather than collapsing, they introduce a Prototype Positioning loss that explicitly disentangles class-level separation (human vs machine) from the finer-grained structure that separates prototypes within the same class. The method is trained and evaluated as an input-only detector, suitable for deployment settings where only the text is available.

Result: Across five evaluation settings built from three large-scale benchmarks (MAGE, CDCM, RAID) that systematically vary domains, generators, languages, and adversarial manipulations, MD-ProTector outperforms existing encoder-based detection methods.
