It achieves the highest average recall (AvgRec) on MAGE CDCM and RAID, as well as the best AUROC and lowest FPR at 95% TPR (FPR95) on RAID, indicating stronger detection performance and better robustness to challenging shifts and adversarial edits.

Conclusion: Using multiple prototypes per class in encoder-based LLM-text detectors, together with a dedicated Prototype Positioning loss to structure the embedding space, leads to more robust and accurate detection of machine-generated text across diverse and adversarial scenarios. MD-ProTector demonstrates that modeling within-class variation is crucial for scalable, practical deployment of LLM-content detectors and sets new performance baselines on several large benchmarks.

Abstract: As LLM-generated content becomes more sophisticated, detection systems for distinguishing those texts from human-written text must operate at scale while handling diverse writing styles, domains, languages, and generator models. Input-only encoder detectors are suitable for practical deployment setting, but standard binary classification supplies only the class label and does not explicitly organize the substantial variation within either class. We propose MD-ProTector, which represents each class with multiple trainable reference vectors in the encoder embedding space, referred to as prototypes. These prototypes provide separate decision boundaries for different groups of texts within the same class. However, adding multiple prototypes alone does not determine which variation each prototype should represent. MD-ProTector addresses this problem with Prototype Positioning loss, which separates class-level structure from the within-class variation that differentiates individual prototypes. Evaluated across five settings from three large-scale benchmarks covering domain, generator, language, and adversarial variation, MD-ProTector achieves the highest AvgRec on MAGE CDCM and RAID and the highest AUROC and lowest FPR95 on RAID among the compared encoder-based methods.

</details>


### [21] [Calibrating Post-Training Feature Shifts for LLM Data Contamination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10462)
*Zhen Yang,Mengqi Wang,Gengda Zhao,Mo Zhou,Jianwei Wang,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose CalibDCD, a calibration framework that fixes feature-based data contamination detectors so they still work well on instruction-tuned / post-trained LLMs, significantly boosting detection accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data contamination detection (membership inference) for LLM pre-training relies on features extracted from model behaviors on a text. But modern LLMs are heavily post-trained (instruction tuning, preference optimization, reasoning training), which changes these behaviors and thus the membership features, making it much harder to distinguish whether a text was in the pre-training corpus. There is a need for a way to adapt or calibrate existing feature-based DCD methods so they stay effective despite such distribution shifts caused by post-training.

Method: They introduce CalibDCD, a generic calibration framework that can be wrapped around existing feature-based DCD methods. It has two main components: (1) Multi-View Shift Detection: For known non-member texts, they craft multiple controlled prompt variants (different “views”) and query the target model, then analyze which views expose recurrent systematic shifts in the feature space that are attributable to post-training rather than membership. They select and consolidate the most informative views to characterize these recurring shifts. (2) Bounded Feature Correction: Given the identified shift directions/components, they selectively adjust only those parts of the membership feature vectors for new samples, while imposing bounds on the magnitude of correction so they do not overwrite useful membership signals. This calibrated feature representation is then fed into an existing detector, improving member vs. non-member separability.

Result: On multiple benchmarks and models, integrating CalibDCD with prior feature-based DCD approaches yields consistent performance gains. Reported improvements reach up to 7.0 percentage points in AUC and up to 15.0 percentage points in TPR at 5% FPR, indicating substantially better ability to correctly identify contaminated (member) texts at low false-positive rates.

Conclusion: Post-training-induced behavior shifts substantially hurt feature-based data contamination detection for LLMs, but these shifts are structured enough that they can be detected from non-member data and then compensated for. CalibDCD provides a general-purpose, model-agnostic calibration layer that detects multi-view feature shifts and applies bounded corrections, thereby restoring and significantly enhancing the effectiveness of existing DCD methods under realistic post-trained LLM settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are trained on massive and largely undisclosed corpora that may contain copyrighted or privacy-sensitive content. Data contamination detection (DCD) therefore aims to determine whether a given text is a member of the pre-training corpus of a target LLM. Recent state-of-the-art DCD methods follow a feature-based paradigm that derives membership features from the input text and the corresponding model output. However, most modern LLMs undergo post-training, such as instruction tuning, preference optimization, and reasoning-oriented training, which can alter model outputs and shift the corresponding membership features, thereby reducing the separability between members and non-members.
  To address this problem, we propose CalibDCD, a broadly applicable calibration framework for feature-based DCD methods, comprising (1) Multi-View Shift Detection, which identifies recurring feature shifts associated with post-training, and (2) Bounded Feature Correction, which selectively mitigates their influence on membership prediction. Specifically, Multi-View Shift Detection evaluates controlled prompt variants on known non-member texts and consolidates the most informative views to identify recurring feature shifts. Bounded Feature Correction selectively adjusts feature components aligned with the detected shifts and controls the correction extent to preserve useful detection information.
  Experiments show that CalibDCD consistently improves existing feature-based detectors, with gains of up to 7.0% in AUC and 15.0% in TPR@5%FPR.

</details>


### [22] [Every Token Counts: Exact Likert-Scale Distributions for Measuring LLM Attitudes and Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10503)
*Davood Wadi,Mohsen Ghodrat,Matthew Philp*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a precise, experiment-style framework to measure LLMs’ values and biases using factorial designs and exact token probabilities instead of noisy, unstructured benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM bias and value evaluations rely on large, unstructured benchmarks that only reveal aggregate effects. These cannot distinguish whether observed bias comes from inherent model tendencies, specific contexts, or interactions between factors. This makes it impossible to causally attribute or precisely quantify biases and latent values, which is increasingly problematic as LLMs are deployed as autonomous agents making consequential decisions.

Method: The authors adapt tools from human psychometrics to LLMs and design a controlled behavioral evaluation framework. (1) They use fully crossed factorial experimental designs in prompts, allowing each factor (e.g., demographic attributes, contexts) to be systematically varied so that main effects and interactions can be causally identified. (2) Instead of sampling generated text multiple times, they operate directly on exact token-level probability mass functions (PMFs) output by the model, removing Monte Carlo sampling noise. (3) They introduce a multivariate ordinal consensus metric to aggregate token-level probabilities into interpretable behavioral measurements and develop a distributional ANOVA suitable for analyzing the full predictive distributions. This allows analytical, rather than simulation-based, inference on LLM behavior.

Result: Using a case study on consumer ethnocentrism, they apply their framework to five different LLMs. The analysis reveals systematic country-of-origin biases—i.e., models’ differential treatment of products or information depending on the country involved. These biases can be decomposed into main effects and interactions attributable to specific design factors in the prompts. The discovered patterns are not visible in traditional aggregate benchmark evaluations, which would obscure such structured biases.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that unstructured benchmarks are inadequate for causal, fine-grained analysis of LLM values and biases. Their analytically exact, factorial, PMF-based framework provides a more rigorous way to isolate and quantify main and interaction effects underlying model behavior. By connecting psychometric principles with LLM mechanics, they offer a path toward more transparent, reliable, and causally interpretable evaluation of LLM biases, as illustrated by the consumer ethnocentrism case study across multiple models.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, accurately evaluating their latent values and biases is critical. The NLP community typically evaluates models using large, unstructured benchmarks. While effective for general capabilities, these datasets fundamentally conflate causal mechanisms: even when an aggregate bias is detected, unstructured evaluations cannot disentangle whether it stems from baseline traits, contextual confounders, or complex interactions. To address this, we introduce an analytically exact framework for the controlled behavioral evaluation of LLMs. We bridge human psychometrics with LLM mechanics by resolving gaps in design, measurement, and analysis. First, we replace unstructured prompting with fully crossed factorial experiments to systematically isolate causal main and interaction effects. Second, we eliminate Monte Carlo text sampling noise by operating directly on exact, token-level Probability Mass Functions (PMFs). Third, we derive a multivariate ordinal consensus metric and a distributional ANOVA to process these PMFs analytically. We validate our framework with a case study on consumer ethnocentrism across five LLMs, demonstrating how our approach isolates systemic country-of-origin biases that aggregate benchmarks otherwise obscure.

</details>


### [23] [ASR-Roundtrip Evaluation Can Mask Context- and Convention-Dependent Reading Errors in Chinese News TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10606)
*Shijun Luo,Lizhi Wan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that using ASR roundtrip as the only metric for Chinese news TTS intelligibility can miss many contextual reading errors, so it should be used only as a screening tool, not as ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASR-roundtrip evaluation (TTS→audio→ASR→text) is popular because it scales cheaply, but practitioners noticed that it often fails to flag TTS reading mistakes that human listeners would catch, especially in context-dependent spans in Chinese news. The authors want to quantify how often such errors are masked and under what conditions, to assess the reliability of ASR-roundtrip as an evaluation method for reading-risk in Chinese news TTS.

Method: They focus on high-risk Chinese news spans where correct reading depends on context or domain convention (e.g., sports scores, aircraft models, units, membership titles). They run targeted audits over 110 MiMo TTS cases, examining "Raw TTS" outputs and how ASR systems transcribe them. They categorize each case into masked false negatives (ASR hides a TTS mistake), exposed TTS errors, or no raw TTS error. They also use a span-isolation diagnostic (isolating the risky span rather than full context) to see if masked errors become visible. A separate audit with CosyVoice TTS on the same cases provides cross-system evidence. They compare how two ASR systems (Qwen3-ASR and Paraformer) surface-recover masked cases.

Result: In the MiMo TTS audit of 110 high-risk spans, they find 46 masked false negatives (TTS error, but ASR output looks correct), 9 exposed errors, and 55 cases with no raw TTS error. When they isolate spans, 18 of the 46 masked errors become exposed. In a CosyVoice-only audit of the same pool, they identify 51 masked cases. Combining both audits yields 97 unique TTS audio files with confirmed masking. Qwen3-ASR surface-recovers 40 of these, while Paraformer recovers only 2, indicating large differences in how ASR systems mask or expose TTS errors.

Conclusion: ASR-roundtrip is helpful for large-scale screening of TTS intelligibility but is unreliable as a standalone ground-truth measure for Chinese news reading-risk, especially in context-sensitive spans. Different ASR models vary greatly in how many TTS reading errors they mask, so robust TTS evaluation requires additional diagnostics (such as span isolation) and possibly human or task-specific checks rather than relying solely on roundtrip scores.

Abstract: ASR-roundtrip evaluation is widely used as a scalable proxy for text-to-speech (TTS) intelligibility, but it can produce false negatives for reading errors perceived by listeners. We study Chinese news TTS spans whose correct reading depends on context or domain conventions, such as sports scores, aircraft models, technical units, and membership names. In these cases, Raw TTS can choose a plausible but wrong reading while ASR transcribes the audio as the intended or surface-correct text. A targeted audit over 110 high-risk MiMo TTS cases, reported with a complete denominator, confirms 46 masked false negatives, 9 exposed TTS errors, and 55 cases with no Raw TTS error. A span-isolation diagnostic re-exposes 18/46 previously masked errors. A Raw-only CosyVoice audit on the same targeted pool confirms 51 masked cases. Across the 97 TTS-specific audio files labeled confirmed masked across the two audits, Qwen3-ASR surface-recovers 40 cases, whereas Paraformer does so in only 2. The results suggest that ASR-roundtrip is useful for screening but insufficient as standalone ground truth for Chinese news reading-risk evaluation.

</details>


### [24] [Simplex Relaxation for Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10615)
*Jinya Sakurai,Patrick Pynadath,Satoshi Hayakawa,Jaehong Yoon,Xulei Yang,Nancy F. Chen,Xun Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Simplax, a Dirichlet–categorical augmentation for discrete diffusion that enriches training and sampling while preserving the original categorical corruption process, leading to better text and Sudoku generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing discrete diffusion models for categorical data are constrained by the choice of corruption kernel, such as uniform diffusion, which fixes the intermediate state space and reverse problem. Researchers want to improve training objectives and reverse transitions without altering the underlying categorical corruption, so they can get better generative performance while keeping the desirable properties of standard discrete diffusion.

Method: The authors propose Simplax, an exact augmentation that associates each corrupted categorical state with an auxiliary variable living on the probability simplex, drawn from a Dirichlet distribution. This construction is designed so that when the auxiliary simplex variable is marginalized out, one recovers the original uniform discrete diffusion process on categories. Using this joint Dirichlet–categorical formulation, they derive a Rao–Blackwellized training objective for the reverse bridge and construct a stochastic reverse sampling procedure, while still feeding the corrupted categorical state (not the simplex variable) into the denoiser network.

Result: On unconditional OpenWebText generation, Simplax improves the tradeoff between perplexity and entropy compared with baselines, meaning it can generate text that is both more likely under the data distribution and more diverse. On Sudoku, a model trained only on 30-clue puzzles attains the highest solution accuracy across all tested clue densities, including the challenging 17-clue uniquely solvable regime, and also produces the most valid boards in unconditional generation among compared methods.

Conclusion: Augmenting uniform discrete diffusion with an exact Dirichlet–categorical mechanism (Simplax) enables richer training objectives and sampling schemes without modifying the original categorical corruption kernel. This leads to practical gains in both language and combinatorial puzzle generation, suggesting that such augmentations are a promising direction for improving discrete diffusion models while preserving their core structure.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models for categorical generation are defined by a corruption kernel, which determines the intermediate state space and the associated reverse prediction problem. We study uniform discrete diffusion and ask whether its training objective and reverse transitions can be enriched without changing the underlying categorical corruption process. We introduce Simplax, an exact Dirichlet--categorical augmentation that couples each corrupted categorical state with an auxiliary simplex-valued variable while preserving the original uniform diffusion process as its categorical marginal. This augmentation yields a tractable Rao--Blackwellized reverse-bridge objective and a corresponding stochastic reverse sampler, while retaining the corrupted categorical state as the denoiser input. Empirically, Simplax improves the generative perplexity--entropy tradeoff on unconditional OpenWebText generation. On Sudoku, a model trained exclusively on $30$-clue puzzles achieves the highest accuracy among the compared methods across all evaluated clue densities, including the minimum uniquely solvable $17$-clue regime, and also achieves the highest validity in unconditional generation.

</details>


### [25] [Dual-Loop Self-Evolution via Verifiable Emotion Feedback for Multi-Turn Empathetic Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10626)
*Yi Wei,Shuo Jiang,Huaixia Dou,Jie Zhu,Junhui Li,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a dual-loop self-evolution RL framework using emotion-based rewards to improve multi-turn empathetic dialogue in LLMs, dynamically adapting both the policy and its training interaction distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empathetic dialogue is multi-turn and path-dependent, with users revealing emotions gradually and responses influencing trust over time. Existing RL methods with emotion rewards only update the policy while keeping the interaction data distribution fixed, causing a mismatch between what the model learns and the scenarios it actually needs to handle. This limits performance on long-horizon, emotionally supportive conversations.

Method: They design a dual-loop self-evolution framework that leverages verifiable emotion feedback. With a frozen user simulator and emotion verifier, the inner loop optimizes a multi-turn dialogue policy using continuous emotion rewards. The outer loop then uses the same rollouts to estimate how useful different interaction conditions are for that evolving policy and adapts the experience distribution accordingly. To combat sparse and noisy rollouts, they group trajectories by shared scenario and state, keep these fixed within groups, and prioritize groups whose success rates sit near the model’s current competence boundary. A hierarchical controller shares information across different support intents, while uncertainty-based exploration and uniform rehearsal prevent the system from dropping scenarios too early.

Result: The adapted interaction distribution closes both the policy and data loops without requiring more rollouts. On the SAGE benchmark, this framework improves Qwen3-8B’s overall score from 53.87 to 79.24 and beats a uniform emotion-reward RL baseline (under the same protocol) by 7.23 points.

Conclusion: Jointly evolving both the dialogue policy and its training interaction distribution, guided by verifiable emotion feedback, significantly improves long-horizon empathetic support. Targeting scenarios near the model’s competence frontier and carefully managing exploration yields better sample efficiency and stronger empathetic conversation performance than standard emotion-reward RL with a fixed data distribution.

Abstract: Large language models have demonstrated conversational capabilities, yet empathetic competence remains challenging. Empathetic support is inherently multi-turn and path-dependent: users disclose concerns gradually, emotions evolve over time, and early responses shape trust and receptivity. Reinforcement learning with verifiable emotion rewards provides scalable supervision for long-horizon interactions. However, existing methods evolve the dialogue policy while keeping its training interaction distribution fixed, creating a mismatch between policy competence and training experience. We introduce a dual-loop self-evolution framework driven by verifiable emotion feedback. With the user simulator and verifier frozen, the inner loop optimizes the multi-turn policy using continuous emotion rewards, while the outer loop uses the same outcomes to estimate policy-relative interaction utility and adapt experience. To obtain estimates from sparse, stochastic rollouts, the framework holds the scenario and interaction state constant within each group and prioritizes conditions whose group pass rates lie near the policy's competence boundary. A hierarchical controller shares evidence across support intents, while uncertainty-guided exploration and uniform rehearsal prevent premature exclusion. The resulting distribution generates trajectories, closing both loops without increasing the rollout budget. On SAGE, our framework raises Qwen3-8B Overall from 53.87 to 79.24 and outperforms protocol-matched uniform emotion-reward reinforcement learning by 7.23 points.

</details>


### [26] [Decomposition-Induced Context-Memory Conflict: When Fact-Checking Pipelines Contradict Their Own Source Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10627)
*Yu-Feng Yen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that the claim-decomposition step in fact-checking pipelines can itself hallucinate, replacing what the source text says with the model’s own beliefs, and analyzes this as a specific, partially mitigable failure mode called Decomposition-Induced Context-Memory Conflict (DI-CC).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern factuality pipelines rely on an initial decomposition of long text into atomic claims, implicitly assuming this step is neutral and faithful. The authors suspect that this assumption is wrong: the decomposer may inject its own parametric beliefs, causing conflicts with the original context and undermining downstream fact-checking, while current reference-free methods and mitigations might miss or mishandle this problem.

Method: They define DI-CC as cases where the decomposer’s output contradicts the source passage due to parametric beliefs overriding context, and connect it mechanistically to classical context-memory conflict. Using NQ-Swap data with known context-memory conflicts, they train a linear probe on internal model features and test whether it can distinguish faithful vs. conflicting decompositions. They also evaluate SelfCheckGPT-style self-consistency sampling as a detection baseline and test context-aware decoding, a training-free mitigation from the classical context-memory conflict setting, when applied to decomposition. Finally, they empirically probe how often DI-CC occurs naturally, and how it varies with model scale.

Result: A linear probe trained only on classical context-memory conflict data (NQ-Swap) can accurately identify decomposition positions that yield DI-CC vs. faithful decompositions (AUC 0.86–0.88, strongly significant). SelfCheckGPT-style self-consistency performs at chance (AUC 0.51), failing to detect DI-CC because the erroneous content is stable across resamples. Context-aware decoding transfers to decomposition and reduces DI-CC but introduces major side effects: many decompositions fail to parse under coreference-heavy conditions, often due to fabricated identities. They also find that DI-CC appears at a non-negligible rate on naturally hallucinated text and that a minimum model size is required for it to manifest.

Conclusion: Decomposition in fact-checking pipelines is not a neutral pre-processing step: it is subject to Decomposition-Induced Context-Memory Conflict, where parametric beliefs override the source text. DI-CC shares a mechanism with classical context-memory conflict, is detectable via internal-feature probes trained in the classical setting, and is invisible to popular self-consistency baselines. While context-aware decoding can mitigate DI-CC, it brings serious usability costs and is not ready for deployment. Overall, DI-CC is a real, mechanistically grounded, partially treatable failure mode that must be considered when designing and evaluating factuality and fact-checking systems based on decomposition.

Abstract: Decompose-then-verify pipelines, including FActScore-style fact-checkers and long-form factuality evaluators, first split a passage into atomic claims before checking each one. Decomposition itself is treated as a neutral preprocessing step. We show it is not: a decomposer can be induced to substitute its own parametric belief for what the source passage says, producing a claim that contradicts the text it was supposed to summarize faithfully. We call this Decomposition-Induced Context-Memory Conflict (DI-CC) and show it is mechanistically the same phenomenon as classical context-memory conflict, occurring inside a different pipeline stage than prior work has examined. A linear probe trained only on classical context-memory conflict data (NQ-Swap), never exposed to any decomposition output, significantly separates decomposition positions that produce DI-CC from faithful decompositions (AUC = 0.86-0.88, permutation p < 0.0005). An existing reference-free baseline, SelfCheckGPT-style self-consistency sampling, fails to detect DI-CC at all (AUC 0.51, chance-level), because DI-CC content is stably recoverable and recurs across resamples, unlike the variability self-consistency methods rely on. Context-aware decoding, a training-free mitigation from the classical setting, transfers to decomposition and suppresses DI-CC, but at a severe cost: many decompositions under coreference-heavy conditions fail to parse, often because the decomposer fabricates a different identity. We do not consider this mitigation deployment-ready. We further characterize the mechanism's boundaries: its natural occurrence rate is too sparss not manifest on naturally-occurring hallucinatedtext, and it requires a minimum model scale to detecablish DI-CC as a real, mechanistically grounded, andpartially treatable failure mode, with a scope we chhan overstate.

</details>


### [27] [Seeds Before Objectives: Rethinking Evaluation for Low-Resource Garhwali ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10670)
*Karamvir Singh Batra,Prathamjyot Singh,Ashima Sood,Jasmeet Singh,Sahil Sharma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates ASR methods for low-resource Garhwali and shows that many reported improvements vanish when tested across multiple random seeds; standard CTC with w2v-BERT 2.0 and proper multi-seed evaluation is more reliable than fancier methods or larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-run experiments on small corpora, common for low-resource dialects, may report improvements that are just random seed noise. Garhwali, a low-resource Indo-Aryan language, lacks a rigorous, reproducible ASR benchmark with proper statistical testing, making it hard to know what really helps model performance.

Method: The authors build an ASR benchmark for Garhwali on the official VAANI data splits, run multiple training seeds for each method, release per-seed outputs, and apply significance testing across seeds. They re-evaluate several proposed improvements: Focal CTC, a matra-weighted loss targeting specific script features, and Hindi-to-Garhwali transfer, and compare them against standard CTC with different pretrained encoders such as w2v-BERT 2.0 and MMS-1B, also testing speed perturbation augmentation.

Result: Many seemingly promising methods do not yield statistically reliable gains: Focal CTC and the matra-weighted objective do not significantly outperform standard CTC; the matra objective does not even reliably reduce its targeted errors; and Hindi-to-Garhwali transfer performs no better than direct Garhwali fine-tuning. In contrast, w2v-BERT 2.0 with standard CTC achieves about 47.0% WER averaged over five seeds, outperforming larger models like MMS-1B, and speed augmentation provides a small but mostly consistent improvement.

Conclusion: For low-resource ASR on Garhwali, careful multi-seed evaluation on official splits is essential to distinguish genuine improvements from random seed variance. Sophisticated loss functions and cross-lingual transfer offer no robust advantage over a strong baseline using w2v-BERT 2.0 with standard CTC; model pretraining strategy matters more than sheer parameter count, and simple augmentations offer minor but reliable gains.

Abstract: At corpus sizes typical of low-resource dialects, single-run comparisons can yield gains that do not replicate. We show this for Garhwali, an under-resourced Indo-Aryan language of the central Himalaya, building the first reproducible multi-seed ASR benchmark on the official VAANI splits, with per-seed outputs and significance testing. Re-examining plausible gains, we find them fragile: neither Focal CTC nor a matra-weighted objective beats standard CTC under seed-level testing, the matra objective fails to cut even its targeted errors, and Hindi-to-Garhwali transfer gives no gain over direct fine-tuning. What holds up is mundane: w2v-BERT 2.0 with standard CTC reaches 47.0% WER over five seeds, beating the larger MMS-1B and comparable models; pretraining design, not parameter count, drives performance, and speed augmentation gives a small, largely consistent gain. Multi-seed evaluation on official splits separates real gains from seed noise.

</details>


### [28] [Auditing Chinese Web-scale Corpora via Sampled BPE Token Statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10678)
*Qingjie Zhang,Ziqi Tang,Jie Zhang,Gelei Deng,Jinfeng Li,YueFeng Chen,Yitong Yang,Hui Xue,Tianwei Zhang,Han Qiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Sampled-BPE, a lightweight token-level auditing pipeline to detect and quantify Chinese web pollution in large language model training corpora efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chinese web content used to train LLMs includes various forms of implicit and evolving "pollution" (e.g., low‑quality or harmful content). Existing auditing approaches are too expensive for web-scale corpora, are too coarse to inspect token-level issues, and struggle with the implicit and rapidly changing nature of Chinese web pollution. There is a need for an efficient, fine-grained method to audit large Chinese corpora and understand how pollution is distributed across datasets and over time.

Method: The authors propose Sampled-BPE, which samples a small subset of a large corpus and trains a BPE (Byte Pair Encoding) tokenizer on this subset. By examining the learned tokens and their contexts, the method surfaces polluted tokens and estimates their prevalence. The approach emphasizes efficiency in runtime and memory while maintaining accuracy of pollution estimates. They apply this pipeline to 11 open Chinese corpora and 6 yearly Chinese Common Crawl snapshots (2021–2026).

Result: Sampled-BPE achieves a 148.4× speedup and a 35.8× reduction in memory usage compared to full-corpus auditing, while introducing only 4.25% relative error in estimating pollution categories. Applying the method uncovers widespread but uneven levels of pollution in open Chinese corpora and shows that Chinese web content is highly polluted with patterns that change significantly over time across the 2021–2026 snapshots.

Conclusion: Sampled-BPE offers a practical, scalable solution for token-level auditing of large Chinese text corpora, balancing efficiency with reasonable accuracy. The audits reveal that Chinese web pollution is both pervasive and temporally dynamic, underscoring the importance of continuous, fine-grained monitoring of training data. The authors further contribute a hierarchical Chinese web token dataset with over 660k annotated token records (including context, category, and explanations), structured as trees to facilitate human review, analysis, and tracing of pollution sources.

Abstract: Chinese web pollution has surfaced in LLMs, motivating audits of upstream Chinese corpora. However, auditing such corpora faces three challenges: (1) their web-scale size makes full scan costly; (2) prior analyses are often too coarse to expose token-level pollution; (3) Chinese web pollution is implicit and rapidly changing. We propose Sampled-BPE, a lightweight token-level auditing pipeline that sample a small subset and train BPE tokenizer to surface polluted tokens. Experiments show that Sampled-BPE preserves usable estimates while substantially reducing runtime and memory: a 148.4 $\times$ speedup and a 35.8 $\times$ memory reduction induce only 4.25% relative error for pollution categories. We apply the pipeline to 11 open Chinese corpora and 6 Chinese Common Crawl snapshots from 2021 to 2026. The audit reveals widespread but uneven pollution across open corpora, as well as highly polluted and temporally shifting Chinese web content. We further release a hierarchical Chinese web token dataset with 660k+ token records, each with web context, category, and explanation fields, organized as trees to support review and tracing of pollution.

</details>


### [29] [Leveraging Human Reading Behavior for Keyphrase Extraction: A Webcam-based Eye-tracking Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10688)
*Chengzhi Zhang,Xinyi Yan,Wenqi Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds a webcam-based eye-tracking corpus for Chinese LIS abstracts and shows that adding simple eye-tracking features improves automatic keyphrase extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing keyphrase extraction models focus on text representations and ignore human reading behavior, partly because eye-tracking data—especially for Chinese academic texts—are scarce and costly to obtain. The authors want to see whether inexpensive webcam-based eye-tracking can provide useful cognitive signals to boost keyphrase extraction performance.

Method: They developed a lightweight data collection platform using the open-source SearchGazer library to record webcam-based eye movements while people read Chinese LIS abstracts. From these recordings they constructed the Chinese LIS Eye-Tracking Corpus (CLIS-ET) with three character-level features: first fixation duration (FFD), fixation number (FN), and total fixation duration (TFD). These features were then integrated into keyphrase extraction models (notably an Att-BiLSTM+CRF) to measure performance changes.

Result: Across experiments, models enriched with eye-tracking features outperformed text-only baselines in keyphrase extraction. The best performance was achieved when combining fixation number (FN) and total fixation duration (TFD) within the Att-BiLSTM+CRF framework, showing that these two features were particularly informative.

Conclusion: Lightweight webcam-based eye tracking is a practical way to collect cognitive data for Chinese academic reading, and the resulting features (FFD, FN, TFD) significantly enhance keyphrase extraction models. The newly released CLIS-ET corpus and associated code provide a resource and methodology for incorporating human reading behavior into NLP tasks, especially KPE.

Abstract: Purpose: Keyphrases are statistically and semantically important textual units that can also attract readers' attention during comprehension. However, existing keyphrase extraction (KPE) studies mainly focus on improving textual representation while largely overlooking human reading behavior. This study examines whether lightweight webcam-based eye-tracking features can improve KPE from Chinese academic abstracts in Library and Information Science (LIS).
  Methodology: To address the limited availability of eye-tracking data for Chinese academic reading, we developed a lightweight webcam-based data collection platform using the open-source SearchGazer library and constructed the Chinese LIS Eye-Tracking Corpus (CLIS-ET). Three character-level eye-tracking features, first fixation duration (FFD), fixation number (FN), and total fixation duration (TFD), were incorporated into KPE models to evaluate their effects on extraction performance.
  Findings: Eye-tracking features consistently improved KPE performance. The combination of FN and TFD achieved the best results on the Att-BiLSTM+CRF model, indicating that readers' fixation behavior provides useful signals for identifying keyphrases in academic abstracts.
  Originality/value: This study introduces a cost-effective webcam-based eye-tracking approach for KPE and presents CLIS-ET, a Chinese academic eye-tracking corpus containing FFD, FN, and TFD features. The results demonstrate the value of incorporating human reading behavior into keyphrase extraction. Dataset and code: https://github.com/yan-xinyi/ET_AKE and https://github.com/yan-xinyi/Reading_ET_System.

</details>


### [30] [Can Released LLM Vocabularies Support Token-Level Estimation of Hidden Corpora?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10690)
*Qingjie Zhang,Xingzhang Ren,Zixuan Chen,Jinfeng Li,YueFeng Chen,Yitong Yang,Hui Xue,Dayiheng Liu,Han Qiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method to estimate the composition of an LLM’s pretraining corpus using only its released tokenizer, achieving low error in predicting how much different corpora contributed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model weights are sometimes released, but details of the pretraining data are often hidden. Existing work can only infer coarse corpus mixtures or track limited token groups from tokenizer vocabularies. There is a need for a method that can estimate, at a fine-grained token level, what proportion of different corpora likely contributed to training, using only the public tokenizer and known reference corpora.

Method: The authors empirically observe that BPE tokenizers trained on different corpora exhibit stable relationships between token IDs and the ratios of those tokens across corpora. Leveraging this, they propose Quantile-Guided Density Estimation (QGDE), which transfers a learned token ID–ratio distribution from reference corpora to a target tokenizer trained on unknown data. QGDE models the distribution via multiple quantile trends and refines token-level ratio estimates using local density weighting, allowing it to estimate how much each corpus contributes to specific tokens and categories of tokens.

Result: In both controlled experiments and a realistic scenario with the publicly released SmolLM tokenizer, QGDE achieves mean relative errors as low as 3.00% at the token level and 3.08% when aggregating tokens into higher-level categories. These low errors indicate that the method reliably recovers underlying corpus proportions from tokenizer vocabulary information alone.

Conclusion: Tokenizer vocabularies encode stable statistical signals about the underlying pretraining corpora. By exploiting these signals via QGDE, it is possible to estimate corpus composition at a much finer granularity than prior coarse-grained approaches, even when the actual training data remain undisclosed. This suggests tokenizer release is more revealing of data provenance than previously appreciated and provides a practical tool for analyzing LLM pretraining data composition.

Abstract: Pretraining corpus composition shapes LLM capabilities, but it often remains hidden even when model weights are released. Prior work has inferred corpus mixtures or traced specific token groups from released tokenizer vocabularies; in contrast, we estimate corpus ratios for arbitrary target tokens. We first show that BPE tokenizers trained on different corpora share stable token ID--ratio distributions, motivating distribution transfer from known corpora to a target tokenizer trained on hidden corpora. We then propose Quantile-Guided Density Estimation (QGDE), which approximates this distribution with multiple quantile trends and uses local density weighting to produce token-level estimates. In controlled settings and a realistic setting using the released SmolLM tokenizer, QGDE achieves mean relative errors as low as 3.00% for token-level estimation and 3.08% after aggregation into category-level mixtures. These results suggest that released tokenizer vocabularies provide a useful signal for fine-grained corpus estimation beyond coarse composition inference.

</details>


### [31] [SPIEval: Evaluating Large Language Models as Mobile Assistants over Scattered Personal Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10692)
*Junjie Ye,Zhuohui Sheng,Shaofan Liu,Yulun Zhu,Wenjie Fu,Dingwei Zhu,Ming Zhang,Yujiong Shen,Weichao Wang,Xin Zhao,Shihan Dou,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang,Xuanjing Huang,Pluto Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SPIEval is a human-curated benchmark to test how well LLM-based mobile assistants use scattered personal information across apps; experiments show current models perform poorly, mainly due to failures in finding and verifying the right information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used as mobile assistants, they must handle user instructions that require accessing and integrating personal data distributed across many apps. There was no dedicated, realistic, and systematic benchmark to measure these capabilities, so it was unclear how well current models actually perform in this setting.

Method: The authors build SPIEval, a benchmark based on five cognitive capabilities critical for mobile assistance: reasoning, disambiguation, integration of information, user preference inference, and decomposition of multi-intent queries. SPIEval includes 250 tasks involving 4,335 personal records residing in 10 different apps, with interactions mediated by 21 tools that simulate app functionalities. They design tasks to support multi-turn interaction and to ensure outcomes are verifiable. They then evaluate nine representative LLMs on this benchmark, logging and analyzing their tool-use and retrieval behavior.

Result: Across nine LLMs, performance is modest: the best model (GPT-5.5 (xhigh)) reaches 57.3% accuracy, while the worst reaches 16.4%. Detailed failure analysis shows that about 79% of errors are due to inaccurate information localization, where models settle on plausible but wrong information instead of continuing retrieval or verification. Less than 2% of retrieval actions use advanced search strategies, and there is large variation between models in search efficiency and behavior.

Conclusion: SPIEval exposes substantial limitations of current LLM-based mobile assistants in using scattered personal information effectively. The dominant failure mode is inadequate retrieval and verification rather than pure reasoning limits. The benchmark, with its diverse, controlled, and verifiable tasks, provides a foundation for future work on better retrieval strategies, tool use, and cognitive capabilities in LLM-powered mobile assistants, and the authors publicly release data and code to support such research.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as mobile assistants, where a key challenge is leveraging personal information scattered across multiple applications (apps) to complete user instructions. However, due to the lack of dedicated benchmarks, their capabilities remain poorly understood. To address this gap, we introduce SPIEval, a human-curated benchmark grounded in five cognitive capabilities (i.e., reasoning, disambiguation, integration, preference inference, and multi-intent decomposition). SPIEval comprises 250 tasks spanning 4,335 personal records distributed across 10 apps and supports multi-turn interaction through 21 tools. Analysis shows that the benchmark exhibits diverse scenarios, challenging tasks, scattered information, controllable environments, and verifiable outcomes. We evaluate nine representative LLMs and find substantial room for improvement. The best-performing model, GPT-5.5 (xhigh), achieves only 57.3% accuracy, while the weakest achieves just 16.4%. Further analysis reveals that 79% of failures stem from inaccurate information localization, as LLMs often commit to plausible but incorrect information instead of continuing retrieval for verification. We also find that fewer than 2% of retrieval actions employ advanced search methods and observe substantial variation in search efficiency across models. These findings expose fundamental limitations of current LLM-based mobile assistants and motivate future research in this direction. Data and code are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Junjie-Ye/SPIEval.

</details>


### [32] [EVIL-Detect for NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 6: LLM-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10698)
*Hongrui Bao,Hangyu Rong,Zhuoshang Wang,Yubing Ren,Yanan Cao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EVIL-Detect is an ensemble framework that reliably detects LLM-generated and LLM-refined Chinese text, winning NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 6 with macro-F1 0.8888.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the rapid deployment of LLMs, distinguishing human-written, LLM-generated, and LLM-refined text in realistic Chinese settings is increasingly important for integrity, attribution, and safety, yet existing detectors often fail under distribution shifts.

Method: The paper proposes EVIL-Detect, a multi-signal ensemble detector combining (1) edit-extent regression to estimate how much text has been edited by an LLM, (2) zero-shot likelihood-contrast signals from LLMs, (3) lexical statistical features, and (4) conservative rule-based signals, all integrated via a conflict-aware fusion mechanism with calibrated decision boundaries to enhance robustness.

Result: On the NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 6 benchmark with strong out-of-distribution conditions, EVIL-Detect achieves a macro-F1 score of 0.8888, ranking first in the official evaluation.

Conclusion: A carefully calibrated, conflict-aware multi-signal ensemble significantly improves robustness of LLM-text detection in realistic Chinese scenarios, outperforming existing systems and providing a strong baseline for future work in LGT/HLT identification.

Abstract: The rapid development of large language models (LLMs) has increased the need for reliable detection of LLM-generated text, especially in realistic Chinese scenarios involving human-written text (HWT), LLM-generated text (LGT), and LLM-refined text (HLT). This paper presents EVIL-Detect, a multi-signal ensemble framework with conflict-aware fusion for NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 6. The system integrates edit-extent regression, zero-shot likelihood-contrast signals, lexical statistics, and conservative text rules. With calibrated decision boundaries and conflict-aware integration, our system improves robustness under strong out-of-distribution shifts, achieving a macro-F1 score of 0.8888 and ranking first in the official evaluation. Our code is available at https://github.com/bbbbhrrrr/evildetect.

</details>


### [33] [Most biomedical publications show signs of LLM-assisted writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10715)
*Lena Holzwarth,Rita González-Márquez,Dmitry Kobak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes and validates an unbiased method to estimate how often LLMs are used in academic writing by examining shifts in word frequencies, finding very high and growing LLM usage in biomedical research articles, especially in Discussion sections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based tools are increasingly used for academic writing, offering benefits (e.g., overcoming language barriers) but also raising concerns about misconduct and fraud. Policymakers and institutions need reliable estimates of how prevalent LLM-assisted writing is in scholarly literature, but existing detection and estimation methods are biased or unreliable. This motivates the development of a robust, corpus-level approach to quantify LLM usage without relying on fragile per-document detectors.

Method: The authors design an estimation framework that infers LLM usage from systematic changes in word frequency patterns associated with LLM-generated text. They identify an "LLM-associated vocabulary" and model how its prevalence changes over time in a large corpus. By comparing observed frequencies to expected baselines, they derive an unbiased estimate of the proportion of text likely influenced by LLMs. They apply this approach to full-text open-access biomedical articles from PubMed Central, and further stratify the analysis by article sections (e.g., Methods vs. Discussion) and paragraph-level granularity.

Result: Applying the method to PubMed Central biomedical papers, the authors estimate that by the end of 2025, 89% of papers exhibit an excess of LLM-associated vocabulary, suggesting widespread LLM involvement. They observe strong section-level differences: paragraphs in Discussion sections are estimated to be written with LLM assistance about 68% of the time, whereas Methods paragraphs are at 32%. Nonetheless, even within Methods sections, the overall prevalence of LLM usage exceeds 50%, indicating substantial penetration across article components.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLM-assisted writing has become pervasive in biomedical research publications and that its usage varies systematically across manuscript sections, being especially common in Discussions. The proposed word-frequency-based approach provides an unbiased way to estimate LLM usage at scale and can support the development of evidence-based guidelines and policies governing LLM use in scientific writing.

Abstract: Over the past several years, LLM-powered chatbots and agents have become widely used as a tool for academic writing. LLM-assisted writing can be valuable by removing language barriers but at the same time causes concerns about misconduct and fraud. To inform policy decisions, it is necessary to monitor the prevalence of LLM-altered texts in scholarly publications. Despite some recent progress in this direction, no existing method can produce reliable estimates. Here we suggest and validate a new unbiased approach to estimate LLM usage in a corpus of texts based on changing word frequencies. We apply our method to the full texts of open-access biomedical papers from Pubmed Central, and show that by the end of 2025, 89% of papers show excess of LLM-associated vocabulary. We also find that LLMs are twice as likely to be used when writing a paragraph in the Discussion section (68%) compared to a paragraph in the Methods section (32%), but even inside the Methods section, the overall prevalence of LLM usage is over 50%. We believe that our estimates are crucial to shape future guidelines and policies.

</details>


### [34] [Mitigating Context Interference for Reliable and Efficient Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10743)
*Boyang Xue,Bin Wu,Shuofei Qiao,Sheng Wang,Rui Wang,Yiming Du,Hongru Wang,Jeff Z. Pan,Emine Yilmaz,Kam-Fai Wong,Aldo Lipani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies and mitigates “context interference” in multi-turn LLM search agents, where newly retrieved but partly irrelevant documents hurt reliability and efficiency. It diagnoses where interference comes from, proposes a distillation-based context refiner, and shows that integrating this refinement into RL training improves multi-turn search performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-turn LLM search agents retrieve documents and reason across several steps, but each step adds more text, including irrelevant or distracting content. This long, noisy context can cause models to focus on the wrong evidence, lowering answer quality and wasting computation. There is little systematic analysis of which context parts cause the most harm or how to redesign agents to continually clean up their own context, especially within RL training setups. The paper aims to fill this gap and make search agents more reliable and efficient.

Method: 1) Empirically dissect the agent’s multi-turn context (prior reasoning, intermediate actions, past and current retrieved documents) to measure which segments most degrade performance when present or ablated, identifying the main sources of interference. 2) Based on findings that the latest retrieved documents are the dominant interference source, design a distillation-based context refiner module that takes the current query, history, and retrieved documents and outputs a compact, less noisy context. 3) Integrate this refiner into the agent’s loop (“refine context then generate”) and into RL training pipelines, so the agent learns policies that assume refined inputs and can co-adapt with the refiner. 4) Evaluate on multi-step search tasks in terms of accuracy, reliability, and efficiency (e.g., fewer steps or shorter contexts) with and without refinement and RL integration.

Result: The study finds that context interference is real and substantial, with the most recent retrieved documents being the main contributors to distraction and errors. The proposed distill-based context refiner effectively filters or summarizes these documents, reducing harmful noise. When plugged into existing multi-turn search agents, it improves reliability (higher task success rates, more faithful use of evidence) and efficiency (shorter or fewer interactions). Incorporating the refiner into RL training pipelines yields further gains beyond plug-and-play use, indicating a beneficial synergy between learned policies and context refinement.

Conclusion: Context interference, especially from newly retrieved documents, is a key bottleneck for multi-turn LLM search agents. A distillation-based context refiner that precedes generation in each step can substantially reduce this interference. Embedding such refinement into RL-based training further boosts agent reliability and efficiency. The work suggests a general design principle for AI agents: explicitly refine and compress evolving context before reasoning or generation, shifting from a “retrieve-then-generate” to a “refine-context-then-generate” paradigm.

Abstract: Recent research empowers Large Language Models (LLMs) as multi-turn search agents to iteratively retrieve and generate outputs until complex tasks are solved. However, the contexts of multi-turn search agents are lengthy and complex. For example, the retrieved set of documents in each turn would inevitably introduce irrelevant information that distracts LLMs, referring to \textit{context interference}, potentially hindering the reliability and efficiency of search agents. Therefore, we conduct a systematic study on context interference in multi-turn search agents, focusing on investigating i) which parts of the context of search agents will contribute to the context interference, ii) how to refine the contexts of search agents to mitigate the interference, and iii) can incorporating context refinement into search agent training yield further improvements. We reveal that interference primarily arises from the latest retrieved documents. Based on the explored findings, we then introduce a distill-based context refiner to dynamically mitigate context interference for multi-turn search agents. Finally, we validate that incorporating context refinement into RL training pipelines of search agents can significantly enhance both reliability and efficiency. This study highlights the importance of mitigating context interference of search agents, inspiring a novel paradigm of ``refine context and then generate'' for AI agents.

</details>


### [35] [Assessing Reliability of BERT-Based Models on Question Answering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10806)
*Pooja Yadav,Priyanka Harjule,Basant Agarwal,Marko Robnik Šikonja*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines how reliable BERT-based QA models are by measuring how stable their answers remain under model stochasticity (Monte Carlo Dropout) and paraphrased inputs, finding RoBERTa to be the most reliable and ALBERT/DistilBERT the least.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While transformer-based QA models achieve high accuracy, their reliability—how consistently they answer under small model or input variations—is underexplored, yet crucial for trustworthy deployment in real applications.

Method: Evaluate four encoder-only pretrained transformers (BERT, RoBERTa, ALBERT, DistilBERT) on QA tasks framed as classification, using SQuAD and QuAC. Induce internal stochasticity via Monte Carlo Dropout at inference with varying dropout rates, and apply paraphrasing to inputs to create lexical perturbations. Measure prediction consistency and perform statistical analyses to verify that MCD-based variability reflects reliability without changing the underlying inference dynamics.

Result: RoBERTa shows the most stable and consistent answers under both Monte Carlo Dropout and paraphrased inputs, while ALBERT and DistilBERT’s outputs fluctuate much more. Changes in dropout rate significantly affect consistency, but enabling MCD does not fundamentally alter how the models infer answers. Lexical paraphrasing can meaningfully impact answer stability, especially for the less robust models.

Conclusion: Reliability, defined as stability of predictions under internal and input perturbations, varies notably across BERT-based QA models and is not guaranteed by high accuracy alone. RoBERTa provides comparatively high reliability, whereas ALBERT and DistilBERT show substantial instability. Monte Carlo Dropout can serve as a valid, non-disruptive tool for reliability assessment. Practitioners should jointly evaluate accuracy and stability when selecting QA models for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Reliability estimation of large language models is in many cases as crucial as their accuracy, as reliable models are more trustworthy, robust, and suitable for practical applications. Recent advancements in natural language processing (NLP), particularly those based on transformer architectures, have significantly accelerated progress across various NLP tasks. This study focuses on the reliability of transformer-based question answering (QA) models, specifically BERT models and its variants (RoBERTa, ALBERT, DistilBERT). These encoder-only pretrained transformers have demonstrated remarkable accuracy in QA tasks that can be treated as classification tasks. However, their reliability remains underexplored. This study evaluates the reliability of four BERT-based models by assessing response stability under two conditions: (1) internal model variations induced via Monte Carlo Dropout (MCD) and (2) input perturbations through paraphrasing. Using the SQuAD and QuAC datasets, we investigate how dropout rates affect prediction consistency and whether lexical changes impact answer stability. Our findings reveal that RoBERTa maintains higher reliability, whereas AlBERT and DistilBERT exhibit significant inconsistencies. Statistical analyses confirm that enabling MCD during prediction does not disrupt inference dynamics, validating its effectiveness as a reliability metric. These findings underscore the importance of evaluating both accuracy and stability in QA models to ensure stability in real-world applications.

</details>


### [36] [Surfacing the Unsaid: CUE-Bench for Affective Stance in Chinese Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10810)
*Zhenyan Zheng,Yunyao Zhang,Junxi Sheng,Junqing Yu,Zikai Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a new Chinese benchmark (CUE Bench) for understanding unsaid and implicit emotions in discourse and show it improves emotion and intent recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard emotion benchmarks mostly label surface polarity or final emotion categories and miss how people convey emotions indirectly through politeness, irony, mismatch between words and feelings, or implicit cues. This makes current evaluations unable to expose model failures in deeper, pragmatic emotion understanding where affect is concealed, weakened, inverted, or reshaped.

Method: They design CUE Bench, a Chinese benchmark focusing on "unsaid" emotions in discourse. It centers on the notion of Affective Stance, defining nine human-interpretable stance types derived from the interaction between explicit and implicit polarity. For each instance, they annotate affective stance, communicative intent, and fine-grained emotion labels, enabling structured affective inference. They then run experiments with strong baseline models, with and without using Affective Stance as part of the modeling, to measure its impact.

Result: Using Affective Stance information in models yields better performance: fine-grained emotion recognition improves by 3.5 percentage points and pragmatic intent detection by 7.8 percentage points compared to strong baseline systems that lack stance modeling.

Conclusion: Modeling Affective Stance and providing structured annotations that capture the interaction of explicit expression, implicit affect, and pragmatic intent leads to more accurate and nuanced emotion understanding in discourse. CUE Bench fills a key gap in Chinese emotion evaluation by enabling systematic testing of models on indirect and pragmatically complex affective meaning.

Abstract: Emotion understanding in discourse requires reasoning beyond surface sentiment because speakers often convey affect through indirect, implicit, polite, ironic, or deliberately mismatched expressions. Existing emotion benchmarks mainly annotate surface polarity or final emotion categories, while lacking a structured account of how explicit expression, implicit affect, pragmatic intent, and fine grained emotion interact. This limitation makes current evaluations insensitive to cases where affective meaning is concealed, weakened, inverted, or pragmatically reshaped, thereby obscuring model failures in deeper emotion understanding. To address this gap, we introduce CUE Bench, a Chinese Unsaid Emotion benchmark that centers on Affective Stance and covers diverse communicative scenarios. CUE Bench constructs nine human interpretable affective stances from explicit implicit polarity interaction and further provides intent and fine grained emotion annotations for structured affective inference. Experiments show that incorporating Affective Stance improves fine grained emotion recognition by 3.5 percentage points and pragmatic intent detection by 7.8 percentage points over strong baselines.

</details>


### [37] [Reference-Free Post-Training of Open Large Language Models for Multilingual Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10812)
*Chris Han,Pengzhi Gao,Pei Fu,Jian Luan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They improve multilingual machine translation by applying reference-free reinforcement learning to an existing supervised model and interpolating checkpoints, achieving better quality than strong open and several proprietary systems across 46 languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual MT models, including open large language models, still have room for quality improvements, especially in a reference-free evaluation and training setting where test-time references are unavailable. The authors aim to improve translation quality across many languages using post-training methods that do not rely on parallel reference translations, while also outperforming both open-source and proprietary baselines.

Method: Starting from supervised-finetuned MiLMMT-46-v0.1 models, the authors apply Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning algorithm. The reward signal is based on the average of two reference-free quality estimation models and is gated with language identification to ensure language-appropriate evaluation. After RL, they linearly interpolate the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) checkpoints with the RL checkpoints to form MiLMMT-46-v1.0. They also explore on-policy distillation as an alternative or complement to RL with interpolation.

Result: Across 46 languages, the new MiLMMT-46-v1.0 models consistently outperform their SFT-only baselines in translation quality. They also surpass strong open-source baselines such as Seed-X, HY-MT2, and TranslateGemma. In reference-free evaluation metrics, they achieve leading scores compared to several proprietary machine translation systems, including Google Translate, Gemini 3 Pro, and GPT-5. On-policy distillation matches but does not exceed the performance frontier achieved by RL plus checkpoint interpolation.

Conclusion: Reference-free post-training via GRPO, combined with linear interpolation between SFT and RL checkpoints, is an effective strategy for improving multilingual MT quality in large language models, enabling them to outperform both open and proprietary baselines on reference-free metrics. While on-policy distillation can reach similar performance, it does not surpass RL with interpolation. The authors provide their models and code publicly to support further advancement in this area.

Abstract: We study reference-free post-training for multilingual machine translation with open large language models. Starting from the supervised-finetuned MiLMMT-46-v0.1 models, we apply Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a reward that averages two reference-free quality estimation models and is gated by language identification. We then linearly interpolate the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) model checkpoints to obtain MiLMMT-46-v1.0. Across 46 languages, the resulting models consistently improve translation quality over their SFT counterparts, outperform strong recent open baselines, including Seed-X, HY-MT2, and TranslateGemma, and achieve leading reference-free scores against evaluated proprietary systems such as Google Translate, Gemini 3 Pro, and GPT-5. We further investigate on-policy distillation and find that it reaches, but does not surpass, the quality frontier achieved by RL with checkpoint interpolation. We release the models and code to facilitate future research.

</details>


### [38] [VibeLifeBench: Can Your Life Agent Be Proactive and Persistent in a Living World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10875)
*Xiaohongshu Inc*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces VibeLifeBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on long-horizon, real-life assistance tasks in dynamic simulated environments, revealing large performance gaps in current models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent evaluations focus on short, static, and explicitly specified tasks, which do not reflect the demands of real-life personal assistance where tasks span weeks, environments change autonomously, and many constraints are implicit. There is no existing benchmark that tests proactivity, long-term consistency, and awareness of silent changes in such settings, creating a need for a more realistic evaluation framework.

Method: The authors design VibeLifeBench, a benchmark of 200 scripted long-horizon tasks across ten everyday-life domains, embedded in a simulated world with 22 mock services (e.g., calendars, email, shopping). Each task unfolds over a multi-week timeline governed by its own clock, with many environmental changes occurring silently. Agents must decide when to act, query, or stay idle, and must maintain coherent plans over time. Evaluation uses fine-grained, weighted checks that examine only the artifacts the agent leaves behind (states, actions, timing), scoring end states, timeliness, and adherence to implicit constraints.

Result: Seven state-of-the-art frontier LLM agents are evaluated on VibeLifeBench, and all achieve low scores across the defined metrics. This indicates that current systems struggle with long-horizon planning, proactive monitoring of a changing environment, and robust adherence to implicit constraints in everyday-life scenarios.

Conclusion: VibeLifeBench exposes a substantial gap between current LLM agent capabilities and the requirements of realistic, long-term personal assistance. By open-sourcing the tasks, environments, and evaluation framework, the authors aim to provide a standardized, challenging benchmark that can drive future research on more proactive, consistent, and situationally aware LLM agents for real-life use cases.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed as personal assistants. Existing evaluations, however, mostly use short, self-contained requests in static environments. Everyday life assistance is different. A task runs for weeks rather than minutes. The world keeps changing while the agent is not being prompted. Many constraints are never stated outright. An agent that merely answers the request in front of it will fail at such a task. What is needed instead is an agent that stays proactive and consistent. It decides on its own when to act, when to ask, and when to stay silent. It notices changes that nobody announced. It keeps one plan coherent from the first day to the last. No current benchmark measures this. We introduce VibeLifeBench, a benchmark of 200 long-horizon tasks across ten everyday-life domains. Each task is a scripted multi-week timeline in a simulated world of 22 mock services. The world advances on its own clock, and many of its changes are silent, so only an agent that re-inspects the world discovers them. Every task is graded by fine-grained, weighted checks that read only what the agent actually left behind, covering the end state, the timeliness of its actions, and whether it upheld the implicit constraints. We evaluate seven frontier models. All of them score low, which shows how far current agents are from assisting with real life. We will open-source all tasks, environments, and the evaluation framework.

</details>


### [39] [X2-Turn: Frame-Synchronous Dual-Head Modeling for Joint Streaming ASR and Turn State Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10878)
*Kaiqi Fu,Rime Wen,Altman Lin,Shawn Qin,Roy Gan,Hao Wang,Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes X2-Turn, a frame-synchronous turn-taking model that jointly predicts ASR tokens and fine-grained turn states in real time, improving accuracy and latency for spoken dialogue systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spoken dialogue systems must react appropriately to user speech in real time, distinguishing between interruptions, backchannels, and completed utterances. Existing modular methods usually predict turn state at the utterance or fixed-chunk level, which mismatches the continuous nature of turn-taking. They also often depend on a separate ASR model, adding latency and complexity and hurting responsiveness. A unified, low-latency, frame-level approach is needed.

Method: The authors build on the pretrained Voxtral Realtime model and add a frame-synchronous turn state head that shares streaming representations with the ASR head. This new head runs in parallel with ASR, using delayed-stream modeling to predict a fine-grained turn state label for every audio frame while the ASR head predicts tokens. Thus the model jointly performs streaming ASR and continuous turn state estimation from the same features.

Result: On the bilingual Chinese-English Easy-Turn benchmark, X2-Turn achieves accurate turn-taking detection with low latency, outperforming or matching prior modular approaches (details not given in the abstract but implied by “demonstrate its effectiveness”). The method works across both Chinese and English dialogue data.

Conclusion: Joint, frame-synchronous modeling of ASR and turn state using a shared streaming backbone (Voxtral Realtime) yields more accurate and responsive turn-taking decisions than modular, utterance- or chunk-level methods. X2-Turn reduces system complexity by eliminating a separate ASR module and is effective for bilingual Chinese-English dialogue, maintaining low latency while improving turn-taking prediction quality.

Abstract: Accurate and responsive turn-taking is essential for spoken dialogue systems, which must distinguish in real time between user interruptions, backchannels that should be ignored, and the completion of an utterance. Prior modular approaches typically optimize turn state prediction at the utterance or fixed-chunk level, creating a mismatch with the continuous turn state estimate, and often depend on an auxiliary ASR model, which limits responsiveness and increases overall system complexity. Therefore, we present X2-Turn, a frame-synchronous turn state prediction method via delayed-stream modeling. Specifically, building on the pretrained Voxtral Realtime model, we introduce a frame-synchronous turn state head that operates in parallel with the ASR head on shared streaming representations, jointly predicting ASR tokens and fine-grained turn states at the frame level. We evaluate our method on the bilingual Chinese-English Easy-Turn test sets, and the results demonstrate its effectiveness in achieving accurate turn-taking detection while maintaining low latency.

</details>


### [40] [Certify or Refuse: A Cross-Model Map for Selective Risk Control with Coverage Floors under Covariate Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10893)
*Jiamiao Liu,Dewen Qiao,Yu Zhang,Xuetao Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies certified selective prediction under automation floor; derives feasibility frontier and complexity tradeoffs under covariate shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In selective prediction, models can abstain, but real-world automation often requires answering at least some fixed fraction of incoming queries while maintaining error guarantees. Existing certification theory does not fully handle this “automation floor” under covariate shift between labeled source and unlabeled target data. The paper aims to understand when and how we can certify both a target error rate and a minimum answer rate, and what sample complexities (source vs target) are required.

Method: Formalize an automation floor constraint: answer at least a β fraction of target instances with error at most α under bounded-ratio covariate shift. Introduce the Floor Certification Map describing feasibility and a two-resource (labeled source vs unlabeled target) complexity tradeoff. Use local analysis under regularity assumptions (frontier margin, slack conditions, lattice conditions) to obtain lower and upper bounds. Consider three model classes: a pessimistic lower bound model (Model-B), an oracle-weight upper bound model (Model-A), and a practical implementable upper bound model (Model-B') under an exact stratified-shift structure with explicit nuisance costs. Analyze complexity via a localized accepted-region functional rather than global effective sample size.

Result: Derive a feasibility frontier for certifying both selection-conditioned risk α and automation floor β under bounded-ratio covariate shift. Establish matching (up to constants) lower and upper bounds on the sample complexity, decomposed into contributions from labeled source risk estimation and unlabeled target floor estimation. Show that the match occurs across different models (Model-B vs Model-A/B'), not via a single-model minimax result, and prove that no single unknown-weight procedure can be minimax-optimal over the whole bounded-ratio class (Model-B is inconsistent, e.g., at α=β=1/2). Demonstrate that complexity is governed by a localized accepted-region functional, not by global effective sample size, and that both axes of the lower-bound tradeoff vanish as β→0, highlighting the central role of the automation floor. Provide empirical evidence: a “registered bite” family with empirical log-log divergence slope about -2.002, zero violations in a 1,024-cell audit where certificates apply, and an honest refusal outcome on a SQuAD-to-NewsQA transfer feasibility audit.

Conclusion: Automation floors fundamentally change the certification problem in selective prediction under covariate shift, introducing a feasibility frontier and a two-resource sample complexity map that splits between labeled source and unlabeled target data. Achievable rates are local and depend on detailed structural conditions (frontier margins, lattice constraints), and minimax optimality cannot be achieved by a single unknown-weight procedure over the full bounded-ratio shift class. Practical certification is possible under structured shift models (e.g., stratified shift with priced nuisance), where implementable methods can match oracle-weight rates. The key quantity controlling complexity is a localized accepted-region functional, not global ESS, and empirical audits support the theoretical guarantees and also show that sometimes the only honest outcome is to refuse certification.

Abstract: Certified selective predictors attain whatever coverage they attain; operators impose an automation floor: answer at least a $β$-fraction of shifted target traffic with at most an $α$-fraction of answers wrong. Under bounded-ratio covariate shift we prove the Floor Certification Map: once that floor must be certified alongside the selection-conditioned risk $α$, certification acquires a feasibility frontier and a two-resource complexity map, additive up to constants: risk in labeled source, the floor in unlabeled target samples. The rates are local, needing a regular frontier margin, slack below the local-regime threshold, and lattice conditions: pre-registered with a lattice margin for the upper bounds, compatible per-slack for the lower. The displayed split is the operational route; oracle weights also allow a labeled-source floor estimate. Three model-tagged results: a lower bound (Model-B), a matching oracle-weight upper bound (Model-A), and an implementable upper bound (Model-B') valid under a pre-registered exact stratified-shift model with nuisance cost priced explicitly. The match is across these models rather than a single-model minimax theorem, and necessarily so: over the full bounded-ratio class no unknown-weight procedure matches at any sample size (Model-B is inconsistent, witnessed at $α=β=1/2$). The nuisance's necessity is only partially settled. Complexity tracks a localized accepted-region functional, not global effective sample size (ESS), on both sides, though a fixed-ESS separation theorem is left open; both lower-bound axes vanish as $β\to0$, so the floor creates the map. Empirically, the registered bite family diverges with log-log slope $-2.002$ within its pre-registered band; a 1,024-cell audit records 0 violations where the formal certificates fire; and a single-corpus SQuAD-to-NewsQA feasibility audit returns honest refusal.

</details>
