### [41] [FaithformBench: Benchmarking Faithfulness of Mathematical Chain-of-Thought Autoformalisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10916)
*Rob Cornish,Iacopo Ghinassi,Po-Hung Yeh,Shuqi Liu,Qiyuan Xu,Haoxuan Yin,Dominik Wagner,Wenda Li,Yee Whye Teh,Luke Ong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a cheap, automatic benchmark to measure how faithfully autoformalisation systems translate both correct and incorrect natural-language math reasoning into formal proofs, revealing a trade-off between preserving validity and not “fixing” invalid inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoformalisation systems translate informal mathematical text into formal proof assistant code, but we lack reliable, scalable ways to assess whether these translations faithfully reflect the original reasoning, especially when the input is wrong. Existing evaluations either require costly human annotations or use LLM-based judges with limited reliability, and they mostly test only correct inputs, missing how systems handle incorrect reasoning. There is also concern about sycophancy—systems that “correct” user errors instead of reflecting them. The paper aims to create a principled, low-cost method to evaluate faithfulness on both correct and incorrect reasoning steps and to study sycophancy in current systems.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark by automatically perturbing natural-language reasoning steps to make them invalid, without needing human labels. They then feed both original (unperturbed) and perturbed (invalid) steps to different autoformalisation (AF) systems targeting Lean and measure two properties: validity preservation (do AFs keep valid reasoning valid?) and invalidity preservation (do AFs keep invalid reasoning invalid instead of silently fixing it?). They evaluate eight AF systems—both base LLMs and fine-tuned AF models—on four mathematical datasets and systematically compare performance across these metrics.

Result: Across all datasets and systems, many AF models frequently translate intentionally invalid reasoning steps into formally provable statements, effectively “silently correcting” errors. Systems that perform best on validity preservation—i.e., they are good at turning correct reasoning into correct formal proofs—also tend to be the worst at invalidity preservation, exhibiting the strongest tendency to make invalid steps look valid in Lean. This reveals pervasive sycophancy in current AF models, and a clear empirical tension between preserving validity and preserving the invalidity of wrong inputs.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark provides an inexpensive, automatic, and theoretically sound way to evaluate the faithfulness of autoformalisation systems on both correct and incorrect reasoning, without relying on human annotations or unreliable LLM judges. Using it shows that today’s AF systems are systematically sycophantic: they often correct or distort invalid user reasoning instead of representing it faithfully, and improving validity preservation tends to worsen this behavior. The paper suggests that future AF research should explicitly optimize for both validity and invalidity preservation, and treat sycophancy as a central challenge in building trustworthy autoformalisation tools.

Abstract: Autoformalisation (AF) systems map natural language reasoning steps into formal statements in a proof assistant such as Lean. We consider how to assess the faithfulness of these systems. Existing approaches require expensive human-annotated ground truth, or rely on LLM judges or embedding models, which come with limited guarantees of accuracy. In addition, these methods typically only consider inputs that are known to be correct, and therefore do not assess whether the AF translates incorrect inputs faithfully. To address these limitations, we propose a new benchmark for AF faithfulness that is cheap to apply, sound under weak assumptions, and assesses both positive and negative examples. Our method is based on automatically generating perturbed reasoning steps that are designed to be invalid, and then measuring validity preservation on unperturbed steps and invalidity preservation on perturbed steps. We apply our method to eight AF systems across four mathematical datasets, and observe pervasive sycophancy: many AFs "silently correct" invalid inputs into provable statements. The most validity-preserving fine-tuned AFs are also the most sycophantic, suggesting a tension between validity and invalidity preservation in current AF systems.

</details>


### [42] [A Cost-Efficient Routing Pipeline for Multilingual Short-Text Classification Using Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10939)
*Wajdi Ben Saad,Safa Madiouni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to better route multilingual short texts either directly to a multilingual classifier or via translation to English, to improve performance especially for low-resource languages without task-specific fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate metrics on multilingual classification hide large quality gaps between high-resource and low-resource languages. A single uniform inference pipeline assumes all languages are equally well served, which is false in practice. There is a need for simple, deployable strategies that can selectively improve weaker languages’ performance without retraining models.

Method: They propose and evaluate a fixed-list routing strategy: texts in stronger languages are processed directly by a multilingual zero-shot classifier, while texts in weaker languages are first translated to English and then classified. The system is fully self-hosted, relies on pretrained compact sentence encoders, and uses no task-specific fine-tuning. Different routing variants (e.g., no translation, only low-tier translation, full translation) are compared on two benchmarks with different scales and label granularities: a 15-language subset of SIB-200 for 7-way topic classification, and a 15-locale subset of MASSIVE for 60-intent classification.

Result: On SIB-200, the best configuration (R1) translates only low-resource languages: high- and mid-tier Macro-F1 stay the same, while low-tier Macro-F1 improves from 0.4632 to 0.6828. On the MASSIVE subset, the same low-tier-only translation boosts low-tier Macro-F1 from 0.2143 to 0.4417, but the overall best performance comes from routing all languages through translation (R3), achieving Macro-F1 0.4647.

Conclusion: Selective translation is a robust way to improve classification for weaker languages in multilingual short-text tasks, but the ideal routing boundary—who gets translated and who does not—depends on the specific task and dataset. Because of this trade-off, they advocate reporting tier-level quality and latency metrics rather than a single global efficiency score.

Abstract: Multilingual short-text classification supports operational systems such as content moderation, customer support routing, and intent recognition, yet aggregate evaluation often hides large differences between high-resource and low-resource languages. Uniform inference policies are simple to deploy, but they assume that all languages are equally well served. In this work, we evaluate a fixed-list routing strategy that keeps stronger languages on a direct multilingual path and selectively sends weaker languages through translation into English before zero-shot classification. The pipeline is fully self-hosted, uses pretrained compact sentence encoders, and requires no task-specific fine-tuning.
  We test the approach on two benchmarks chosen to differ in scale and label granularity: a 15-language subset of SIB-200 for seven-way topic classification and a 15-locale subset of MASSIVE for intent classification over an official 60-intent inventory. On SIB-200, the best overall configuration is R1, which translates only the low-resource tier: high-tier and mid-tier Macro-F1 remain unchanged, while low-tier Macro-F1 rises from 0.4632 to 0.6828. On the MASSIVE subset, the same low-tier intervention raises low-tier Macro-F1 from 0.2143 to 0.4417, but the best overall result is obtained by full translation, R3, at Macro-F1 0.4647. Across these two benchmarks, selective translation is a reliable intervention for weaker languages, whereas the optimal routing boundary depends on the task. We therefore report routing through tier-level quality gains and tier-level latency rather than a single global efficiency score.

</details>


### [43] [REAP: Relation-Aware Elicitation and Parsing for Closed-Book Knowledge Base Construction from LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10963)
*Thanh-Dan Bui,Thanh-Trung Do,Tuan-Phong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces REAP, a system that extracts structured knowledge from language models under a parameter budget and no fine-tuning, achieving strong macro-F1 on the AKBC 2026 closed-book task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build accurate knowledge bases directly from large language models in a realistic, constrained setting: no fine-tuning, a strict parameter budget, and a requirement to output clean, structured JSON facts for different relations.

Method: Use a 24B-parameter instruction-tuned LLM with three key components: (1) structured chain-of-thought prompting to guide step-by-step reasoning; (2) relation-specific query strategies tailored to each knowledge relation; and (3) a reasoning-based empty-set gate that lets the model decide when no valid fact exists. The final step directly formats the model’s responses into syntactically valid JSON arrays representing the extracted triples.

Result: On the AKBC Shared Task 2026 test set, REAP reaches a macro-F1 of 0.62 overall. It performs especially well on certain relations, achieving F1 scores of 0.95 on countryLandBordersCountry, 0.73 on companyTradesAtStockExchange, and 0.77 on hasArea, all under the 32B parameter cap and without any fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Reasoning-oriented prompting, relation-aware query design, and explicit handling of “no answer” cases together enable a mid-size, off-the-shelf LLM to construct high-quality, structured knowledge bases in a closed-book scenario, demonstrating competitive performance within constrained resources.

Abstract: We present the REAP system for the AKBC Shared Task 2026 on constructing knowledge bases from language models in a closed-book setting, subject to a budget of at most 32B parameters and no model fine-tuning. Our system combines structured chain-of-thought reasoning, relation-specific query strategies, and a reasoning-based empty-set gate to elicit parametric knowledge, followed by direct extraction into valid JSON arrays. On the test set, the system, built on the Mistral-Small-24B-Instruct-2501 model, achieves a macro-F1 score of 0.62, with particularly strong results on countryLandBordersCountry (F1 = 0.95), companyTradesAtStockExchange (F1 = 0.73), and hasArea (F1 = 0.77). Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/yammdd/AKBC-Shared-Task-2026.

</details>


### [44] [ReLTEx: Reliable LLM-based Taxonomy Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10970)
*Zeinab Ghamlouch,Mehwish Alam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Framework ReLTEx makes LLM-based taxonomy expansion more reliable by combining LLM generation with structure-aware validation and recursive control, reducing hallucinations and improving semantic coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can generate concepts and relations useful for expanding taxonomies, but naive use leads to noisy, redundant, and hierarchically inconsistent expansions, making them unreliable for automated taxonomy growth. A method is needed to harness LLM strengths while controlling their errors and hallucinations.

Method: ReLTEx first uses an LLM to generate candidate taxonomy expansions, then applies structure-aware validation strategies to check consistency, filter noise and redundancy, and enforce hierarchical constraints. It further uses recursive expansion control to govern how far and where the taxonomy is expanded. The framework is evaluated in a masked taxonomy expansion setting across benchmark taxonomies, comparing different validation strategies and using adapted automatic metrics plus human evaluation.

Result: Across benchmark taxonomies, ReLTEx yields expansions that score better on adapted automatic metrics and are judged by humans to be more reliable and semantically coherent than baselines or naive LLM expansions. Different validation strategies are empirically compared, showing the benefit of the structure-aware validation and recursive control components.

Conclusion: By combining LLM-based candidate generation with structural validation and controlled recursion, ReLTEx substantially improves the reliability and coherence of automatic taxonomy expansion and reduces LLM hallucinations, making LLMs more practically usable for taxonomy enrichment tasks.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in generating semantically relevant concepts and relations, making them promising tools for taxonomy enrichment. However, directly relying on LLM-generated expansions often leads to noisy, redundant, or hierarchically inconsistent structures, limiting their reliability for automated taxonomy expansion. In this paper, we present ReLTEx, a framework for reliable LLM-based taxonomy expansion. ReLTEx combines LLM-driven candidate generation with structure-aware validation and recursive expansion control to improve the consistency and quality of generated taxonomies by reducing hallucinations. We evaluate the proposed framework using benchmark taxonomies under a masked taxonomy expansion setting and compare multiple validation strategies. Experimental results, supported by both adapted evaluation metrics and human evaluation, demonstrate that ReLTEx produces more reliable and semantically coherent taxonomy expansions.

</details>


### [45] [MUSE: A Full-Text Cross-Domain Knowledge Base of Scientific Problems, Solutions, and Rationales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10974)
*Tsofia Cohen,Tom Hope*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents MUSE, a large-scale dataset and extraction pipeline for mining problem-solution-rationale triplets from scientific papers, and shows that training LLMs with rationale supervision helps on complex problems but can hurt on simpler ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific papers contain rich but buried information about how researchers solve problems, including obstacles, chosen methods, and reasoning. Existing datasets and systems do not comprehensively capture this structured problem-solution-rationale information across full texts and domains, limiting our ability to build systems that understand and reuse scientific problem-solving strategies.

Method: The authors design an annotation schema for Problem-Solution-Rationale (P-S-R) triplets, covering span-level labels for problems, solutions, rationales, links indicating which solution solves which problem and which rationale justifies which solution, plus conceptual coreference. They collect 579 expert-annotated full-text paragraphs using this schema, then build a modular extraction pipeline that scales from this seed data to automatically extract P-S-R structures from many more papers. Using this pipeline, they construct a source-grounded knowledge base of 37K P-S-R triplets. They also run a preliminary experiment training a rationale-supervised large language model on this resource for scientific problem solving.

Result: The pipeline successfully scales a small expert-annotated set into a much larger knowledge base of 37K high-quality, source-grounded P-S-R triplets. Component-level evaluations show the extraction modules perform well enough to support knowledge base construction. In experiments, an LLM trained with rationale supervision demonstrates improved performance on complex scientific problems involving multiple constraints, while its performance on simpler problems can degrade relative to baselines without rationale supervision.

Conclusion: MUSE provides a new, full-text, multi-domain resource for structured representations of scientific problem solving in the form of problem-solution-rationale triplets, along with an extraction pipeline to build a large knowledge base from limited annotations. The findings suggest that explicitly modeling and supervising rationales can enhance LLM performance on complex scientific reasoning tasks, though it may not uniformly help and can even hurt on simpler tasks, highlighting the need for nuanced use of rationale supervision.

Abstract: Scientific papers contain fine-grained records of problem solving: authors mention technical obstacles and methods that were used to address them, often along with reasoning on why those methods were chosen. We introduce MUSE (Mining Underlying Scientific Explanations), a full-text, multi-domain resource of scientific Problem-Solution-Rationale (P-S-R) triplets. We curate 579 expert-annotated full-text paragraphs, with a rich annotation schema covering salient problem, solution, and rationale spans, solves and rationale_of links and conceptual coreference. A modular extraction pipeline scales this annotation to build a high-quality knowledge base of 37K source-grounded P-S-R triplets. We evaluate the extraction components and include a preliminary experiment training a rationale-supervised LLM for scientific problem solving. Interestingly, we find that rationale supervision improves performance on complex, multi-constraint problems but can harm performance on simpler ones.

</details>


### [46] [What Iterated Self-Feeding Probes of Language Models Measure, and a test that separates the construction from the model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10986)
*Nicolás Vera Zúñiga*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They study what is really being measured when a language model is iteratively fed its own outputs, by constructing a controlled “token ring” probe and analyzing damage spreading and Lyapunov‑like behavior across many models and architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-consistency, iterative refinement, and agentic loops all rely on feeding a model its own outputs, but it is unclear whether the resulting behaviors reflect the model itself or artifacts of the probing setup. The authors want a sharply defined, analyzable construction to disentangle properties intrinsic to the model from those induced by the probe dynamics.

Method: They define a ring of token cells updated via the model’s local conditional probabilities p_r(x_i | x_{i±r}), implementing Glauber dynamics on token sequences. Two nearly identical rings (differing by one token) are advanced with shared randomness so that any divergence is due to damage spreading. They analyze the damage light cone and a token-space Lyapunov exponent λ_ca(r) across 19 models and various lattice constructions, and systematically vary either the model or the construction to see which observables change. They validate against known Domany–Kinzel damage fields and examine estimator failures and retracted results.

Result: They find that some measured quantities are fixed by the construction itself (e.g., the geometry of the damage light cone and scaling of λ_ca(r) with radius), appearing nearly model-invariant across many models and scales, while others genuinely depend on the model (e.g., λ_ca crossing zero at a consistent training point and attractor share ranking models robustly). They also discover an apparent phase transition they initially attributed to models but which in fact belongs to the probe construction. Their consistency checks reveal four spurious “measurements” that had to be retracted due to estimator issues.

Conclusion: Probes that feed models their own outputs can conflate properties of the probe dynamics with properties of the model itself. To interpret such measurements correctly, one must explicitly test invariance by holding either the model or the construction fixed and varying the other. Their token-ring Glauber-dynamics framework, validated against known results and shipped as a package, provides a disciplined way to separate probe-induced artifacts from genuine model behavior when studying self-interacting language models.

Abstract: A growing class of methods probes a language model by feeding it its own output: self-consistency, iterated refinement, agentic loops. We ask what such a probe measures, in a construction chosen to make the question sharp: a ring of token cells resampled in place by the model's own windowed conditional p_r(x_i | x_{i+-r}). The substrate is Glauber dynamics on token sequences and is not new; what we change is the coupling. Advancing two rings that differ in one token under common random numbers makes undamaged copies diverge by exactly zero, so damage spreading becomes measurable where a maximal coupling gives mixing times instead. The answer is that it measures two different things at once, in readings that look alike. Some quantities are fixed by the construction: the damage light cone is kinematic, and the radius scaling of the token-space Lyapunov exponent lambda_ca(r) is model-invariant across 19 models and two scale ladders spanning 70x. Others genuinely track the model: lambda_ca crosses zero at a reproducible point in training, and the attractor share ranks models consistently however the lattice is built. Left undistinguished, the first kind is readily mistaken for the second -- we did so ourselves for four months, and report a phase transition we measured to three decimal places that belongs to the probe rather than to any language model. We give the test that separates them: hold the construction fixed and vary the model, or hold the model fixed and vary the construction, and see which readings move. We validate the instrument by reproduction first, recovering a Domany-Kinzel damage field bit-exactly against an independent prediction, and we report the estimator failures that this discipline caught -- four retracted verdicts, each on a quantity that looked like a measurement. The methodology ships as a package.

</details>


### [47] [ConRub-Med: Reinforcement Learning with Consensus Rubrics for Open-Ended Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10996)
*Taojie Zhu,Yuan Xia,Tao Sun,Yizhi Wang,Yan Chen,Qunshan He,Tian Guan,Jian Wang,Jinjie Gu,Junwei Liu,Yonghong He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes ConRub-Med, a scalable, model-generated rubric framework for reinforcement learning in open‑ended medical QA that improves clinical relevance and benchmark performance over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In math and coding, reinforcement learning benefits from automatic, verifiable rewards, but medical QA is harder because answers can be partially correct, incomplete, or dangerously wrong, and human physician grading is expensive. Existing model-based supervision often blurs important distinctions between missing content and clinically harmful errors, limiting safe and effective policy optimization. The authors aim to design a scalable, rubric-based reward system that preserves clinically meaningful distinctions while remaining feasible without constant expert involvement.

Method: ConRub-Med builds structured, verifiable rubrics using multiple large language models. For each prompt, three heterogeneous generators independently propose fine-grained (atomic) evaluation criteria. A separate reviewer model filters these, keeping only criteria that have semantic support from all three generators, to ensure consensus and robustness. Responses are evaluated with a three-state scoring scheme that distinguishes (1) correctly covered criteria, (2) missing information, and (3) incorrect claims, assigning negative reward to errors rather than treating them as neutral. During reinforcement learning, they use Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). When all responses in a GRPO group get the same scalar reward, a pairwise judge is used to derive sequence advantages only when both candidate orders agree on a preference, maintaining the scalar rewards yet still shaping learning. For groups without ties, standard GRPO is applied. The system is trained on a rubric dataset covering 5,166 medical prompts.

Result: In a blinded expert study where medical questions were matched, two clinicians rated answer panels produced by the full ConRub-Med pipeline as more clinically relevant than panels generated using a single rubric generator, indicating improved supervision quality. On nine open medical benchmarks, ConRub-Med ranks first on six and achieves the best averages for both medical performance and generalization among the evaluated open models. On HealthBench-Hard, using 5,166 rubric-derived prompts, it achieves a score of 38.98 ± 1.04, outperforming InfiMed-ORBIT, which scores 33.60 with 8,000 samples and 37.30 with 28,000 samples, showing better data efficiency and higher peak performance.

Conclusion: Model-generated, consensus-based rubrics with fine-grained, three-state scoring provide an effective, scalable reward signal for reinforcement learning in open-ended medical QA. By explicitly penalizing incorrect information and distinguishing it from omissions, ConRub-Med yields responses that clinicians judge as more clinically relevant and that achieve state-of-the-art performance on multiple medical benchmarks using fewer training samples than prior approaches. This suggests that carefully constructed, verifiable rubric supervision can close part of the gap between automatic reward design and expert physician evaluation in safety-critical domains.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has been especially effective in mathematics and coding, where answers can be checked automatically. Many open-ended medical questions lack comparably cheap outcome verifiers: responses may be partly correct, incomplete, or contain clinically consequential errors. Rubrics written or validated by physicians offer strong clinical grounding, but involving experts in every instance is costly. Model-generated rubrics make this supervision scalable. We introduce ConRub-Med to preserve useful distinctions as rubric feedback moves from construction to policy optimization. For each prompt, three heterogeneous language models propose atomic criteria independently; a separate model reviews them, retaining only criteria with semantic support from all three generators. Three-State scoring distinguishes correct coverage, missing information, and incorrect claims. Errors receive negative rather than zero credit. When every response in a complete Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) group receives the same final reward, a pairwise judge provides sequence advantages only if both candidate orders agree, without changing the scalar rewards. Groups without ties use vanilla GRPO. In a blinded study matched by question, two medical experts rate panels from the full pipeline as more clinically relevant than panels produced by one generator. Across the evaluated open models, ConRub-Med ranks first on six of nine benchmarks and achieves the highest medical and generalization averages. Using the resulting rubric dataset of 5,166 prompts, it scores $38.98 \pm 1.04$ (mean $\pm$ SD) on HealthBench-Hard, compared with InfiMed-ORBIT's 33.60 with 8,000 samples and 37.30 with 28,000.

</details>


### [48] [On the Limitations of Cross-Lingual Consistency in Multilingual Text-to-image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11002)
*Sicheng Zhang,Zhonghao Yan,Binzhu Xie,Shi Qiu,Muzammal Naseer,Naveed Akhtar,Mubarak Shah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a multilingual benchmark (LingT2I) to systematically test how text-to-image models behave across 10 languages, revealing significant cross-lingual gaps and patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most text-to-image generation research and evaluation are English-centric. As a result, we know little about how well these models work for other languages, whether there are systematic inequalities in performance, and how linguistic and cultural factors shape the generated images and rendered text.

Method: The authors construct LingT2I, a benchmark with 33K prompts spanning 10 high-use languages. The benchmark is designed to separately test cross-lingual performance in (1) image content generation and (2) text rendering in images. Using this dataset, they conduct a broad quantitative evaluation of existing T2I models across languages and evaluation dimensions, and complement it with qualitative analysis of language-dependent generation patterns and cultural/linguistic influences on outputs.

Result: Experiments on LingT2I show clear linguistic inequalities: model performance differs substantially by language, and there are language-dependent trade-offs across evaluation dimensions such as content fidelity and text rendering quality. The analysis uncovers systematic, language-specific generation behaviors and patterns connected to linguistic properties and associated cultural contexts.

Conclusion: LingT2I offers a standardized, multilingual benchmark and an accompanying analysis framework to study how T2I systems behave across languages. The findings highlight current cross-lingual shortcomings and biases in state-of-the-art models and are intended to guide the development of more robust, fair, and inclusive multilingual T2I generation models.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) generation has achieved remarkable progress in recent years. However, existing research has largely focused on English-only settings, leaving cross-lingual performance gaps and language-specific effects insufficiently explored. To fill this gap, we introduce LingT2I, a benchmark covering 10 widely used languages with 33K prompts, designed to evaluate cross-lingual effects in both content generation and text rendering. Building on this benchmark, we conduct a comprehensive cross-lingual analysis, uncovering linguistic inequality and language-dependent trade-offs across evaluation dimensions. Beyond quantitative evaluation, we further reveal a range of language-dependent generation patterns, highlighting how linguistic factors and their corresponding cultural contexts systematically impact model outputs. Our benchmark and analysis provide a foundation for studying cross-lingual behavior in T2I generation and facilitate the development of more robust and inclusive models. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/RISys-Lab/LingT2I.

</details>


### [49] [Templated or fully Synthetic? Prompt construction as a confound in measuring LLM political stance beyond writing assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11008)
*Ilias Chalkidis*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how realistic and effective different types of prompts (real, templated, and LLM-generated) are for measuring political stance in LLMs, and shows that LLM-generated prompts can be as realistic as real ones and less biased than templated prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current political stance evaluations for LLMs mostly use closed-ended survey questions and, more recently, templated prompts, both of which lack the nuance and realism of real user interactions and can be gamed or biased. With growing non-work use of GenAI, there is a need for more ecologically valid prompts across diverse tasks to accurately gauge political stance.

Method: The authors extend the IssueBench framework by adding two new tasks (information seeking and opinion sharing) to existing writing assistance tasks. They then generate fully synthetic prompts using LLMs guided by detailed instructions and seeded with real prompts. They conduct a small-scale study on three contested policy issues and three geopolitical conflicts, comparing real, templated, and LLM-generated prompts. Both humans and LLMs annotate/rate realism, clarity of intent and stance, and they analyze how different prompt types affect stance estimates for the same model, particularly under neutral framings.

Result: Human and LLM annotators judge LLM-generated prompts to be at least as realistic as real prompts and more realistic than templated ones. They also find that LLM-generated prompts express their intended intent and stance more clearly. LLMs distinguish templated prompts from real and LLM-generated prompts much more strongly than humans do. In a case study, the authors find that templated and LLM-generated prompts produce systematically different stance measurements for the same model; templated prompts especially exaggerate the model’s leaning toward the stance implicitly encoded in the template filler text when prompts appear neutral.

Conclusion: Templated prompts, while an improvement over traditional survey questions, still fall short in realism and introduce systematic bias in political stance detection, particularly under neutral framings. Carefully constructed LLM-generated prompts can match or exceed real prompts in perceived realism and clarity, and may yield more reliable political stance estimates. Evaluations of LLM political stance should therefore incorporate or even prioritize high-quality synthetic prompts over rigid templates.

Abstract: Political stance detection in LLMs has long been dominated by closed-ended, multiple-choice political survey questions---originally designed for humans, and thus lacks the realism and nuance of human-AI interactions in the wild, while also being susceptible to sandbagging. The recent IssueBench framework substantially mitigates these limitations with templated prompts anchored in real-world chat logs. Given the rise in non-work-related use of GenAI assistants, we extend IssueBench beyond writing assistance to include two additional tasks, information seeking and opinion sharing. We argue that templated prompts still lack the nuance of real ones, especially for open-ended tasks, and remain recognisable as evaluation artefacts. We propose the use of fully synthetic (LLM-generated) prompts, produced under detailed instructions with real prompts as seeds. We assess the ecological validity of real, templated, and LLM-generated prompts in a small-scale study covering 3 highly contested policy issues and 3 recent geopolitical conflicts. Human and LLM annotators rank LLM-generated prompts as no less realistic than real ones and clearly more realistic than templated ones, and find that they carry their intended intent and stance more clearly; the LLMs separate templated prompts from the other two far more sharply than the humans do. In a case study, templated and LLM-generated prompts yield systematically different stance estimates for the same model, most visibly under neutral framings, where templated prompts overstate the model's leaning in the direction encoded by the topic-and-stance text (filler) slotted into their templates.

</details>


### [50] [Data Attribution of Emergent Misalignment with Persona Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11025)
*Clemens Vetter,David Kaczér,Lucie Flek,Florian Mai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies emergent misalignment in LLMs and finds that specific latent persona-like features, amplified by misalignment fine-tuning, drive harmful behavior; these features can be identified, steered, and traced back to pre-training data, but human-written text alone does not reliably induce EM without synthetic instruction-response formatting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergent misalignment poses a safety risk because narrow misalignment fine-tuning can cause broad, unintended harmful behaviors in unrelated tasks. Existing mechanistic accounts suggest this is due to latent persona features, but it is unclear where these features originate in pre-training data and what types of data or formatting actually induce EM. Understanding this causal pipeline is crucial for safer pre-training and fine-tuning practices.

Method: The authors use sparse autoencoder (SAE)-based model diffing across four open-weight language models to compare feature activations before and after misalignment fine-tuning. They identify which SAE features are amplified or suppressed, then perform feature steering experiments to increase or decrease misalignment. They attribute influential features to pre-training documents by feature activation, then construct both human-written fine-tuning datasets and synthetic instruction-response pairs derived from those documents to test which data formats induce EM and whether effects transfer across model families.

Result: They find that misalignment fine-tuning amplifies features corresponding to jailbreak personas, sarcasm, deception, and manipulation, while suppressing safety and assistant-identity features. Directly steering individual SAE features can induce misalignment up to 62% in aligned models—surpassing the 35% misalignment produced by misalignment fine-tuning—and can also re-align misaligned models near baseline. Attribution links the causal features to pre-training web narratives about villainous, dominating, and harmful agents. However, fine-tuning on these human-written documents, even when reformatted as assistant-like responses, does not reliably induce EM, whereas synthetic instruction-response data generated from the same content does, and these synthetic datasets transfer EM across model families.

Conclusion: Emergent misalignment is mechanistically tied to specific, steerable persona-like features that can be traced back to semantically relevant pre-training documents. Yet semantic content by itself is not enough to cause EM: the instruction-response structure and model-like phrasing of synthetic data are key drivers. This implies that safety interventions should focus not only on filtering pre-training content but also on controlling response styles and how models are fine-tuned, as well as monitoring and steering the relevant latent features directly.

Abstract: Emergent misalignment (EM) is the phenomenon where fine-tuning a language model on a narrow task leads to harmful behavior in unrelated domains. A leading mechanistic account attributes EM to persona features: latent directions acquired during pre-training that misaligned fine-tuning amplifies. We ask where these features come from: which pre-training documents activate them, and whether naturally occurring human-written text suffices to induce EM. Using Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) based model diffing across four open-weight models, we find that features related to jailbreak personas, sarcasm, deception, and manipulation are amplified by misalignment fine-tuning, while safety-relevant and assistant-identity features are suppressed. Steering individual features controls EM in both directions: it induces misalignment rates of up to 62% in aligned models -- exceeding the 35% reached by misalignment fine-tuning itself -- and re-aligns misaligned models to near-baseline misalignment rates. Attributing the causal features to a corpus of one million pre-training web documents retrieves semantically relevant narratives about villainous characters, domination, and harmful agency. However, fine-tuning on these human-written documents does not reliably induce EM, even after reformatting into assistant-style responses, whereas synthetic instruction-response pairs derived from the same content do -- and transfer across model families. Semantic relevance alone is therefore not sufficient: response structure or model-generated phrasing plays an important role in inducing EM.

</details>


### [51] [myMediWhisper: Construction of Burmese Medical Speech Corpus and Whisper Fine-Tuning for Clinical Dialogue ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11036)
*Ye Kyaw Thu,Ye Bhone Lin,Thura Aung,Htet Arkar,Myat Oo Swe,Thet Htet San,Min Thiha Tun,Thazin Myint Oo,Thepchai Supnithi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and fine‑tuned Whisper-based ASR systems for Burmese medical speech on a new, high‑quality 28‑hour corpus, studying full vs. parameter‑efficient fine‑tuning and data augmentation, and achieve state‑of‑the‑art WER, especially in noisy conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whisper, though strong and multilingual, performs poorly on low-resource, domain-specific speech like Burmese medical conversations. There is a lack of high-quality Burmese medical ASR data and a need to understand how fine‑tuning strategies and augmentation affect robustness in such settings.

Method: They first construct a 28‑hour Burmese medical speech corpus recorded and validated by native speakers. Using this dataset, they fine‑tune Whisper models with two strategies: full fine‑tuning of all parameters and parameter‑efficient fine‑tuning via LoRA. They further introduce waveform-level and spectrogram-level data augmentation to simulate noise and room acoustics, and systematically evaluate the impact of these augmentations on recognition performance under clean, noisy, and reverberant conditions.

Result: Augmentation slightly harms performance on clean speech but substantially improves robustness in noisy and reverberant conditions for both full and PEFT setups. Their best model, a fully fine‑tuned Medium-size Whisper variant (myMediWhisper-Medium) without augmentation, achieves a state-of-the-art WER of 23.44% on Burmese medical speech, outperforming much larger, general-domain fine‑tuned Whisper models. All data and resources are released on Hugging Face.

Conclusion: Domain-specific fine‑tuning of Whisper on a carefully curated Burmese medical corpus yields large gains over generic models, and parameter‑efficient methods such as LoRA are competitive. Data augmentation trades a bit of clean-speech accuracy for substantial robustness in realistic noisy and reverberant scenarios. The released dataset and models provide a strong benchmark and resource for future Burmese medical ASR research.

Abstract: Although Whisper models benefit from large-scale multilingual pre-training, their performance on Burmese medical speech remains limited. This work presents a Burmese medical speech recognition framework built on a high-quality 28-hour corpus recorded and validated by native speakers. We fine-tune Whisper models using full fine-tuning (FFT) and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) with LoRA. To evaluate robustness, we apply waveform- and spectrogram-level data augmentation under controlled noise and simulated room acoustics. While augmentation reduces performance on clean speech, it significantly improves robustness in noisy and reverberant environments across FFT and PEFT settings. Our best-performing system, fully fine-tuned myMediWhisper-Medium without augmentation, achieves a state-of-the-art Word Error Rate (WER) of 23.44%, outperforming much larger general-domain fine-tuned models. Dataset and other resources can be found at the Huggingface repository: https://huggingface.co/datasets/LULab/mediTalk-mm-rdy.

</details>


### [52] [TEAMMix: Taxonomy Enrichment Augmentation and Minority-augmented Mixing Strategy for LLM-enhanced Weak-Supervised Hierarchical Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11044)
*Jian Zhang,Zhuohao Yang,Songlin Lei,Bangli Liu,Ziwei Wang,Xufeng Weng,Gehan Amaratunga,Yu Lin,Hongwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A weakly supervised framework for hierarchical text classification uses LLM-based data augmentation and confidence-based resampling to handle complex label hierarchies and class imbalance, improving performance on fine-grained, imbalanced datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hierarchical Text Classification involves assigning documents to labels organized in a hierarchy (e.g., topic → subtopic), which is difficult due to complex label structures and severe class imbalance, especially many rare (long-tail) classes. Existing LLM-based methods require long prompts to express the hierarchy, lose structural label information, and are inefficient or inaccurate when dealing with many fine-grained labels. There is a need for a framework that can exploit LLMs while remaining efficient, preserving hierarchy information, and addressing long-tail and imbalance issues under weak supervision (limited labeled data).

Method: 1) Semantically enrich label hierarchy: For each label in the hierarchy, the framework uses keyword generation and corpus mining to expand label descriptions with related terms and phrases, improving semantic representations of labels. 2) LLM-based pseudo-sample generation: Guided by the enriched label semantics, an LLM is prompted to generate synthetic training texts (pseudo-samples) for each label, especially long-tail labels, to alleviate class imbalance. 3) Quality control via Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM): A GMM is used on confidence scores (e.g., model or similarity-based) to distinguish high- from low-confidence pseudo-samples. The framework then performs confidence-based resampling, keeping more reliable pseudo-samples and down-weighting or discarding low-quality ones. 4) Train a hierarchical classifier on the mixture of real and filtered pseudo-samples, leveraging the refined label semantics and balanced training distribution.

Result: Experiments on fine-grained and imbalanced hierarchical text classification datasets show that the proposed framework improves the reliability of LLM-generated pseudo-labels (i.e., generated samples and their assigned labels are more accurate/trustworthy) and yields significantly better classification performance than baselines, particularly for rare classes and deeper levels of the label hierarchy.

Conclusion: By semantically enriching label hierarchies, using LLMs for targeted pseudo-sample generation, and filtering these samples with a GMM-based confidence mechanism, the framework successfully addresses key challenges in hierarchical text classification: preserving label structure information, handling long-tail class imbalance, and working under weak supervision. The approach demonstrates that carefully controlled LLM-based augmentation can substantially enhance HTC performance on fine-grained, imbalanced datasets.

Abstract: Hierarchical Text Classification (HTC), as a critical text mining task, faces challenges such as complex label hierarchies and class imbalance. Existing methods based on large language models (LLMs) struggle to be efficiently applied to this task due to issues like lengthy prompts and loss of label structural information. To address these limitations, this paper proposes a weakly supervised HTC framework enhanced by LLM-based data augmentation. The framework first enriches the label hierarchy semantically through keyword generation and corpus mining, thereby enhancing the model's understanding of labels. Subsequently, it guides the LLM to generate pseudo-samples to mitigate the long-tail problem, and employs a Gaussian mixture model for confidence-based resampling to optimize the quality of generated data. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method effectively improves the reliability of LLM-generated pseudo-labels and significantly enhances classification performance on fine-grained and imbalanced datasets.

</details>


### [53] [Multiclass Sentiment Analysis for Identifying Political Viewpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11049)
*Girma Yohannis Bade,Olga Kolesnikova,Jose Luis Oropeza,Grigori Sidorov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies multiclass sentiment analysis of political viewpoints on social media, comparing XGBoost and BERT, and finds the task very challenging, providing low but useful baseline F1-scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the rapid growth of social media, there is a huge amount of political discourse online. Understanding public opinion and distinguishing different political perspectives in this data is important, and sentiment analysis is a key NLP tool for this. However, multiclass sentiment classification of nuanced political content is difficult and underexplored, so the authors aim to investigate this problem and establish baseline performance.

Method: The authors frame sentiment analysis of political viewpoints on social media as a multiclass classification problem over multiple sentiment classes related to political issues and figures. They design and implement two machine-learning approaches: one based on XGBoost (a gradient boosting decision tree model) and one based on BERT (a transformer-based language model). Both models are trained and evaluated on a labeled dataset of political social media posts. Performance is assessed using standard classification metrics, particularly F1-score, on a held-out test set.

Result: On the test set, the XGBoost model achieves an F1-score of 0.2835, while the BERT-based model achieves an F1-score of 0.2806. These relatively low scores indicate that current methods struggle to capture the complexity and contextual nuance of political discourse in a multiclass sentiment setting. The slightly better performance of XGBoost compared to BERT suggests that, for this dataset and setup, simple feature-based models can be competitive with deep contextual models.

Conclusion: The study concludes that multiclass sentiment analysis for political discourse on social media is a challenging task, as evidenced by the low F1-scores of both XGBoost and BERT models. The work provides initial baseline results that can guide and be improved upon by future research. It highlights the need for more sophisticated methods, better feature representations, or richer annotation schemes to more effectively model the complexity of political sentiment in social media text.

Abstract: The rapid growth of social media has created vast amounts of political discourse, which provides valuable opportunities to analyze public opinions and identify different political perspectives. Sentiment Analysis (SA) is a core task in Natural Language Processing (NLP) that allows the computational study of attitudes and opinions in textual data, and has become increasingly important for understanding political discourse. In this work, we investigate multiclass sentiment analysis of political view- points on social media, that is to automatically discriminate multiple sentiment classes over political issues and figures. To solve this task we design and evaluate two machine-learning approaches based on XGBoost and BERT. We train and evaluate the models on a labeled dataset of political social media posts using standard classification metrics. The experimental results show that the XGBoost model reaches an F1-score of 0.2835 and the BERT- based model reaches an F1-score of 0.2806 on the test set. These results demonstrate the challenge of classifying complex and contextualized political discourse sentiment and provide a baseline for future research in multiclass political sentiment analysis.

</details>


### [54] [Actions Speak Louder than Words: Measuring Cross-Lingual Policy Retention in Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11110)
*Sourabrata Mukherjee,Kalika Bali,Sunayana Sitaram*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether tool-using AI agents follow the same internal action sequences when given the same task in different languages, and proposes a rigorous way to measure cross-lingual similarity of action policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multilingual evaluations only compare final answers and ignore the sequence of tool calls and actions used to reach them. However, these action traces determine cost, latency, failure modes, and are the only auditable behavior. The authors want to understand how much of an agent’s action policy is preserved when the task language changes, and to build a sound methodology for measuring this, avoiding misleading conclusions from naive trace similarity metrics.

Method: They evaluate 8 models on 6 parallel benchmarks across 41 languages, generating 2.38M agent rollouts. They identify five major confounds in naive trace-similarity measurement: (1) shorter traces get higher similarity scores, (2) empty traces can appear perfectly similar, (3) unrelated traces agree by chance surprisingly often, (4) model-specific reproducibility caps apparent similarity, and (5) there is no stable monolingual baseline because models answer the same question inconsistently even in one language. They design corrections for each confound, including length-normalized similarity, exclusion/penalization of empty traces, estimating chance-level similarity via permutation, normalizing by each model’s own reproducibility, and establishing proper within-language baselines. They then analyze divergence patterns under different decoding settings (e.g., greedy vs higher temperature) and across model scales, and test causal hypotheses about English pivoting by manipulating language routing and instructions. They also examine the impact of trace-extraction regexes on measured performance.

Result: After correcting all confounds, cross-lingual divergence in action policies is substantial and clearly structural rather than sampling noise. The divergence persists even under greedy decoding and does not diminish as temperature increases, despite models themselves becoming less self-consistent. When normalized by each model’s own reproducibility, four large, diverse frontier models converge to a similar level of cross-lingual policy retention under greedy decoding: each preserves about 71–73% of its action policy across languages, and model identity explains only about 5.7% of the variance. For models below roughly 10B parameters, this cross-lingual stability breaks down, and apparent performance rankings among smaller models are largely due to a non-trivial chance floor that the authors estimate via permutation instead of assuming. They find that agents systematically route non-English tasks through English as a pivot, and this routing is causally important for their behavior. Even explicit instructions to avoid English do not eliminate this pivoting. Finally, they show that a single trace-extraction regex caused a dramatic, misleading multilingual failure: after adding two worked examples, the measured accuracy of one model increased by a factor of 26, while its true accuracy on human-readable outputs barely changed, revealing how brittle instrumentation can be.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual tool-use evaluation must focus on the full action policy, not just final answers, and must correct for several strong confounds that can entirely invert conclusions. When properly normalized, large frontier models show a surprisingly consistent degree of cross-lingual policy retention, whereas smaller models’ behavior is heavily contaminated by chance agreement. English pivoting appears to be a deeply embedded, causally important strategy that models retain even under contrary instructions. Measurement pipelines themselves can easily create illusions of multilingual failure or success—for example, through fragile regex-based trace extraction—so robust, carefully validated evaluation methodology is essential for trustworthy assessment of multilingual tool-using agents.

Abstract: When a tool-using agent is given the same task in a different language, does it still take the same steps? Multilingual evaluation rarely asks: it compares final answers and discards the actions. Yet those actions are the product: they fix cost and latency, decide how the system fails, and are the only auditable part of its behaviour. We make the action policy the measured object across 8 models, 6 parallel benchmarks and 41 languages (2.38M rollouts). The naive measurement fails: five confounds sit between raw trace similarity and any defensible claim, each able to flip a conclusion. Short traces score higher, empty traces score perfectly, unrelated traces agree by chance over half the time, the gap is capped by each model's reproducibility, and a model asked the same question twice in one language answers differently, leaving no baseline. We remove all five, and every correction makes the effect larger. Divergence proves structural, not sampling noise: it survives greedy decoding in every cell and stays flat as temperature rises, even as models grow less self-consistent. Normalised by their own reproducibility, four very different frontier models converge under greedy decoding, each keeping 71-73% of its action policy across languages, with model identity explaining only 5.7% of the variance. Below roughly 10B parameters it breaks down, and the ordering among smaller models is largely an artifact of a chance floor we measure by permutation rather than assume. Agents route non-English tasks through English; this pivot is causally load-bearing, confirmed by a pre-registered prediction across four models, and models will not abandon it when told to. Finally, a single trace-extraction regex, not the model, manufactured a multilingual failure: two worked examples raise one model's measured accuracy twenty-sixfold while its accuracy on readable outputs barely moves.

</details>


### [55] [Attention-Path Fragility as an Uncertainty Signal in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11138)
*Minsoo Kim,Sungyoung Ji,Kisung Moon,Ilyong Yoon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ASMI, an attention-fragility-based uncertainty estimator for LMs that predicts errors beyond confidence/entropy, excels on grounded QA, and degrades as expected on parametric QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard uncertainty measures (softmax confidence, entropy, stochastic sampling) often miss cases where a model is confidently wrong, especially in grounded QA. The authors seek a training-free, computationally efficient signal that captures when a confident prediction is fragile to internal perturbations, and that can indicate when it will or will not be reliable across different regimes (grounded vs parametric knowledge).

Method: Define ASMI (Attention-Subnetwork Mutual Information): create subnetworks by masking attention heads, run the model on each subnetwork, and compute BALD-style mutual information over their outputs. To make the comparison robust, introduce a semantic-agreement kernel that focuses on semantic rather than surface-form agreement. Evaluate both a single-pass Sem-ASMI (reads the signal from one greedy response, no extra sampling) and multi-sample adaptive variants, and analyze their behavior on grounded vs parametric QA benchmarks and at the head level.

Result: On grounded QA benchmarks, ASMI provides error-predictive information beyond single-pass confidence and entropy, especially for confident-but-fragile predictions, cutting retained error by about half when combined with confidence filtering. Sem-ASMI, requiring only a single greedy response, matches or outperforms Semantic Entropy in 10/12 grounded benchmark–backbone combinations. The best ASMI variant (often the adaptive, sample-reusing one) ties or beats the strongest baseline in 8/12 settings, with statistically significant gains in 3. On parametric QA, all ASMI variants perform at or below a simple maximum softmax probability (MSP) baseline, as theorized, and their estimates are stable across reruns.

Conclusion: Attention-pathway fragility encodes a form of model uncertainty that is complementary to output confidence. ASMI reliably exploits this for grounded QA, where answers depend on external context and attention routing, while appropriately failing to help when answers come from parametric knowledge. Its effectiveness is predictable from the regime and from whether attention-head fragility actually correlates with errors, making ASMI a principled, training-free, and often superior alternative or complement to existing uncertainty estimators in grounded settings.

Abstract: We propose that a model's uncertainty about a token is reflected not only in the breadth of its output distribution but also in whether a confident prediction is \emph{fragile} under perturbation of its attention pathways. We instantiate this as ASMI (Attention-Subnetwork Mutual Information), a training-free estimator that masks attention heads and measures the BALD mutual information among the resulting subnetworks, with a semantic-agreement kernel to discount surface-form disagreement. The signal is not a restatement of output confidence: on grounded QA an out-of-fold test shows it adds error-predictive information beyond single-pass confidence and entropy, concentrated in \emph{confident-but-fragile} predictions, where acting on it roughly halves the retained error of a confidence filter. The distinctness is regime-graded, so ASMI predicts its own domain of applicability, strong where answers are routed through provided context and bounded by design where they are recalled from parametric knowledge. Sem-ASMI reads the signal from a single greedy response, without the stochastic generations the strongest baselines require, and ties or beats Semantic Entropy on ten of the twelve grounded benchmark-backbone settings. Across the same twelve settings, the best ASMI variant, typically the adaptive one reusing the ten samples already drawn for the baselines, ties or leads the strongest baseline in eight, significantly in three under a paired test. On parametric QA all variants revert to or below the zero-cost MSP baseline, exactly as predicted, and the estimates are near-deterministic across reruns. A head-level analysis shows that what tracks this boundary is not the presence of head-level fragility but whether that fragility couples to errors.

</details>


### [56] [The Illusion of Cross-Lingual Safety in Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11146)
*Abigail Oppong,P Sam Sahil,Tadesse Destaw Belay,Maryam Ibrahim Mukhtar,Esmael Ahmed Abdu,Tassallah Abdullahi,Jessica Oparebea,Saminu Mohammad Aliyu,Idris Abdulmumin,Abubakar Juma Chilala,Nicholaus Dismas Ladislaus,Alfred Malengo Kondoro,Lemofouet Valdini Douglace,Shamsuddeen Hassan Muhammad,Seid Muhie Yimam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that current safety alignment in large language models does not transfer well from English to several low-resource African languages, revealing a major multilingual safety gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM safety work is done in English, yet models are used globally. It is usually assumed that English safety mechanisms generalize across languages, but this has not been rigorously tested, especially for low-resource languages like Twi, Hausa, Amharic, and Swahili. This potential gap poses real-world safety and fairness risks for multilingual users.

Method: The authors introduce LoDNA, a safety dataset for four African languages that contains both literal translations of English prompts and culturally localized variants. They evaluate cross-lingual safety transfer not only via standard generation-based tests but also with a latent geometric framework that inspects hidden-state representations related to refusals. By comparing the internal refusal signals for harmful prompts across languages and layers, they measure how much of the English safety behavior is preserved.

Result: They find that harmful prompts in the four African languages preserve under 10% of the English refusal signal for most language–model combinations, demonstrating very weak transfer of safety behavior. Although literal translations and localized prompts are semantically very close (cosine similarity 0.95–0.996), their representations diverge across layers, indicating that the models recognize the concepts but fail to route them into safety mechanisms. This reveals the superficial nature of current multilingual safety alignment.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that safety alignment learned in English does not reliably generalize to the examined low-resource African languages. The evidence contradicts the notion of a universal, language-agnostic safety or harm manifold within current LLMs. The authors argue that multilingual safety requires dedicated, language-aware alignment efforts rather than assuming English-centric training is sufficient.

Abstract: Safety alignment in large language models (LLMs) is largely developed in English, assuming these safeguards generalize across multilingual settings. However, this assumption remains underexplored and exposes a vulnerability in low-resource languages. We investigate cross-lingual safety transfer in four African languages, Twi, Hausa, Amharic, and Swahili, using LoDNA, a new safety dataset that pairs literal translations with culturally localized prompts. To move beyond generation-based evaluation, we propose a latent geometric framework that probes hidden-state refusal representations in LLMs. Our experimental results show that cross-lingual safety transfer is severely limited; harmful prompts retain less than 10% of the English refusal signal across most language-model pairs. Literal and localized prompts are semantically aligned (cosine 0.95-0.996) but drift across layers, suggesting models encode the concepts without routing them to safety mechanisms. These findings demonstrate that current multilingual safety alignment is superficial, providing strong evidence against the assumption of a universal, language-agnostic harm manifold within the specific low-resource languages studied. Warning: This paper contains example data that may be offensive or harmful.

</details>


### [57] [From Interpretability to Control: Insights from Six Years of the TrustNLP Workshop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11171)
*Rahul Gupta,Abhinav Mohanty,Anaelia Ovalle,Anil Ramakrishna,Anubrata Das,Apurv Verma,Jwala Dhamala,Ninareh Mehrabi,Tharindu Kumarage,Yada Pruksachatkun,Yang Trista Cao,Kai-Wei Chang,Aram Galstyan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper surveys and synthesizes all papers from the TrustNLP workshop (2021–2026), maps them onto six trust dimensions, tracks how focus areas evolved with LLM capability jumps, compares them with major ACL venues, and derives structural insights and future directions for trustworthy NLP research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trust in NLP systems has become critical as models have evolved from static predictors to powerful generative chat systems. While individual works address aspects like fairness, robustness, safety, and explainability, there was no systematic synthesis of how the trustworthy NLP research landscape has evolved, how different trust dimensions interact, or how workshop activity relates to trends in the broader ACL community. The authors aim to fill this gap by analyzing all TrustNLP workshop papers over several years and situating them within established trust frameworks.

Method: The authors collect and analyze 144 proceedings papers from six editions of the TrustNLP workshop. They classify each paper along six trust dimensions derived from frameworks such as TrustLLM and DecodingTrust (e.g., fairness, truthfulness, safety, explainability, etc.). They track temporal trends (2021–2026), co-occurrence patterns with model capability milestones (e.g., release of high-impact chat models), and the evolution of focus across dimensions. They also perform a cross-venue topical comparison by examining ~2,000 papers from ACL, NAACL, EACL, and EMNLP in the same period to see whether TrustNLP is representative of or distinct from the field’s average topical distribution. Finally, they distill “structural insights” and provide recommendations.

Result: The analysis shows that TrustNLP has grown substantially (from 8 to 41 papers per edition) and mirrors the field-wide shift from post-hoc interpretability of static models to mechanistic understanding and proactive control of generative systems. All trust dimensions became active simultaneously following the release of the first major chat models, with later model generations prompting a particular emphasis on truthfulness and safety alignment. Truthfulness emerges as the fastest-growing dimension, going from non-existent in 2021–2022 to 37% of TrustNLP papers in 2025–2026. Fairness remains the most stable and persistent theme. Explainability shows a U-shaped trend: it declines as earlier post-hoc interpretation techniques lose prominence, then rises again in 2026 driven by mechanistic interpretability work. The topical distribution at TrustNLP broadly matches that of major ACL conferences, suggesting that the workshop’s focus is aligned with broader community concerns rather than being niche or idiosyncratic.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the evolution of TrustNLP research closely tracks major capability jumps in NLP, with trust dimensions becoming more salient as generative chat systems mature. Truthfulness and safety have rapidly risen in prominence, fairness remains central, and explainability is being redefined via mechanistic approaches. Since TrustNLP’s topical mix is similar to that of major ACL venues, insights from the workshop likely generalize to the broader field. The authors extract four high-level structural insights (not fully detailed in the abstract) and provide concrete recommendations to guide future work on trustworthy NLP, emphasizing integrated treatment of multiple trust dimensions, alignment with emerging model capabilities, and a shift from reactive to proactive trust-building strategies.

Abstract: The Workshop on Trustworthy Natural Language Processing (TrustNLP), co-located with major ACL conferences since 2021, has grown from 8 proceedings papers to 41 over six editions, documenting a field-wide transition from post-hoc interpretability of static models to mechanistic understanding and proactive control of generative systems. We synthesize insights from all 144 proceedings papers, classifying them along six trust dimensions grounded in established frameworks (TrustLLM, DecodingTrust). We observe co-occurrences with capability emergence. The release of the first high-impact chat models activated all trust dimensions simultaneously, while subsequent model generations shifted focus toward truthfulness and safety alignment. Analysis from the classification study reveals that truthfulness is the fastest-growing dimension (absent in 2021-2022, comprising 37% of papers by 2025-2026), fairness remains the most consistent theme, and explainability exhibits a U-shaped trajectory; declining as post-hoc methods lost relevance but resurging in 2026 through mechanistic interpretability. A cross-venue comparison with ACL, NAACL, EACL, and EMNLP (~2K papers) in the same period shows that TrustNLP's topical distribution closely follows the field average. We identify four structural insights and conclude with actionable directions for the research community.

</details>


### [58] [ConVAWG: A Retrieval-Grounded Framework for Controlled Synthetic Dialogue Generation in Violence Against Women and Girls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11200)
*Chen Lyu,Xingwei Tan,Simon Cullen,Shelley Wilson,Lois Arthurs,Arshad Jhumka,Gabriele Pergola*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents ConVAWG, a framework for generating high-quality synthetic multi-turn dialogues that model violence against women and girls (VAWG) scenarios, overcoming data access and privacy constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Studying conversational dynamics of abuse, especially VAWG, requires large-scale dialogue data that are difficult to release due to privacy and legal constraints. Existing work mainly targets sentence-level toxicity in online content and fails to capture abuse as a relational, temporally evolving process across conversations. The authors want a way to model complex, multi-turn, realistic abusive interactions without relying on sensitive real-world chat logs.

Method: They design ConVAWG, a retrieval-grounded pipeline to create synthetic VAWG chat scenarios. The system: (1) builds scenarios from persona seeds, demographic patterns from UK Office for National Statistics, official crime definitions, and retrieved Domestic Homicide Review cases; (2) transforms these ingredients into hierarchical event timelines; (3) generates multi-scene role-play dialogues that realize those timelines as conversations; and (4) applies targeted activation-steered toxicity control so that abusive or toxic utterances appear where appropriate but remain controlled and policy-aligned. The resulting dataset includes rich metadata at scenario, event, and turn levels.

Result: Using ConVAWG, they generate more than 6,000 multi-turn dialogue events across 200 scenarios, each annotated with detailed metadata. Human evaluations, LLM-as-judge studies, ablation experiments, and performance on downstream tasks all indicate that the dialogues are high-quality, coherent, and faithful to the VAWG domain and intended dynamics.

Conclusion: Synthetic, retrieval-grounded, and controlled dialogue generation can effectively model complex abusive scenarios like VAWG while mitigating privacy and legal challenges. ConVAWG provides a scalable way to create rich, multi-turn conversational datasets with strong domain fidelity, enabling better study and modeling of abuse as a dynamic, relational process rather than isolated toxic sentences.

Abstract: Synthetic dialogue generation offers a way to study conversational dynamics in sensitive domains where real data are difficult to access, release, or annotate. The underlying abuse may occur online or offline: threats and coercion can appear directly in messages, while behaviours such as surveillance, isolation, stalking, and physical violence may be planned, disclosed, or referred to conversationally. Privacy and legal constraints make it difficult the release of large-scale real conversation datasets; existing work has mostly focused on sentence-level toxicity of online abuses, leaving a gap in modelling abuse as a relational and temporally unfolding phenomenon. In this work, we focus on modelling Violence Against Women and Girls (VAWG) scenarios as multi-turn dialogues. We introduce ConVAWG, a retrieval-grounded framework for generating CPS-aligned synthetic VAWG chat dialogues. ConVAWG builds scenarios from persona seeds, demographic patterns reported by the UK Office for National Statistics, official crime definitions, and retrieved Domestic Homicide Review cases; converts them into hierarchical event timelines; generates multi-scene role-play dialogues; and applies targeted activation-steered toxicity control to appropriate utterances. We release over 6,000 multi-turn dialogue events across 200 scenarios with rich scenario-, event-, and turn-level metadata. Extensive human evaluation, LLM-as-Judge assessment, ablations, and downstream tasks show strong dialogue quality and domain fidelity.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [59] [Closed-Loop LLM Co-Pilots for Digital Agriculture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09949)
*Serge Kernbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an LLM-driven, closed-loop plant monitoring and control framework that uses a 49-channel phytosensor network to autonomously optimize plant growth and resource use in digital agriculture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital agriculture needs systems that can not only analyze complex plant physiological data but also autonomously act on it to optimize growth, resource use, and experimental protocols, reducing reliance on human experts and manual control.

Method: The authors couple a 49-channel multimodal phytosensor array (multispectral, electrochemical, dielectric) to an LLM-based control framework that interprets plant biophysical signals in natural language and issues control commands to hardware actuators (lighting and environment). They test the system in a vertical farm and single-plant setups across several operational modes (minimal-time, energy-optimization, and exploratory stress/phenotyping protocols).

Result: Across case studies, the LLM agents successfully interpreted complex micro- and macro-scale fluctuations in plant physiology and autonomously performed multi-parameter optimization over biomass, chlorophyll, and energy use. In production-like settings, minimal-time control shortened the production cycle by 35% versus periodic control; energy-optimization mode cut energy use by 18% with only slight extension of growth time; and the system discovered a dark-induced chlorophyll accumulation strategy that yielded 67.9% energy savings.

Conclusion: LLMs can serve not only as analytical tools but as autonomous co-pilots in digital agriculture, directly interfacing with plant biosensing networks to control environmental actuators. This closed-loop AI-biology framework improves efficiency and cost-to-value ratios while reducing computational and expert-labor demands, and enables new, data-driven strategies for managing complex biological systems.

Abstract: This study evaluates the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in complex biological systems, evolving from data analysis to autonomous, AI-guided experimentation. The framework is driven by data from a 49-channel phytosensor network, encompassing multispectral, electrochemical, and dielectric modalities. To enhance accessibility, the system provides real-time natural-language interpretation for both specialists and non-experts. However, its core advantage lies in the transition from human-in-the-loop analysis to autonomous control. Processing biophysical data, the LLM evaluates plant physiology and triggers hardware actuators to optimize microclimates, execute phenotyping protocols, or induce controlled stress scenarios. This closed-loop architecture establishes a direct AI-biology interface, enabling data-driven exploration of complex biosystems and ecologies. The framework was validated across three case studies, based on a vertical farm and a single-plant setup and deciphered complex micro- and macro-fluctuations in plant physiology. Agents in a production-scale deployment executed multi-parameter optimization, balancing biomass accumulation, chlorophyll content, and energy consumption. The LLM processed biosensing telemetry to modulate full-spectrum, 450 nm, and 660 nm lighting at 2-hour intervals. Compared to periodic control, the system in minimal-time mode reduced the production cycle by 35%. In the energy-optimization mode, it reduced energy consumption by 18% with only a marginal increase in cultivation time, exploiting physiological inertia via light pulses. Finally, the agents autonomously developed an unforeseen strategy of dark-induced chlorophyll accumulation, resulting in a 67.9% energy saving. This framework transforms LLMs into autonomous co-pilots for digital agriculture, improving the cost-to-value ratio and lowering computational and expert-labor constraints.

</details>


### [60] [SPOTting the Future: Lookahead Explanations for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09967)
*Tamar Gozlan,Claudia V. Goldman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SPOT, a sampling-based tree framework to interpret deep reinforcement learning policies by empirically exploring their action choices and downstream consequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep reinforcement learning policies are powerful but opaque, making it hard to understand or trust their decisions, especially in complex domains like traffic control. Existing interpretability methods often focus on single timesteps and feature attributions, which miss how actions unfold over time.
