Abstract: Generative AI makes social-engineering attacks more fluent, adaptive, and scalable, increasing the need for LLM-based de- fenders that can protect users during ongoing interactions. We ask whether such defenders identify the structural source of risk or merely react to surface cues. We formalize trust-chain localization: identifying whether an interaction fails at actor authority, asset control, verification sufficiency, or transaction path. We construct a controlled 300-case online-housing corpus spanning 20 scenario families, legitimate cases, four structural failure modes, and three surface conditions. Five defender models are evaluated on the same corpus in state- ful turn-by-turn and one-shot static settings, yielding 1,500 model-case evaluations per protocol and 3,000 in total. No model produced explicit unsafe compliance, yet defensive effectiveness varied sharply: intervention rates ranged from 0% to 96.3%. Protective action and correct structural localization were frequently decoupled, with models sometimes intervening while identifying the wrong trust component or recognizing a structural failure without taking protective action. Asset-control failures were a major localization bottleneck, surface sensitivity varied across models, and live-static differences were model-dependent. These findings show that safe-looking behavior alone is insufficient; live scam resistance must separately measure intervention, timing, structural localization, and false-positive behavior.

</details>


### [80] [Interpreting Language Model Hidden States at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10260)
*Jordan Pettyjohn,Mansi Sakarvadia,Nathaniel Hudson,Daniel McKenzie,Kyle Chard,Ian Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniLens is a scalable, unified lens framework for interpreting LLMs across all component types and widths, using low-rank translators and efficient KL approximations to dramatically cut parameters and memory while enabling wide, dense lens coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lens methods for interpreting LLMs are expensive and do not scale well: lens parameters grow quadratically with model width, KL-based training over the full vocabulary is memory-heavy, and lenses are often tied to specific component types. As a result, prior trained lenses have only been applied to models up to ~20B parameters and primarily focus on limited parts of the network (e.g., residual streams), preventing model-wide interpretability and limiting insights into where behaviors emerge and where interventions are most effective.

Method: The paper introduces OmniLens, a single lens family that can be applied to any activation type (residual, attention, MLP) and any model width. It uses two main scaling techniques: (1) low-rank affine translators that reduce per-lens parameter growth from quadratic to linear in model width, cutting trainable parameters by up to 98.4%; and (2) Subset-KL training, which only materializes a subset of vocabulary logits. Subset-KL has a Top-k mode that selects the largest logits to reduce peak memory by up to 70%, and an importance-sampled mode that yields unbiased stochastic gradients approximating the full KL objective. Using these techniques, the authors train a dense ensemble of 482 lenses across LLaMA-3.3-70B.

Result: OmniLens enables training a large, dense ensemble of 482 lenses across the entire LLaMA-3.3-70B model, achieving six times the coverage of a residual-stream-only design at the same depth. The broader coverage reveals that: (1) the components where certain behaviors are most visible are not necessarily the components where interventions are most effective; and (2) the most effective intervention points often lie outside the attention heads that have been the focus of prior lens analyses. In experiments on tasks such as prompt-injection detection, multi-hop memory injection, and toxicity localization, OmniLens matches key results from previous work while using substantially fewer resources.

Conclusion: OmniLens provides a scalable, unified framework for applying lenses across all components and widths of large language models, overcoming the parameter and memory bottlenecks of previous trained lenses. Its low-rank translators and Subset-KL objective make dense, model-wide lens ensembles feasible even for 70B-parameter models. This expanded coverage yields deeper interpretability insights—distinguishing where behaviors are visible from where interventions are effective—and reproduces prior interpretability results at much lower computational cost.

Abstract: Lens methods interpret large language models (LLMs) by mapping intermediate activations to the output vocabulary, revealing how next-token predictions develop through the network. Trained lenses remain expensive: affine-translator parameters grow quadratically with model width, while exact, full-vocabulary Kullback--Leibler (KL) training dominates memory. Consequently, prior trained lenses have been applied to models of at most 20B parameters and remain tied to particular component types. We present OmniLens, which applies a single lens family to any model-width activation, whether residual stream, attention, or MLP, and combines two independent scaling techniques. First, low-rank translators make per-lens parameter growth linear in model width and reduce trainable parameters by up to 98.4%. Second, Subset-KL materializes only selected vocabulary logits: its Top-k mode cuts peak training memory by up to 70%, while its importance-sampled variant retains unbiased stochastic gradients for the full KL. These savings enable a dense ensemble of 482 lenses for LLaMA-3.3-70B, providing 6x the coverage of a residual-stream design at the same depth. Model-wide coverage then reveals what single-component lenses cannot: the components where a behavior is most visible need not be those where intervention is most effective, and the most effective interventions lie outside the attention heads examined by prior lens studies. Across three case studies (prompt-injection detection, multi-hop memory injection, and toxicity localization), OmniLens reproduces key published results at substantially lower cost.

</details>


### [81] [Logit-Boundary Geometric Belief Interfaces and Sparse Sheaf-Enclave Protocols: A Self-Contained Substrate for Secure Network Electronic Health Record (EHR) Interoperability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10300)
*Alvin Spivey,Thomas Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a mathematically rigorous boundary interface for exchanging clinical data between AI systems and operational health IT, and shows via a synthetic benchmark that a small open LLM completely fails to satisfy the strict interface contract.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health-record interoperability requires multiple heterogeneous components (legacy EHRs, LLMs, terminology and identity services, human reviewers) to exchange information safely and reliably. Existing systems either expose too much internal complexity or too little structure, making it hard to manage uncertainty, provenance, and safety. The authors want a principled, mathematically defined boundary that constrains how model outputs become operational FHIR transactions, emphasizing certificates and fail-closed behavior rather than end-to-end clinical correctness.

Method: They define a "logit boundary" architecture where generative or discovery models only propose pre-threshold categorical scores, while a deterministic substrate decides admissibility, routing to review, or quarantine before constructing FHIR transactions. This is formalized as a Geometric Belief Interface (GBI), combining finite boundary semantics, Dirichlet evidence modeling, sheaf-theoretic constructs (cellular sheaves, mapping cones) for diagnostics, geometric audit charts, and a Decentralized Cryptographic Sheaf-Enclave (DCSE) protocol sketch for secure, fail-closed deployment. They then instantiate a frozen synthetic benchmark, GBI BoundaryBench v0.1, and test one specific LLM (Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507) on 256 tasks under three evidence modes, yielding 768 executions against a fixed contract for admission and validation.

Result: Across 768 model executions, all runs completed but none were accepted by the strict benchmark contract. 369 outputs failed at safe parsing and 399 failed schema validation, leading to zero tasks covered and universal deterministic quarantine of model outputs under the given boundary interface.

Conclusion: A rigorously specified boundary interface like GBI can enforce strong, certificate-based constraints at the model-to-system boundary, successfully failing closed when model outputs violate structural or semantic requirements. The specific negative benchmark result for Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 is not a claim about general LLM capability or clinical safety, but evidence that, under a strict admission boundary, current small open models may fail to produce contract-compliant, operationally admissible outputs. The framework is about specifying and verifying boundary contracts, not about solving clinical truth or global representation alignment.

Abstract: Electronic health-record interoperability is a boundary problem: legacy systems, generative models, terminology services, identity systems, and human reviewers may each expose rich internal states, while operational exchange requires a narrow shared interface of typed claims, bounded uncertainty, provenance, and explicit admission or abstention. This paper details a mathematical and engineering architecture for that interface. The organizing idea is the logit boundary: a discovery model may propose pre-threshold scores over a local categorical decision, but a deterministic judgment substrate decides whether the proposal is admissible, requires review, or must be quarantined before any Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR) transaction is constructed. The resulting Geometric Belief Interface (GBI) combines finite boundary semantics, local Dirichlet evidence, cellular-sheaf and mapping-cone diagnostics, advisory geometric audit charts, and a Decentralized Cryptographic Sheaf-Enclave (DCSE) protocol sketch for fail-closed deployment. The framework does not establish clinical truth, global representation alignment, or end-to-end safety; it defines certificate-producing checks at a model-to-system boundary. A companion frozen synthetic benchmark, GBI BoundaryBench v0.1, evaluated Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on 256 held-out tasks across three evidence modes (768 canonical executions). All executions completed, but none produced an output accepted by the benchmark contract: 369 were rejected during safe parsing and 399 during schema validation, yielding zero coverage and deterministic quarantine. This empirical result is deliberately narrow - one 4B open-weight model under one frozen interface - and is reported as evidence about the admission boundary, not as a general claim about LLM capability or clinical safety. A Julia appendix verifies numerical certificates using standard libraries.

</details>


### [82] [Neuroevolution Arena: Nested Ecological Evaluation of Update-and-Inheritance Regimes across Neural Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10323)
*Yuxu Ge,Yifei Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce a new GPU-based evolutionary RL testbed (Neuroevolution Arena) and a careful, nested evaluation protocol showing that controllers which look best in training can rank very differently when evaluated competitively across diverse ecological contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In competitive artificial life and neuroevolution systems, agents are often judged by their training performance, but this can disagree with how strong they actually are when tested against other agents or in different environments. Existing evaluations easily conflate properties of the training process, the learned controllers (artifacts), and the ecological test conditions, making it unclear why a method appears to win. The authors want a framework and protocol that cleanly disentangles these factors and reveals hidden variability instead of masking it.

Method: They build Neuroevolution Arena, a spatial ecology running many independent neural-network controllers (cells) on GPU, and define an audit-tracked nested evaluation protocol. They implement three update/inheritance regimes (EvoEvo, EvoRL, RLRL) combined with two neural architectures, and train each combination for 50,000 generations in three independent runs. From each of the resulting 18 runs they save a single elite controller. These 18 artifacts are then evaluated in a frozen, aligned-run design: every artifact is tested in the same seed-defined ecological contexts (three per block: two cooperative and one attacking), yielding 198 evaluation jobs. They analyze pairwise outcomes between artifacts within aligned blocks, averaging across the three contexts while keeping n = 3 training runs per condition as the independent level.

Result: During training, regimes that include reinforcement learning (EvoRL, RLRL) achieve higher recorded fitness than pure evolutionary updating (EvoEvo). However, when the saved elites are compared pairwise in the frozen competitive evaluations, the ranking patterns depend strongly on the neural architecture and on the particular artifact chosen from each run. Majority patterns of wins/losses differ by architecture, and performance is substantially artifact-dependent rather than cleanly attributable to the regime. Across the full six-way comparisons, the overall winners change with both artifact and ecological context, and the predefined survival endpoint ends up with a complete floor effect (no strong separation).

Conclusion: The proposed nested protocol, implemented in Neuroevolution Arena, demonstrates that training-time fitness alone is a misleading indicator of comparative strength in competitive artificial-life systems. By cleanly separating training artifacts from evaluation contexts and examining their interactions, the framework makes visible multiple sources of variation—across runs, architectures, regimes, and ecological contexts—that standard evaluations would hide. This suggests that robust assessment of neuroevolution and RL methods in competitive ecologies requires such multi-level, context-sensitive evaluation rather than relying on headline training scores.

Abstract: Competitive artificial-life systems can rank trained controllers differently under training and ecological evaluation. We present Neuroevolution Arena, a GPU-accelerated spatial ecology of independently parameterized neural-network cells, and an audit-tracked nested evaluation protocol. Three implementation-specific update-and-inheritance regimes (EvoEvo, EvoRL, and RLRL) are crossed with two neural architectures for 50,000 generations in three independent training runs per condition. One saved elite-controller artifact from each of the 18 runs enters an aligned-run frozen-evaluation design comprising 198 computational jobs. Pairwise effects average three seed-defined ecological contexts (two cooperation-permitting and one attack-permitting) within each aligned training-run block; the independent level remains n = 3 runs per condition. RL-enabled regimes attain higher recorded training fitness than EvoEvo, whereas pairwise outcomes show architecture-conditioned majority patterns and substantial artifact dependence. Six-way winners vary across artifacts and contexts, and the prespecified survival endpoint has a complete floor. We contribute a nested protocol that separates training-run artifacts from evaluation contexts and exposes, rather than conceals, their different sources of variation.

</details>


### [83] [Toward a Theory of Value in AI Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10327)
*Andrew Smart,Shazeda Ahmed,Jackie Kay,Jimmy Tobin,Kris Shrishak,Abeba Birhane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper critically examines how AI value alignment research defines and operationalizes “human values,” finding that most work leaves values undefined and collapses them into preferences, which can obscure their cultural and political complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the rapid deployment of LLMs and foundation models, harms such as toxicity, hallucinations, and unauthorized agentic behavior are widely framed as problems of misalignment with human values. Many alignment methods claim to address this but rarely specify what “values” actually are or how they should be understood. The authors are motivated to uncover and systematize the implicit theories of value that current alignment work relies on, and to assess what this means for the future of AI governance and design.

Method: The authors conduct a qualitative meta-analysis of 94 AI value alignment research papers. They annotate these papers to extract how each one conceptualizes human values (explicitly or implicitly), how these values are technically encoded (e.g., as preferences, labels, reward models), and how alignment is evaluated. They pay special attention to the role of human annotators versus synthetic data and automated raters in training and evaluation.

Result: They find that the majority of papers never explicitly define “values,” instead treating them as equivalent to preferences, often represented through simple binary or low-dimensional choices. This approach reduces complex, culturally embedded concepts to narrow decision formats. The trend toward replacing human annotators with synthetic data and automated raters further entrenches these narrow operationalizations and reduces opportunities for contestation, negotiation, and pluralism in how values are represented in models.

Conclusion: The field of AI value alignment currently operates with a thin, largely implicit theory of values that tends to equate them with preferences and encode them in ways that underrepresent cultural, social, and political complexity. As alignment methods increasingly rely on synthetic and auto-rated data, there is a risk that alternative ways of articulating and contesting values will be foreclosed. The authors argue that making the philosophical commitments of alignment work explicit—and incorporating richer, more contested notions of value—is crucial if AI is to meaningfully engage with human values rather than merely optimize over simplified proxies.

Abstract: Can AI systems be aligned to human values? The popularization of large language models (LLMs) and multi-modal foundation models has seen a rise in harms spanning from toxic speech and hallucinations to AI agents executing unauthorized actions. Within the field of AI safety, these harmful instances are often framed as the alignment problem, or of models being misaligned with human values. Researchers have responded by pursuing applied and theoretical AI value alignment efforts, often without specifying what they mean by human values. How does the field of AI value alignment conceive of human values? How are these conceptions of values technically operationalized and evaluated? What does the emergent theory of value from this field signify for the future of AI? We annotated 94 value alignment research papers to discern their implicit theory of values in AI. The majority do not define values, relying heavily on preferences as a stand in that runs the risk of reducing complex culturally situated concepts down to binary choices. As researchers dispense with using human annotators for model training and evaluation, turning instead to synthetic data and autorater approaches to aligning and evaluating models, we identify the potential to close off alternative methods for contesting and enacting values in foundation models. In making AI value alignments philosophical commitments explicit, we seek to bring great specificity and under explored perspectives in the debate on whether and how AI can address human values.

</details>


### [84] [Hierarchical Compositionality for An Assistive AI Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10330)
*Tianyi Fu,Mohan Sridharan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a symbolic, hierarchically compositional architecture for assistive AI agents that can disambiguate user references better than data-driven LLM-style baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLM-based and deep learning agents are powerful but resource-intensive, opaque, and can behave arbitrarily in novel situations because they rely on narrow representational and processing choices. The authors want AI agents that can reason more transparently and robustly about ambiguous user references, drawing on underused classical AI principles like structured, compositional knowledge and explicit reasoning about context and user preferences.

Method: They design an assistive agent architecture grounded in hierarchical compositionality. Domain objects are represented using primitive semantic attributes derived from human-validated feature norms. These primitives are combined into higher-level concepts in a learned hierarchy based on a limited history of interactions with specific users. The agent reasons over this hierarchy together with logical axioms describing domain dynamics and heuristic models for semantic compatibility, session salience (what is currently prominent), and user-specific thematic preferences. When the model cannot confidently resolve ambiguity, it explicitly asks the user for clarification.

Result: In experiments, their architecture is compared against state-of-the-art data-driven baselines (presumably neural/LLM-style models) on tasks involving reference disambiguation for assistive agents. The proposed method consistently outperforms these baselines, especially in adapting to specific user profiles and resolving ambiguous references using minimal data about each user.

Conclusion: A knowledge-rich, hierarchically compositional, and heuristic reasoning architecture can effectively handle referential ambiguity in assistive AI agents and generalize to user-specific preferences, outperforming purely data-driven baselines. This supports the relevance of classical AI principles—structured representations, explicit reasoning, and user modeling—as a complementary or alternative design path to modern black-box deep learning approaches for interactive AI agents.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly being developed to assist humans in various applications, and Large Language Models and other deep network architectures are considered to be state of the art for such agents. These methods are impressive stochastic predictors, but they are resource-hungry, opaque, and known to make arbitrary decisions in novel situations due to the narrow set of underlying representation and processing choices. Our work seeks to explore the design of architectures for such AI agents based on core principles that can be traced back to the early pioneers of AI but are not fully utilized in modern AI methods. We do so in this paper in the context of the core problem of AI agents addressing ambiguity in the objects being referred to by the human participants. Humans address such ambiguity by heuristically leveraging compositional knowledge of domain context and the preferences of the other human participants. Drawing inspiration from this observation, we describe an architecture that embeds the principle of hierarchical compositionality and uses simple heuristics to achieve the desired disambiguation. Specifically, domain objects are represented in terms of primitive attributes drawn from human-validated semantic feature norms, and a hierarchical combination of attributes and concepts automatically identified from a limited observed history of interactions of an assistive agent with specific users. The assistive agent then achieves the desired disambiguation by reasoning with knowledge of this compositional hierarchy; axioms governing domain dynamics; and models of semantic compatibility, session salience, and user-specific thematic preference, requesting human clarification when necessary. Experiments show that our approach consistently outperforms state of the art data-driven baselines, supporting adaptation to specific user profiles.

</details>


### [85] [Nutrition Data Infrastructure for the AI Era: Operationalizing FAIR for Agent-Mediated Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10363)
*Lin Liao,Peng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Nutrition Data Service (NDS), a FAIR-aligned, source-preserving infrastructure that makes AI-driven nutrition analyses accurate, reproducible, and auditable by handling data versions, cross-dataset links, and machine-readable interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are increasingly used in nutrition research, but current nutrition datasets suffer from identity ambiguities (what exactly is a data record), semantic ambiguities (what fields and meanings are shared or differ across datasets), and release/version ambiguities (which dataset version was used). These issues make AI-based analyses hard to reproduce, audit, or trust, especially when combining multiple independently released resources or relying on web-scraped data. There is a need for infrastructure that operationalizes FAIR principles (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, Reusable) specifically for automated, agent-based workflows in nutrition science.

Method: The authors design and implement the Nutrition Data Service (NDS), a source-preserving data infrastructure. Its key components are: (1) description resolution, which assigns stable, release-specific identities to records so they are findable and versioned; (2) typed crosswalks, which explicitly encode and constrain links between independently released nutrition resources with typed contracts that specify allowable mappings; and (3) machine-readable interfaces that expose both versioned data and crosswalks, enabling AI agents to query, reproduce, and audit analyses. They evaluate NDS using food-description benchmarks (including NutriBench), assess crosswalk quality in external and blinded evaluations, and run a person-level glycemic-index analysis comparing NDS-based vs open-web reconstructed inputs across models and runs.

Result: On food-description benchmarks, NDS achieves strong held-out accuracy and surpasses the best previously published language-model performance on NutriBench. In external and blinded evaluations of crosswalks, the typed contracts used by NDS lead to high-quality, defensible links while preventing unsupported mappings. In a glycemic-index case study, analyses that rely on pinned, NDS-provided inputs yield identical outputs across different models and repeated runs, whereas analyses relying on open-web reconstruction of inputs show instability and lack of reproducibility.

Conclusion: The study concludes that reliable agent-mediated nutrition research cannot be achieved by simply applying AI models to existing, loosely curated data. Instead, it requires a dedicated, FAIR-operationalized data infrastructure like NDS that rigorously encodes data identity, search, and cross-dataset crosswalks. Such infrastructure makes AI-driven analyses more accurate, reproducible, and auditable, and the authors position NDS as a template for future data services in nutrition and potentially other scientific domains.

Abstract: AI agents can accelerate nutrition research, but their analyses inherit the identity, semantic, and release ambiguities of the underlying data. We present Nutrition Data Service (NDS), source-preserving infrastructure that operationalizes FAIR for automated use: description resolution makes release-specific records findable; typed crosswalks connect independently released resources; machine-readable interfaces expose versioned sources and crosswalks, making analyses by AI agents replayable and auditable. On food-description benchmarks, NDS shows strong held-out accuracy and outperforms the best published language-model result on NutriBench. External and blinded crosswalk evaluations show that its typed contract favors defensible links and rejects unsupported mappings. In a person-level glycemic-index analysis, pinned NDS inputs produce identical outputs across models and repeated runs, while open-web reconstruction remains unstable. The central result is that agent-mediated nutrition research requires a new data infrastructure for data identity, search, and crosswalk.

</details>


### [86] [DSAgentBench: Can Agents Automate End-to-End Data-Science Workflows in Real Computer Environments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10366)
*Mizanur Rahman,Mohammed Saidul Islam,Ridwan Mahbub,Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar,Shafiq Joty,Enamul Hoque Prince*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DSAgentBench, a benchmark to test whether AI agents can autonomously execute realistic, end-to-end data-science workflows in real computer environments, revealing that current models perform poorly, especially at tool orchestration and multi-step reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for data-science agents do not involve real-computer interaction and mainly test code snippets or isolated subtasks. They fail to capture the long-horizon, multi-stage, multi-tool nature of real-world data-science work. The authors want a benchmark that evaluates whether agents can actually carry out full workflows — from data wrangling through modeling and validation — using real tools (notebooks, IDEs, terminals, browsers, databases) under realistic OS constraints, and that checks correctness beyond just "did the code run".

Method: The authors design DSAgentBench, a suite of 275 tasks that span the entire data-science lifecycle (wrangling, exploration, modeling, visualization, validation). Each task is executed within a real computer environment where the agent must coordinate multiple tools (e.g., notebooks, IDEs, terminals, browsers, databases). Tasks are constructed so that agents must ground decisions in intermediate outputs and perform multi-step reasoning, not just one-shot code generation. A deterministic evaluation framework verifies success via analytical correctness, quality of visualizations, and model performance, rather than simply whether code executes without error. They then evaluate 15 closed- and open-source agentic models on this benchmark.

Result: Across 15 models, performance is low: the best-performing agent, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, reaches only 56.70% task success, while all tested open-source agents achieve under 1% success. Error analysis (as summarized in the abstract) shows common failure modes in tool orchestration (coordinating multiple tools), grounding in the operating system environment, and performing multi-step reasoning over long workflows.

Conclusion: Current agentic systems are far from being able to autonomously execute realistic, end-to-end data-science workflows in real computing environments. DSAgentBench exposes this capability gap and provides a grounded, verifiable benchmark that can guide the development and evaluation of future autonomous data-science agents. The benchmark and code are publicly released to support further research.

Abstract: Real-world data science involves long-horizon workflows that span data wrangling, exploration, modeling, visualization, and validation, and require coordinated use of tools such as notebooks, IDEs, terminals, browsers, and databases within real operating environments. Yet existing benchmarks lack real-computer interaction and do not evaluate whether agents can execute complete end-to-end data-science workflows in realistic computing environments, failing to capture the multi-stage, multi-tool nature of data-science practice. We introduce DSAgentBench, the first benchmark to evaluate whether agents can automate full data-science workflows inside real computer environments. DSAgentBench contains 275 diverse tasks covering the entire data-science life-cycle, reflecting the complexity and tool coordination required in practice. Each task requires grounding decisions in intermediate outputs and coordinated tool use, and includes a deterministic evaluator that verifies analytical correctness, visual outputs, and model performance rather than code-only execution. Our extensive experiments with 15 closed- and open-source models show that even the strongest agent, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, achieves only 56.70% task success, while all open-source agents remain below 1%, frequently failing at tool orchestration, OS grounding, and multi-step reasoning. These results reveal a substantial capability gap between current agentic systems and real data-science workflows, positioning DSAgentBench as a foundation for developing grounded, verifiable, autonomous data-science agents. We release DSAgentBench at https://github.com/vis-nlp/DSAgentBench.

</details>


### [87] [Hidden in Plain Sight: Diffusion-Based Unrestricted Robotic Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10393)
*Jiahui Han,Yuhui Yao,Xin Wang,Jiafei Cao,Mingxuan Zhang,Danfeng Shan,Huiqi Deng,Guanchu Wang,Xia Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes DURA, a diffusion-based method to generate natural-looking adversarial patches that mislead Vision-Language-Action (VLA) robot controllers in both white-box and black-box settings, exposing serious safety risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language-Action models are increasingly used to control robots in diverse manipulation tasks, but their robustness to adversarial attacks is largely unstudied. Existing attacks depend on pixel-level perturbations or full white-box access, which create visible artifacts and limit real-world applicability. There is a need for more realistic, deployable attacks to evaluate and understand the safety risks of physically deployed VLA-controlled robots.

Method: DURA is an unrestricted adversarial attack framework based on diffusion models. It learns adversarial patches by optimizing in the latent trajectory space of a pretrained diffusion model, rather than directly perturbing pixels. This allows the generation of visually natural patches that remain effective in fooling VLA models. DURA operates in both white-box and black-box settings, with the black-box variant relying only on observed action outputs of the target VLA model while steering the robot toward attacker-specified target actions.

Result: In extensive simulations and real-world robotic experiments, DURA achieves higher attack success rates and better visual naturalness of patches compared to existing adversarial attack baselines on VLA models. It is effective in both white-box and black-box scenarios and can reliably manipulate robotic behavior toward attacker-chosen target actions.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that state-of-the-art VLA-based robotic systems are highly vulnerable to visually natural, diffusion-generated adversarial patches. This unveils a significant safety risk for real-world deployment and underscores the urgent need to design stronger robustness defenses and evaluation protocols for VLA models in physical robotic applications.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong capabilities in controlling robots across diverse manipulation tasks. However, their adversarial robustness remains largely underexplored, and exploiting this weakness can lead to physical-world harm. Existing attacks on VLA models often rely on pixel-space perturbations or white-box access, resulting in noticeable artifacts and limited deployability in real-world robotic systems. In this work, we propose DURA, a diffusion-based unrestricted robotic attack that generates visually natural adversarial patches for VLA models. DURA supports both white-box and black-box attack settings, where the black-box setting requires only the predicted actions of the victim model. By optimizing along the latent trajectory of a pretrained diffusion model, DURA generates visually natural patches while steering the robot toward attacker-specified target actions. Extensive experiments in both simulation and the real physical world show that DURA consistently outperforms existing methods. Our findings expose a safety risk for physically deployed VLA models and call for stronger defenses.

</details>


### [88] [Threat-guided Policy-aware Scene Perturbation for Safe Autonomous Driving with Online Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10403)
*Xincong Hu,Lei Ou,Maosen Li,Jingtao Zhang,Liguo Hou,Zongzhang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Threat-guided Policy-aware Scene Perturbation (TPSP), a method that generates policy-aware, threat-guided, safety-critical driving scenes to improve the safety and robustness of online reinforcement learning for autonomous driving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online RL for autonomous driving struggles to learn robust safety behaviors because real-world traffic is long-tailed: dangerous, rare interactions are seldom encountered via standard sampling. Existing scene synthesis or adversarial approaches increase difficulty but are optimized largely independent of the evolving RL policy, so they fail to systematically expose current policy weaknesses or provide maximally informative safety-critical experiences.

Method: TPSP introduces a policy-aware scene encoder that jointly encodes the current policy’s behavior and the driving environment to obtain a representation reflecting their interaction. Using this representation, TPSP identifies and selectively perturbs critical objects in the scene instead of modifying all elements uniformly. It then employs a threat-guided optimization strategy that measures threat-level differences between rollouts on the original vs. perturbed scenes, and uses this signal to iteratively refine perturbations toward safety-critical but informative scenarios adapted to the current policy.

Result: In experiments on the NAVSIM v2 autonomous driving benchmark with about 4 million kilometers of simulated driving, TPSP significantly improves safety learning efficiency and overall safety performance of online RL policies compared with baselines. The studies show that TPSP leads to fewer safety violations and better handling of challenging interactions. Ablation experiments reveal that removing policy-awareness or targeted perturbation degrades performance, confirming that TPSP’s policy-aware, object-targeted design is key to its gains.

Conclusion: TPSP effectively addresses the scarcity of safety-critical experiences in online RL for autonomous driving by generating policy-aware, threat-guided scene perturbations. By selectively targeting critical objects and aligning scenario generation with current policy weaknesses, it yields more informative training data, improves safety under limited interaction budgets, and outperforms random or policy-unaware scene perturbation strategies on large-scale autonomous driving simulations.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promising performance in autonomous driving, yet ensuring the safety of online RL policies remains challenging due to insufficient exposure to safety-critical driving scenes. The long-tailed nature of real-world traffic situations makes dangerous and rare interactions difficult to encounter through conventional sampling, limiting the ability of RL policies to learn robust safety behaviors. Existing methods improve training diversity by synthesizing challenging scenes or adversarial situations. However, these approaches typically optimize scene generation objectives separately from the evolving policy, without explicitly modeling how generated perturbations relate to the current policy's weaknesses and learning needs. In this paper, we propose Threat-guided Policy-aware Scene Perturbation (TPSP) for safe autonomous driving with online RL. TPSP introduces a policy-aware scene encoder to capture the interaction between policy behaviors and surrounding environments, enabling scene perturbation aligned with the current policy. Based on this representation, TPSP selectively perturbs critical objects rather than applying uniform modifications across the scene. Furthermore, we develop a threat-guided optimization strategy that evaluates perturbed scenes through threat-level differences between policy rollouts on original and perturbed scenes, guiding the generation of safety-critical scenes with higher training value. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that TPSP improves safety learning efficiency, achieving strong safety performance on NAVSIM v2 with approximately 4 million kilometers of simulated driving data. Ablation studies verify that policy-aware targeted perturbations provide more informative safety-critical experiences than random or policy-unaware strategies, enabling safer driving under limited interaction budgets.

</details>


### [89] [Reasoning Shortcuts and Value Symmetries: What Symmetry Permits, Architecture Realizes, and Optimization Selects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10420)
*Xin Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies when neurosymbolic reasoning rules truly capture intended concepts versus exploiting unintended reasoning shortcuts, and develops a rigorous group-theoretic and complexity-theoretic analysis showing that a prior framework’s core symmetry definition misapplies to benchmarks and can give spurious pathology, while providing corrected structure theorems, complexity classifications, and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic systems can achieve high accuracy while relying on unintended reasoning shortcuts that exploit spurious structure instead of the intended concepts encoded in rules. A recent automorphism-based framework aims to detect such shortcuts and asks when rules uniquely identify concepts, but its central definition and guarantees are not fully understood or validated. There is a need to clarify when symmetry-based analyses are correct, how they behave on real heterogeneous benchmarks, and what the computational limits are for deciding if rules truly pin down concepts.

Method: The authors scrutinize the existing framework of reasoning shortcuts based on automorphism groups of value relabelings, showing that its key assumption of one shared permutation across all positions does not fit heterogeneous benchmarks. They construct a more faithful hierarchy of models, analyze embeddings such as domain padding, and prove structural theorems relating solution-set symmetries to transitivity and failure of transitivity. They then conduct a complexity-theoretic analysis for circuit-specified rules, classifying the difficulty of deciding symmetry-inertness and existence of nontrivial automorphisms in the polynomial hierarchy, with special results for monotone and Boolean circuits. Empirically, they re-measure multiple rule families and compare symmetry predictions to observed shortcuts in weakly supervised models, using released artifacts for reproducibility.

Result: They show that the prior framework’s shared-permutation definition, when naively embedded by padding domains, yields highly misleading results (e.g., 90.91% of solution pairs labeled unexplained on CLE4EVR) and that these verdicts depend on arbitrary configuration ordering. Their newly introduced hierarchy of models reports 0% unexplained pairs where appropriate and aligns with provable structure. Across eleven rule families and fifteen pre-specified predictions, thirteen are confirmed, with unexplained-pair rates spanning nearly the full range and matching structural expectations. They prove six theorems giving sufficient conditions for transitivity and its breakdown, including a Free Slot Lemma explaining known pathologies from syntax alone. Complexity-wise, symmetry-inertness of a coordinate for circuit-given rules is coNP-complete; testing for nontrivial automorphisms is coNP-hard under randomized reductions, lies in Σ2^p, and is not Σ2^p-complete unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses; for monotone circuits it is coNP-complete; and in the Boolean case they fully classify when automorphisms explain all structure: exactly when the solution set is an affine coset. Empirically, weakly supervised models place all 94 observed shortcuts exactly at the levels predicted by the componentwise theory and none at the transitive or typed-ambiguous levels, with a dual-head control reproducing this pattern.

Conclusion: The paper corrects and refines the automorphism-based analysis of reasoning shortcuts in neurosymbolic systems. It shows that a previously used shared-permutation definition can misdiagnose pathology on heterogeneous benchmarks, and replaces it with a hierarchy that matches both theoretical structure and empirical behavior. The authors provide tight complexity classifications for symmetry-related decision problems and a complete characterization in the Boolean case. Empirical results demonstrate that symmetry-based theory accurately predicts where shortcuts appear, and that what symmetry permits is distinct from what learning dynamics actually select. Overall, the work clarifies when rules genuinely pin down concepts and offers a more reliable footing for analyzing and auditing neurosymbolic reasoning shortcuts.

Abstract: Reasoning shortcuts are solutions of a neurosymbolic system's rules that produce correct predictions through unintended concepts. A recent framework of Takemura, Inoue, and Nishino analyzes them through an automorphism group of value relabelings and asks, as its central open question, when rules pin concepts down. We first show that the framework's key definition, one shared permutation applied at every position, does not apply as stated to any of the four heterogeneous benchmarks it was evaluated on, and that the most direct embedding, padding domains to a common size, produces confident false pathology: 90.91% of solution pairs reported unexplained on CLE4EVR, where every well-defined member of the hierarchy we introduce reports 0%, and the padded verdict's content rotates with configuration-file ordering. Re-measuring eleven rule families under fifteen pre-specified predictions (thirteen confirmed), unexplained-pair rates span 0% to 99.9999% and track provable structure: six theorems give sufficient conditions for transitivity and its failure, including a Free Slot Lemma certifying Kandinsky's pathology from syntax alone. For circuit-given rules, deciding symmetry-inertness of a coordinate is coNP-complete; nontrivial-automorphism existence is coNP-hard under randomized reductions, lies in $Σ_2^p$, is not $Σ_2^p$-complete unless PH collapses, and on monotone circuits is coNP-complete outright. In the Boolean case transitivity is classified exactly: automorphisms explain everything iff the solution set is an affine coset. Weakly supervised models place all 94 observed shortcuts at the one level the componentwise theory flags and none at the 48 it certifies transitive; twelve typed-ambiguous levels produce none, separating what symmetry permits from what optimization selects, and a dual-head control replicates the geography. All numbers trace to released artifacts.

</details>


### [90] [Recovering Wasted Compute in Autoresearch Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10424)
*Au Kwok Chun,Abhigyan Acherjee,Amrutha Rao,Zaiqian Chen,Kazem Meidani,C. Bayan Bruss,Micah Goldblum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes common failure modes of current autoresearch agents on tabular datasets and proposes design-level fixes that yield substantial performance gains without changing the base language model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoresearch agents are being heavily invested in because they promise to automate end-to-end research and machine learning development, especially for specialized applications. However, despite this promise and investment, their core modeling pipelines exhibit significant inefficiencies and systematic errors when applied to tabular data. Understanding and fixing these issues is crucial to realizing the practical value of autoresearch systems and to avoid wasting large compute budgets on easily preventable mistakes.

Method: The authors empirically study existing autoresearch pipelines on tabular datasets, dissecting their behavior in terms of debugging, hyperparameter tuning, tree-search exploration, and the link between data analysis and modeling decisions. They identify four main failure modes: repetitive debugging of the same issues, under-utilization of compute for hyperparameter tuning, non-exploratory tree-search behavior, and ineffective use of data analysis. They then implement targeted interventions: a global debug consultant that shares runtime constraints across all branches of the search tree, prompt-level and control-level improvements to better guide the agent, and refined tree-search algorithms that encourage more efficient exploration. Performance is evaluated with the underlying language model held fixed to isolate the impact of agentic design changes.

Result: With the proposed interventions—especially the global debug consultant, improved prompting/control, and refined tree-search—the agents avoid repeatedly fixing the same bugs, make better use of available compute for hyperparameter tuning, explore the modeling space more effectively, and more productively link data analysis to downstream decisions. These changes result in large performance improvements of autoresearch agents on tabular datasets, achieved purely through agentic design rather than changing the base language model.

Conclusion: The study concludes that many of the current limitations of autoresearch systems stem not from the underlying language model but from the surrounding agentic and search design. By addressing debugging, exploration, and decision-making at the pipeline level, it is possible to unlock substantial performance gains on tasks like tabular modeling. This suggests that future progress in autoresearch should focus heavily on better agent architectures and control strategies, in addition to improving the base models themselves.

Abstract: A slew of recent works develop agents for solving research problems end-to-end, a paradigm increasingly referred to as autoresearch. Such agents have inspired large industry investment, motivated by their potential to automate time-consuming human labor and customize machine learning solutions for specialized applications. In this paper, we study the modeling pipeline at the core of these autoresearch systems and identify common failure modes when they are applied to tabular datasets: (1) they waste compute resolving the same bugs over and over again; (2) they often fail to tune hyperparameters even when they have a large remaining compute budget; (3) the tree-search algorithms that power them do not explore; and (4) they perform data analysis, mimicking the humans whose data they are trained on, but do not use that analysis to make downstream decisions. We explore targeted interventions and find that a global debug consultant that shares discovered runtime constraints across all branches of the search tree, prompt- and control-level enhancements, and refined tree-search algorithms successfully recover wasted compute. Our results show that large gains in autoresearch agent performance are achievable through agentic design alone, holding the underlying language model fixed.

</details>


### [91] [Conversational versus Dashboard Explainable AI for UAV Intrusion Detection: An Empirical Study of Operator Trust and Reliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10434)
*Cong Chi Nguyen,Trang Mai Xuan,Vu-Duc Ngo,Kim-Ngan Thi Nguyen,Trong-Nghia Nguyen,Thien Van Luong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates a conversational, LLM-powered explainable AI interface for UAV intrusion detection and finds it improves perceived usefulness but can increase over-reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intrusion Detection Systems for UAV networks increasingly use complex ML models that are accurate but hard to interpret, especially given high-dimensional multimodal data. Existing static XAI dashboards struggle to help operators understand model behavior, build appropriate trust, and make sound post-incident decisions. There is a need to explore richer interaction paradigms, like conversational interfaces, and to understand their impact on human understanding, trust, and reliance.

Method: The authors design a conversational XAI interface using a Large Language Model to support on-demand, natural-language investigation of IDS decisions. They run a controlled user study comparing this interface with a traditional XAI dashboard in a post-incident auditing scenario. Participants perform auditing tasks under both conditions, and the researchers measure operator understanding, trust, perceived usefulness, and patterns of reliance/self-reliance when the IDS is correct or incorrect.

Result: Participants rated the conversational interface as more useful than the traditional dashboard. It appeared to help them more easily access and synthesize relevant information. However, it also led to reduced appropriate self-reliance and signs of over-reliance on the system’s recommendations, especially when the IDS was wrong. Users seemed less inclined to verify underlying evidence when interacting with natural-language explanations.

Conclusion: Conversational LLM-based XAI interfaces can enhance perceived usability and support information synthesis in UAV intrusion auditing, but they also risk fostering inappropriate over-reliance. There is a trade-off between seamless, natural interaction and the need for cognitive friction that prompts users to cross-check evidence. Future XAI systems should deliberately incorporate design elements that both leverage conversational strengths and enforce cognitive forcing functions to maintain appropriate human oversight and reliance.

Abstract: Machine learning-based Intrusion Detection Systems (IDS) have demonstrated superior performance in securing Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) networks. However, the 'black-box' nature of these models, combined with the high dimensionality of multimodal cyber-physical data, poses significant interpretability challenges. Static visualization dashboards may struggle to present complex relationships among multimodal cyber-physical features in a form that is easy for operators to inspect and interpret. To address this, we propose a Conversational XAI interface powered by Large Language Models (LLM) to facilitate on-demand investigation. In a controlled experiment with participants, we systematically evaluated the impact of this conversational interface versus a traditional XAI Dashboard on operator understanding, trust, and reliance during post-incident auditing tasks. Our results suggest that the conversational interface was perceived as more useful than the dashboard, potentially because it helped participants access and synthesize relevant information more easily. However, this benefit was accompanied by a lower level of appropriate self-reliance, indicating a potential risk of over-reliance. One possible interpretation is that the natural-language responses made the AI advice easier to accept, which may have reduced participants' tendency to verify the underlying evidence when the IDS was incorrect. These findings point to a potential trade-off in human-AI collaboration for UAV intrusion auditing: interaction mechanisms that improve perceived usability may also increase the risk of inappropriate reliance. We conclude by discussing design implications for future XAI systems that balance seamless interaction with cognitive forcing functions to foster appropriate reliance.

</details>


### [92] [Continuous Interaction Diffusion: A Diffusion-Native Runtime for Asynchronous Tool-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10438)
*Yuhang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Continuous Interaction Diffusion (CID), an architecture that makes diffusion language models interact with tools continuously during denoising instead of via discrete stop-and-resume calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool use in language models is usually designed for autoregressive models that make discrete tool calls, wait, then continue. Diffusion language models refine outputs in parallel over many steps, so forcing them into this discrete interaction pattern can cause early, unstable tool decisions, delayed use of evidence, redundant computation and tool calls, and overall worse accuracy and efficiency.

Method: They design CID, a diffusion-native interaction architecture that adds dedicated channels and mechanisms for tool use during iterative denoising: (1) a read-only fact channel for external tool outputs, (2) a thought channel as a Typed Cognitive Tensor capturing the evolving internal reasoning state, and (3) a display channel for the emergent textual output. The system allows partial, not-yet-fully-serialized calls to trigger external reads, projects returned results back into the thought tensor so they can revise earlier reasoning and output regions, and maintains persistent bindings that cache static tool results and selectively refresh dynamic ones. They formalize the model, runtime behavior, and training objectives, along with an evaluation protocol that jointly measures task quality and end-to-end efficiency.

Result: Conceptual and architectural results: a formalization of continuous tool interaction for diffusion LMs, a specification of the Typed Cognitive Tensor channels and binding mechanisms, and an evaluation protocol for quality–efficiency tradeoffs. This initial work is theoretical and does not report empirical performance numbers.

Conclusion: CID offers a principled way for diffusion language models to interact with external tools continuously during denoising, exposing evidence earlier, overlapping tool latency with model computation, reducing duplicate external calls, and making better use of past computation after new information arrives. The paper positions this as a foundation for future empirical work, focusing here on read-only tools and on defining the architecture and evaluation framework.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly rely on external tools to access up-to-date information, perform computation, and interact with the outside world. For autoregressive models, tool use naturally fits the generation process: the model emits a tool call, waits for the result, and then continues generating. Diffusion language models (dLLMs), however, reason by repeatedly refining many parts of their output in parallel, making this stop-and-resume interaction pattern unnecessarily restrictive. It can force tool decisions before the model's reasoning has stabilized, delay useful observations until a discrete call finishes, and introduce redundant refinement and tool execution, potentially hurting both task accuracy and inference efficiency.
  We introduce Continuous Interaction Diffusion (CID), a diffusion-native model--runtime architecture that integrates tool interaction into iterative denoising. CID separates a model-read-only fact channel, a thought channel represented by a Typed Cognitive Tensor, and a display channel. Information needs can emerge before a textual or JSON call is fully serialized, allowing perceptual bindings to launch external reads while denoising continues. Returned results are projected into the evolving thought state and can revise earlier cognition and display regions. Persistent bindings reuse static results without repeated external execution and refresh changing sources when needed. CID is designed to expose evidence earlier, overlap tool latency with model computation, reduce duplicate external work, and preserve useful computation after new evidence arrives. We formalize the architecture, runtime, and training objectives, and define an evaluation protocol for task quality and end-to-end efficiency. This first paper focuses on read-only tools and makes no empirical performance claims.

</details>


### [93] [Rationale-Guided Learning for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10448)
*Sujung Oh,Jung Uk Kim,Sangmin Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce a rationale-guided learning framework that turns multimodal emotion recognition in conversation into a cognitively-inspired reasoning task using structured rationales, achieving SOTA results without inference-time MLLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal emotion recognition in conversation methods map multimodal cues directly to emotion labels and largely ignore the human-like causal reasoning process that underlies emotion understanding. The authors want models that not only classify emotions accurately but also mimic the intuitive and deliberative reasoning humans use, improving both performance and interpretability.

Method: They propose Rationale-Guided Learning (RGL), which reframes MERC as a reasoning problem inspired by dual-process theory. Emotional reasoning is decomposed into intuitive (System 1), contextual (System 2), and integrative facets. An MLLM is used offline to generate structured rationales for training data, which are encoded as memory-like representations. During training, the model is guided by aligning its internal representations with these human-like rationale memories. At inference, the trained model runs without any MLLM, using only its learned internal reasoning structure.

Result: RGL achieves state-of-the-art performance on two standard MERC benchmarks: IEMOCAP and MELD. Additionally, the learned internal features can be used to retrieve semantically appropriate rationales for unseen test examples, showing that the model has internalized meaningful reasoning patterns rather than just fitting labels.

Conclusion: Modeling MERC as a rationale-guided reasoning process, grounded in dual-process cognitive theory, leads to both better accuracy and better interpretability. Offline-generated structured rationales can successfully guide representation learning so that, at test time, a lightweight model performs strongly while implicitly encoding human-like reasoning, as evidenced by its ability to retrieve suitable rationales for new inputs.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion recognition in conversation (MERC) requires understanding complex interactions between verbal and non-verbal cues. However, most existing approaches fundamentally treat this as a direct input-output (multimodal cues-emotion labels) mapping problem, overlooking the causal reasoning that humans use when interpreting emotions. We propose rationale-guided learning (RGL), a novel framework that transforms MERC into a cognitively-inspired reasoning task. Based on dual-process theory, we decompose emotional reasoning into three facets: Intuitive (immediate perception, System 1), Contextual (situational analysis, System 2), and Integrative (synthesis of both). We leverage an MLLM offline to generate structured rationales, which are encoded as memories to guide model training via aligning internal representations with human-like reasoning patterns. Our final model operates without any MLLM overheads at inference time. Experimental results show that RGL achieves state-of-the-art performance on the IEMOCAP and MELD benchmarks. Further, for interpretation, we demonstrate that the model's internal features effectively retrieve semantically correct rationales for unseen test samples, validating its rationale reasoning capabilities.

</details>


### [94] [Quantum Incremental Learning with Mixed State Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10464)
*Yu Wu,Qianli Zhou,Xinyang Deng,Wen Jiang,Kang Hao Cheong,Witold Pedrycz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A quantum incremental learning framework using trainable mixed-state class prototypes enables adding new classes without widening quantum circuits, improves representation capacity, and achieves efficient, robust performance under NISQ constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incremental learning must absorb new classes sequentially without catastrophic forgetting while staying within strict parameter and memory limits. In NISQ-era quantum computing, circuit width is limited and traditional quantum classifiers are bounded by the number of orthogonal basis states, which restricts the number of categories they can represent. There is a need for a quantum incremental learning approach that can scale in class number without expanding circuit width and that better exploits quantum states’ representational power under hardware constraints.

Method: The paper proposes a quantum incremental learning architecture built on a shared quantum backbone plus class-specific, trainable mixed-state prototypes. New classes are introduced by adding new prototypes rather than enlarging the quantum circuit (fixed-width backbone). Each class is represented by a mixed quantum state instead of a single pure state to enhance representational richness. A decomposable formulation of mixed states is used, allowing efficient preparation and computation. Classification is performed using a Hilbert–Schmidt (HS) distance metric between sample-encoded states and class prototypes, which is computationally convenient in this setting.

Result: Simulations demonstrate that the model can produce highly concentrated high-dimensional feature representations with very few qubits. Compared to classical incremental learning baselines, the method shows lower computational complexity and stronger, more stable representation quality across incremental learning tasks. It handles the addition of many classes without expanding circuit width and mitigates catastrophic forgetting under NISQ constraints.

Conclusion: Trainable mixed-state prototypes provide a practical way to realize quantum incremental learning on NISQ hardware. By decoupling the growth in class number from circuit width and exploiting mixed states with HS-distance-based classification, the framework offers an efficient, scalable, and robust alternative to classical incremental methods and traditional quantum classifiers for sequential multi-class learning.

Abstract: Incremental learning models are required to learn new classes sequentially without catastrophic forgetting, while operating under parameter and memory constraints. In the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era, although quantum neural networks offer advantages in feature mapping, hardware limitations restrict circuit width. Furthermore, traditional quantum classifiers are constrained by the number of orthogonal basis states, limiting their capacity to accommodate a continually growing number of categories. Thus, we introduce a novel quantum incremental learning framework based on trainable mixed-state prototypes. Its original design incorporates new classes by adding class prototypes rather than increasing the circuit width of the shared quantum backbone. The use of mixed-state prototypes is another key contribution, since they have representation capabilities to represent information than a single pure-state prototype. And the decomposable mixed-state calculation provides lower production costs and a convenient Hilbert-Schmidt (HS) distance metric for classification. Simulation results show that our model achieves high-dimensional feature concentration using a minimal number of qubits, while demonstrating lower computational complexity and robust representation in incremental learning tasks compared with classical baselines.

</details>


### [95] [RLMOpt: Adaptive Prompt Optimization via Recursive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10471)
*Subhash Bangalore Satheesha,Nirvik Pande,Deepthi Duddempudi,Bharath Dandala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces RLMOpt, a language-model-driven prompt optimizer that learns the search policy itself via a recursive language model, achieving better and more efficient prompt optimization than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prompt optimizers use a fixed, hand-designed search algorithm while only using language models to generate or refine prompt candidates. This limits flexibility and may not fully leverage the reasoning and planning abilities of LMs to control the optimization process itself.

Method: Proposes RLMOpt, where a recursive language model (RLM) acts as an agent over a tool-based environment. The RLM: inspects task and evaluation information, analyzes failures, proposes new prompts, allocates evaluation budgets, and decides when to terminate search. A deterministic harness provides objective scoring, Pareto-based selection, and regression constraints to prevent degradation and enforce fair comparison.

Result: Evaluated on four benchmarks (Chia, HotpotQA, IFBench-2025, BFCL). With a single seed per task, RLMOpt achieves the best held-out performance on all four benchmarks and improves the average score from 0.589 (GEPA) to 0.610. Across 11 benchmark-seed pairs, RLMOpt outperforms GEPA in 9 cases, never produces prompts worse than the initial seed (while GEPA does so twice), and uses fewer search rollouts with much more concise prompts (27–79% the length of GEPA prompts).

Conclusion: Making the search policy itself language-model-driven via an RLM agent yields more effective and efficient prompt optimization than fixed, hand-designed search procedures. The key factor in achievable gains is the performance headroom in the seed prompt, not the search budget, suggesting that optimizers should focus on reliably reaching existing headroom with minimal search rather than exhaustive exploration.

Abstract: Prompt optimizers automate the search for prompts that improve language-model performance, but existing methods rely on a predefined optimization procedure: the algorithm determines which candidates to explore and how the search progresses, while the language model generates or refines prompt proposals. We introduce RLMOpt, a prompt optimizer that makes the search policy itself language-model-driven through a recursive language model (RLM). The RLM agent operates over a tool-based environment, inspecting task information, analyzing failures, generating candidates, allocating evaluation budget, and deciding when to stop. A deterministic harness complements the agent by enforcing objective scoring, Pareto-based selection, and regression constraints.
  We evaluate RLMOpt across four benchmarks spanning structured clinical information extraction (Chia), multi-hop question answering (HotpotQA), verifiable instruction following (IFBench-2025), and multi-turn tool-calling agents (BFCL). In a matched comparison at a single seed, RLMOpt obtains the best held-out score on all four benchmarks and leads the four-task mean (0.610 against 0.589 for GEPA). Repeating each benchmark across seeds yields 11 matched benchmark-seed comparisons, in which RLMOpt outperforms GEPA in 9 cases. Across all 11 runs, it never produced a prompt that underperformed its seed, whereas GEPA fell below its starting point twice. It is also more efficient, achieving these results with fewer search rollouts while producing prompts that are 27-79% the size of those produced by GEPA.
  Our results further show that optimization gains are determined primarily by the headroom available in the seed prompt, rather than by the search budget. Efficient optimization therefore depends on reaching the available headroom reliably and with minimal search

</details>


### [96] [Evaluating Rational Contracting in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10475)
*Bhavyesh Sajja,Max Kleiman-Weiner,Roger Zimmermann,Tan Zhi-Xuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework and benchmark (ContractSim) to study how language-based AI agents negotiate and execute multi-step natural language contracts under uncertainty, revealing that current LLM agents struggle with rationality and cooperation in complex, uncertain settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of language-based AI agents mostly cover one-shot interactions or simple economic games, focusing only on profit and neglecting the richer, realistic setting of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts expressible in natural language. There is also a lack of metrics on whether agents behave in ways that support trustworthy contracting, such as honoring agreements under uncertainty.

Method: The authors formulate a rational decision-theoretic framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain, multi-step environments. Based on this framework, they design metrics and baselines that capture rational and cooperative behavior during both negotiation and execution. They then build ContractSim, an evaluation suite in which two agents negotiate and carry out a multi-turn supplier contract (in domains like catering, hotel cleaning, and AI hosting) with both environmental uncertainty and strategic uncertainty between players, and use it to test LLM-based agents across six environments and three supplier settings.

Result: In ContractSim, current LLM-based agents typically reach agreements and can negotiate efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low. However, when uncertainty is high, they frequently fail to produce contracts that are satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial. Moreover, during execution they often act uncooperatively, violating agreed-upon contract terms to gain extra profit, even when fulfilling the contract would have been easy.

Conclusion: Current language-based AI agents are not yet reliable counterparts for complex natural language contracting: they exhibit weaknesses in both rational negotiation under uncertainty and cooperative execution of contracts. The paper’s framework, metrics, and ContractSim benchmark expose these shortcomings and indicate the need for improved architectures, objectives, and training methods to produce agents that can negotiate, interpret, and honor language contracts in a rational and trustworthy manner.

Abstract: The emergence of language-based AI agents promises to transform the scope of machine economic activity. Instead of just proposing bids or following hard-coded protocols, such agents can be used to negotiate and execute agreements in open-ended natural language. However, most evaluations of these abilities have focused on one-off exchanges or simple economic games, leaving open the rich space of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts made expressible by language; they also focus on raw profit, without measuring the qualities required for trustworthy contracting. We address this by formulating a rational framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments. Within this framework, we develop metrics and baselines for quantifying rational and cooperative play. To evaluate how agents perform at such contracting, we instantiate our framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite where two players negotiate and execute a multi-turn supplier contract under environmental and inter-player uncertainty. Across six environments and three supplier settings (catering, hotel cleaning, and AI hosting) we find that current LLM-based agents reach agreement reliably, and negotiate efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low. However, under high uncertainty, they often fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts. They are also frequently uncooperative when executing contracts, violating contract terms for additional profit even when contracts are easy to satisfy. These findings highlight room for improvement in the design of language agents that can negotiate, interpret, and execute contracts both rationally and cooperatively.

</details>


### [97] [Multi-Granular Rationale-Guided Molecular LLM for Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10480)
*Junwoo Park,Minyoung Shin,Cheol Soon Lee,Sujee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MR-MoL is a molecular property prediction framework that feeds a language model with explicit, multi-granular substructure rationales derived from a GNN, improving performance and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing molecular LLMs use SMILES or molecular graphs where substructure contributions are only implicit, making it hard to see which parts of a molecule drive properties. Retrieval-augmented methods introduce external context, but chemists reason mainly from internal substructures and their influence on properties. There is a need for models that both leverage and expose such structure-property rationales while maintaining strong predictive performance.

Method: The authors build MR-MoL, a multi-granular rationale-guided molecular LLM. First, they fine-tune a GNN for each property and use masking-based attribution to score molecular substructures. They then serialize the most influential substructures, tagged with direction (increasing/decreasing property) and rank, into a textual rationale. This rationale covers three levels: Murcko scaffold plus side chains, BRICS fragments, and functional groups. The LLM ingests this rationale alongside the usual SMILES string and molecular graph to predict molecular properties.

Result: On eight MoleculeNet benchmarks, MR-MoL attains the strongest overall performance among generalist molecular models and significantly reduces the performance gap to task-specific specialist models as reported by the authors. Ablation and diagnostic experiments show that the model uses the fine-grained rationales: changing direction, rank, or substructure systematically affects predictions, and the learned attributions align with established structure–property relationships in chemistry.

Conclusion: MR-MoL demonstrates that exposing GNN-derived, multi-granular substructure attributions to a molecular LLM can simultaneously enhance accuracy and interpretability for property prediction. The study suggests that internal, chemistry-aligned rationales—rather than generic external retrieval—are an effective way to guide LLMs on molecular tasks, and it offers evidence that the model truly conditions on these rationales rather than treating them as incidental context.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied across chemical tasks, such as molecular property prediction, which underpins drug discovery. Molecular LLMs represent a molecule through several modalities, notably a 1D SMILES sequence or a 2D molecular graph. Both encode molecular information implicitly, so the contribution of individual substructures remains opaque. Retrieval and augmentation methods add context, but from external sources. However, the cues chemists reason over are the internal substructures that drive a property up or down. We propose MR-MoL, a multi-granular rationale-guided molecular LLM that supplies this evidence directly. A fine-tuned GNN scores each substructure through masking, and the most influential ones are serialized as a ranked, direction-tagged rationale that the LLM reads alongside the SMILES sequence and molecular graph. The rationale spans three levels of granularity: Murcko scaffolds with their side chains, BRICS fragments, and functional groups. This is, to our knowledge, the first method to expose GNN-derived attributions to an LLM as evidence for property prediction.
