On eight MoleculeNet tasks, MR-MoL achieves the best overall results among generalist models and narrows the gap to specialist models tuned for each task. Five diagnostics further confirm that the model reads the rationale rather than merely benefiting from its presence. Its direction, rank, and substructure each shape the prediction, and its attributions reproduce known structure-property relationships.

</details>


### [98] [Predicting Space Groups of Double Perovskites by LLM with Dynamic Few-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10483)
*Jongwon Park,Inhyo Lee,Junhyeong Lee,Seunghwa Ryu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes DyRIS, an LLM-agent framework that predicts space groups of double perovskites from composition using diversity-aware few-shot retrieval and rule-guided inference, significantly improving performance on underrepresented (minor) space groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting crystallographic space groups for double perovskites directly from composition is challenging, especially because available datasets are heavily imbalanced: a few space-group classes dominate, while many minor classes have very few examples. Standard machine-learning models tend to bias toward these major classes, leading to poor performance on rare but scientifically important minor space groups. The authors aim to build a method that handles this imbalance and makes more accurate and fair predictions across all space-group classes using recent advances in large language models and retrieval-based reasoning, while leveraging crystallographic domain knowledge.

Method: The authors develop DyRIS, an LLM-agent-based framework that, given a DP composition, predicts and ranks likely space groups. The method has two main components: (1) diversity-enhanced dynamic few-shot retrieval, which selects in-context examples in a way that limits overrepresentation of major space groups and ensures better coverage of minor ones; and (2) a rule-guided inference module that refines and ranks the Top-3 space-group predictions using domain rules based on B/B' cation ordering, quantitative indicators, and explicit controls on major-SG bias. The framework is evaluated on 3,528 thermodynamically filtered double-perovskite entries, and compared against composition-based (e.g., CrabNet) and descriptor-based (e.g., PyCaret) baselines. Ablation studies remove each DyRIS component in turn to measure its contribution. Additional experiments test whether the rule-guided inference can be substituted by conventional classifiers or rankers.

Result: At a 50% training-data ratio, DyRIS achieves overall accuracy comparable to strong baselines, but clearly outperforms them on macro-averaged and minor-class metrics. It delivers the best Overall Top-1 macro-F1 and consistently superior performance on all minor-space-group metrics. Specifically, DyRIS improves Minor-SG Top-1 accuracy by 3.26 percentage points over CrabNet and attains higher Minor-SG Top-3 accuracy than the best-performing PyCaret-based model. Ablation studies show that diversity-enhanced retrieval, quantitative indicators, explicit major-SG bias control, and B/B' ordering information each produce measurable gains, and that the rule-guided inference stage provides benefits that are not matched by simply training an additional classifier or ranker.

Conclusion: DyRIS demonstrates that combining retrieval-augmented LLM reasoning with crystallographic domain rules is an effective way to predict space groups for double perovskites, especially in the presence of strong class imbalance. The framework improves prediction quality for underrepresented space groups without sacrificing overall accuracy and cannot be trivially replaced by conventional ML classifiers or rankers. This suggests that LLM-agent architectures with carefully designed retrieval and rule-based components can address structural-prediction challenges in materials science where rare classes and domain constraints are crucial.

Abstract: Double perovskites (DPs) offer broad compositional tunability, but predicting the space groups (SGs) of stable structures remains difficult because available datasets are often strongly imbalanced toward dominant SG classes. We refer to dominant SG classes as major SGs and underrepresented classes as minor SGs. We introduce Dynamic and Diversity-enhanced Few-shot Retrieval and Rule-Guided Inference for Space-Group Prediction (DyRIS), an LLM-agent-based framework that predicts ranked SG candidates from a given DP composition. DyRIS uses diversity-enhanced dynamic few-shot prompting to retrieve relevant in-context examples while limiting the dominance of frequently represented SGs. It further incorporates rule-guided inference based on B/B' cation ordering, quantitative indicators, and major-SG bias control to refine and rank the final Top-3 SG candidates. We evaluate DyRIS on 3,528 thermodynamically filtered DP entries and compare it with composition-based and descriptor-based baselines. At a training-data ratio of 0.5, DyRIS achieves competitive overall accuracy while obtaining the best Overall Top-1 macro-F1 score and the best performance across all Minor-SG metrics. DyRIS improves Minor-SG Top-1 accuracy by 3.26 percentage points relative to CrabNet and achieves higher Minor-SG Top-3 accuracy than the strongest PyCaret-based baseline. Ablation studies show that diversity-enhanced retrieval, quantitative indicators, major-SG bias control, and B/B' ordering information each contribute to prediction performance. Additional experiments show that the final rule-guided inference step is not easily replaced by conventional classifier- or ranker-based models. These findings demonstrate the potential of combining retrieval-based LLM reasoning with crystallographic domain knowledge for SG prediction in imbalanced materials datasets.

</details>


### [99] [INSIDE the Student's Mind: Jointly Modeling Latent Reasoning and Action in LLM Student Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10492)
*Rose Niousha,Minwoo Kang,Narges Norouzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: INSIDE is a framework that fine-tunes LLMs to simulate not just students’ actions (e.g., code) but also their internal reasoning, leading to more faithful student simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based student simulators can mimic observable outputs (like solutions or code) but struggle to capture the diverse, underlying reasons students have for producing the same answer. In educational contexts—such as evaluating tutoring systems or studying learning processes—this missing layer of reasoning is critical. The authors aim to create student models that reflect not only what students do, but how and why they think, so that simulations are more realistic and useful for research and system evaluation.

Method: The authors introduce INSIDE (INTERNAL STUDENT DIALOGUE), a student modeling framework that explicitly models students’ internal dialogue. They ground this internal dialogue in Bloom’s Taxonomy, spanning cognitive, affective, and action dimensions. They generate paired data consisting of students’ observable actions (e.g., code submissions) and corresponding ‘think traces’ (internal dialogues). They then fine-tune LLMs on these paired traces and actions so that the models can both generate realistic student outputs and narrate plausible internal reasoning. They compare INSIDE to alternative prompting-based frameworks and evaluate both the fidelity of simulated actions and the quality/alignment of the generated internal dialogue.

Result: INSIDE improves the fidelity of student simulation. It yields better action fidelity, meaning that the generated code or solutions more closely match those of real students. Additionally, it improves reasoning alignment: the internal dialogue produced by the model aligns more closely with actual student reasoning processes than baselines do, achieving the highest reported alignment across tested models, up to 57.9%.

Conclusion: By explicitly modeling internal student dialogue grounded in Bloom’s Taxonomy and fine-tuning LLMs on paired action–reasoning data, INSIDE produces student simulators that more accurately capture both what students do and how they think. This leads to higher-fidelity simulations for educational applications, suggesting that future student models should incorporate structured internal reasoning rather than focusing only on observable actions.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based simulators often reproduce observable actions but fail to capture the underlying reasoning behind them. In education, where student simulation is increasingly used for various applications such as evaluating tutoring systems, this gap is especially pronounced. Two students may submit identical submissions for entirely different reasons. We present INTERNAL STUDENT DIALOGUE (INSIDE), a student modeling framework that fine-tunes LLMs not only to act like students but also to think like them. INSIDE generates internal dialogue grounded in Bloom's Taxonomy across cognitive, affective, and action dimensions, and fine-tunes models on paired think traces and actions. We baseline against different prompting frameworks and evaluate on two axes: fidelity of simulated actions and quality of generated internal dialogue. Our evaluations show that INSIDE improves simulation fidelity in both action fidelity, matching code generation of real students, and reasoning alignment, achieving the highest alignment across models up to 57.9%.

</details>


### [100] [GeoForge: Non-Parametric Self-Evolving Agents for Earth-Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10494)
*Xin Xiao,Jiang Zhong,Junnan Zhu,Yingchao Feng,Peijin Wang,Yidan Zhang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoForge is a training-free, self-evolving framework that improves Earth observation tool workflows by converting past tool-use trajectories into structured, reusable execution knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructing valid Earth observation workflows is hard because tools must respect sensing semantics, product dependencies, spatial-temporal compatibility, and parameter constraints. Existing agents search a wide operation space for each query and self-evolving systems fail to organize past heterogeneous workflows into reusable knowledge across decision levels, leading to inefficient and error-prone planning.

Method: GeoForge builds a training-free, self-evolving agent framework that turns completed tool-use trajectories into a structured, nonparametric execution state. It constrains tool operation space using the current sensing context and then retrieves a task-conditioned prior from three complementary memories: (1) Workflow Graph Memory for global operation order, (2) Action-Level Experiences for local corrections, and (3) Adapted Skill Standard Operating Procedures that encode procedural and data constraints. This retrieved prior guides subsequent tool execution while ensuring that current geospatial observations remain the basis of final answers. After each task, a safety-gated distillation process converts grounded trajectories into reusable execution knowledge, closing an execution–distillation–reuse loop, all without updating the backbone LLM.

Result: On multiple geospatial benchmarks and across diverse LLM backbones, GeoForge improves task accuracy and the quality of tool-use trajectories, and substantially reduces tool-planning and reasoning errors for most models compared with baseline agents.

Conclusion: Structuring and reusing past EO tool-use trajectories via complementary memories and a safety-gated distillation loop enables a training-free, self-evolving framework (GeoForge) that consistently enhances planning, accuracy, and robustness of Earth observation agents without modifying the underlying language model.

Abstract: Earth observation (EO) agents construct scientifically valid tool workflows and ground their conclusions in current geospatial evidence. This is challenging because EO workflows are constrained by sensing semantics, product dependencies, spatial and temporal compatibility, and parameter requirements. Existing agents often search a broad operation space for each query, while recent self-evolving systems do not fully organize heterogeneous EO trajectories into reusable knowledge across different decision levels. To solve this problem, we present GeoForge, a training-free, self-evolving framework that transforms completed trajectories into a structured nonparametric execution state. GeoForge constrains the operation space according to the sensing context, then retrieves a task-conditioned prior from three complementary memories. Workflow Graph Memory captures global operation order, Action-Level Experiences provide local corrections, and the Adapted Skill Standard Operating Procedure preserves procedural and data constraints. The retrieved prior guides tool execution, while current observations remain the basis of the final answer. After each task, a safety-gated distillation process converts grounded trajectories into reusable execution knowledge for future retrieval. This execution, distillation, and reuse loop improves planning without updating the backbone LLM. Experiments on multiple geospatial benchmarks demonstrate that GeoForge consistently improves both task accuracy and tool-use trajectory quality across diverse LLM backbones, while substantially reducing tool-planning and reasoning errors for most LLMs.

</details>


### [101] [From Faulty Memories to Corrected Actions: Dependency-Guided Rollback Repair for Memory-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10502)
*Caili Yu,Yiqi Wang,Jiaqi Zhang,Yiqun Duan,Mingkai Zheng,Zhangkai Wu,Kaize Shi,Taotao Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method to repair an agent’s long-term memory after errors by rolling back only the parts causally affected by faulty memories, keeping correct work and minimizing recomputation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents now use persistent memory across sessions, which makes any memory error—poisoned, outdated, or misattributed—long-lived and influential on future reasoning, tool use, and additional memory writes. Existing defenses mostly focus on detection, deletion, or on-the-fly answer revision, but they fail to fix consequences that have already propagated through the agent’s state and future trajectories. Naively wiping or replaying everything is costly and destroys benign state. The authors want a principled way to recover both answers and memory state after a failure while preserving unaffected work and good memories.

Method: They formalize the task of post-failure memory recovery: given a failed execution and a set of diagnosed faulty memories, reconstruct a correct answer and consistent persistent memory while retaining unaffected computation. Their method, dependency-guided rollback repair, builds a typed memory-to-action dependency graph from runtime provenance, explicitly tracking how memories influence actions and new memories. Using this graph, the system traces downstream dependencies of known-bad memories, identifies which memories and actions are causally affected, preserves items that have independent trusted support, deactivates unsupported or tainted memory, and selectively replays only those parts of the computation that are both affected and relevant to the final answer. This yields a targeted rollback-and-replay process rather than a full reset.

Result: On a 150-case controlled benchmark covering three tool-use domains and four kinds of memory failures, their method attains an 85.3% successful recovery rate, outperforming the best competing recovery baseline at 77.3%. It fully removes all diagnosed faulty memories while preserving all benign memories and limits recomputation to selective replay with modest additional LLM calls. On a 50-case stress test adapted from LongMemEval-V2 trajectories, it reaches 68.0% recovery versus 54.0% for the next best method and achieves the highest claim invalidation F1 score (0.669 vs. 0.603), indicating more accurate identification and neutralization of erroneous claims in memory.

Conclusion: Dependency-guided rollback repair offers an effective and efficient strategy for post-failure recovery in LLM agents with persistent memory. By exploiting fine-grained provenance and dependency structure, it can remove diagnosed faulty memories, maintain benign ones, and only recompute answer-relevant affected steps, yielding a favorable trade-off between recovery quality and computational cost. While it does not universally guarantee superior reconstruction for every trace, it significantly improves recovery rates and error invalidation over existing methods, demonstrating that causal, graph-based rollback is a promising direction for robust long-term agent memory management.

Abstract: Persistent memory lets language-model agents reuse information across sessions, but it also makes errors durable: a poisoned, stale, or misattributed record can alter reasoning, tool use, answers, and subsequent memory writes. Existing defenses mainly detect or delete suspicious memories, or revise the current response. Deleting the source leaves already propagated claims, actions, and derived memories active, whereas resetting the store or replaying the full trace destroys benign state and repeats unnecessary computation. We therefore formulate \textbf{post-failure memory recovery: } \textit{given a failed execution and diagnosed faulty memories, recover both the answer and persistent state while retaining unaffected work.} Our \textbf{dependency-guided rollback repair} builds a typed memory-to-action graph from runtime provenance, traces explicit downstream dependencies, preserves candidates with independent trusted support, deactivates unsupported memory state, and selectively replays only answer-relevant affected computation. We evaluate this approach on a 150-case controlled benchmark spanning three tool-use domains and four memory failure types, and on a 50-case trajectory-derived stress test adapted from LongMemEval-V2. On the controlled benchmark, it achieves 85.3\% recovery versus 77.3\% for the best competing recovery method, removes all diagnosed faulty memories, preserves all benign memories, and requires only selective replay with modest LLM-call cost. On the adapted subset, it reaches 68.0\% recovery versus 54.0\% for the next best method, while also achieving the highest claim invalidation F1, 0.669 versus 0.603. Overall, the results do not imply uniformly better trace reconstruction, but show that dependency-guided rollback repair provides a strong recovery--cost trade-off while repairing faulty memory state and preserving benign memory.

</details>


### [102] [MEGA: Self-Evolving Agent Optimization Infrastructure via Wisdom Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10504)
*Jung Hwan Lee,Kyu Ho Lee,Gwang Hoon Yoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MEGA, a three-layer self-evolving infrastructure for coding agents that turns each optimization cycle into reusable knowledge and uses operational evidence to refine both strategies and agent behaviors over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As code-generating agents take over more implementation work, the key challenge becomes systematically improving whole agent systems rather than tuning isolated models. Existing approaches either (1) optimize agents without accumulating reusable knowledge, (2) store knowledge without enabling structured, compositional reasoning over it, or (3) lack a mechanism for this knowledge to adapt based on real-world performance. The paper aims to build an infrastructure where optimization and knowledge evolution are tightly coupled and continuously improve each other.

Method: The authors propose MEGA (Meta Evaluation-Grounded Adaptation), a three-layer framework. Layer 1 mines reusable “wisdom” from past agent sessions using behavioral-pattern clustering and A/B testing, converting effective process patterns into durable assets. Layer 2 decomposes these assets into atomic PCR (Primary-Context-Resultant) units in a typed Wisdom Graph and applies deductive, abductive, and inductive reasoning to infer new relations and construct compositional, context-specific execution plans that go beyond simple embedding-based retrieval. Layer 3 conducts multi-agent collaborative optimization over complex workflows involving code, LLM calls, and tools, using controlled evaluations to attribute performance gains to specific strategy changes. Evidence from Layer 3 is fed back to refine both the wisdom assets and the higher-level curation and optimization strategies.

Result: MEGA yields an infrastructure in which each optimization cycle both improves the operational behavior of coding-agent workflows and enriches the underlying wisdom base and reasoning mechanisms. It supports compositional retrieval of strategies that simple similarity search would miss and can attribute performance improvements to particular strategy changes, enabling more targeted, evidence-grounded optimization over time.

Conclusion: By tightly integrating knowledge extraction, structured reasoning, and evidence-grounded workflow optimization, MEGA turns the process of optimizing coding agents into a self-evolving loop where operational experience directly updates both the reusable wisdom and the meta-strategies that govern its use. This unifies system optimization and knowledge evolution into a single continual process, offering a scalable path to improving complex coding-agent infrastructures.

Abstract: As coding agents increasingly handle implementation, the central challenge shifts from building individual agents to building an infrastructure that systematically improves them. Current approaches optimize agent systems without accumulating transferable knowledge, accumulate knowledge without compositional reasoning over it, and lack a mechanism for that knowledge to self-evolve through operational evidence. MEGA (Meta Evaluation-Grounded Adaptation) addresses these gaps as a self-evolving infrastructure: each optimization cycle produces durable assets, compositional reasoning over those assets guides subsequent optimization, and operational evidence refines both the accumulated wisdom and the reasoning that governs it. Layer 1 distills reusable wisdom from agent sessions through behavioral-pattern clustering and empirical A/B validation, transforming each process into a durable asset. Layer 2 decomposes these assets into atomic PCR (Primary-Context-Resultant) units within a typed Wisdom Graph and performs deductive, abductive, and inductive reasoning to expand implicit relations; it then assembles context-specific execution plans through compositional retrieval that surfaces bridging knowledge unreachable by embedding similarity alone. Layer 3 performs multi-agent collaborative optimization over heterogeneous agent workflows (code nodes, LLM calls, and tool-using agents), attributing improvement effects to specific strategy changes through controlled evaluation that eliminates data variance. Evidence fed back from Layer 3 drives the self-evolution of both the curation strategies that govern wisdom composition and the optimization trajectories accumulated across runs. The result is an infrastructure in which optimizing an agent system and evolving the knowledge that guides optimization are one and the same process.

</details>


### [103] [RadFusion: Towards Threshold-Controllable Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10505)
*Ying Jin,Noel C. F. Codella,John Corring,Mu Wei,Dinei Florencio,Eric Horvitz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RadFusion is a controllable radiology report generation framework that aligns its diagnostic statements with a tunable sensitivity–specificity threshold, making reports both clinically adaptable and ROC-evaluable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated radiology report generators behave like black boxes: they emit a single fixed report with no way to adjust the sensitivity–specificity trade-off of the diagnoses they state. However, different clinical scenarios require different operating points (e.g., high sensitivity for emergency triage vs. high specificity for confirmatory reads), and regulators expect ROC-based validation of diagnostic systems. Current generators thus cannot be tuned to context or evaluated in standard ROC terms, limiting their clinical and regulatory utility.

Method: RadFusion decomposes report generation into three coordinated components: (1) a multi-label image classifier that outputs per-disease confidence scores; (2) a VQA-style report generator that produces detailed, grounded textual descriptions of radiologic findings; and (3) a large language model that rewrites the generator’s draft report so that explicit diagnostic statements follow the classifier’s binary decisions at a chosen threshold. By sweeping the classifier threshold, users can control whether the final report favors sensitivity or specificity, while the textual content remains grounded in the generator’s visual findings.

Result: On the MIMIC-CXR dataset, when the classifier threshold is swept and final reports are converted back into per-disease labels, RadFusion’s performance traces the same ROC curve as the underlying classifier, demonstrating ROC conformance. Beyond controllability, the combined system outperforms uncontrolled report generation: at a fixed specificity level, sensitivity increases by 6.9%, and at a fixed sensitivity level, specificity increases by 20.7%, indicating both better diagnostic performance and flexible operating-point selection.

Conclusion: RadFusion turns radiology report generation from a fixed, opaque process into a threshold-controllable, ROC-evaluable diagnostic tool. By aligning report content with a tunable classifier while grounding language in detailed image-based descriptions, it enables adapting report behavior to clinical context, supports standard ROC-based validation needed for regulatory approval, and improves diagnostic reliability compared with traditional, uncontrolled generators.

Abstract: Automated radiology report generation is advancing rapidly in response to the shortage of radiologists, yet unlike a perception model, existing generation models offer no control over the sensitivity-specificity trade-off of their diagnostic content. Such control is essential because clinical scenarios diverge: emergency triage prioritizes sensitivity to reduce missed findings, whereas confirmatory interpretation emphasizes specificity to limit unnecessary interventions. A single fixed report can neither adapt to these scenarios nor support the ROC-based validation widely expected for regulatory clearance. We introduce RadFusion, a framework that equips report generation with threshold controllability. Our method fuses a multi-label classifier, which provides per-disease confidence scores, with a VQA-based report generator, which describes medical findings in detail; an LLM then rewrites the report so that its stated diagnoses follow the classifier's decisions at the selected threshold while staying grounded in the generator's descriptions. On MIMIC-CXR, the performance of RadFusion conforms to the classifier's ROC curve: sweeping the threshold and mapping the reports back to class labels reproduces the classifier's validated ROC performance. This conformance makes generated reports quantitatively evaluable through ROC analysis, strengthening the case for regulatory clearance, and enables operating-point selection that matches report behavior to clinical context. Moreover, combining the two model types improves diagnostic accuracy over uncontrolled generation: sensitivity increases by 6.9% at matched specificity, and specificity by 20.7% at matched sensitivity. These results show that RadFusion makes report generation clinically adaptable, quantitatively verifiable, and diagnostically more reliable.

</details>


### [104] [MAP-Graph: Provenance-Aware Shared Memory for Multi-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10509)
*Yiqi Wang,Zihao Yan,Jiaqi Zhang,Zhangkai Wu,Mingkai Zheng,Zequn Sun,Yanming Zhu,Taotao Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MAP-Graph, a provenance-aware memory layer for language-model agents that enforces permissions and trust while retrieving and using shared memories, improving safety and decision accuracy in long workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shared memory allows language-model agents to reuse information, but it can also expose private or untrusted content and enable unsafe actions when authorization rules and trust levels are not enforced. Existing methods handle retrieval, scoping, or lineage, but they fail to clearly separate strict access control from graded trust and do not adapt evidence requirements to the riskiness of actions. There is a need for a mechanism that treats provenance not just as audit metadata but as an active control signal that governs which memories can be used and how.

Method: The authors propose MAP-Graph, a memory layer that models agents, data sources, memories, claims, and actions as nodes in a typed execution graph with explicit ancestry relationships. MAP-Graph filters out records that do not satisfy permission constraints, computes trust over ancestry paths (multiplicative path trust), reranks the remaining candidate memories by both semantic similarity and trust, and then applies a risk-sensitive gate that decides whether an action can proceed given the available evidence. The system also preserves the lineage of the information used, enabling later audit. They evaluate MAP-Graph on a controlled benchmark of 2,700 synthetic tasks per method across three domains and conduct ablations and transfer experiments with different LLM backbones.

Result: On the benchmark, MAP-Graph reaches 94.96% overall task success and 72.70% exact decision accuracy, and 90.22% accuracy in the clean setting where success requires issuing a correct Allow rather than merely defaulting to a safe block or intervention. Ablation studies show that permission filtering, path trust, and action gating each contribute to performance, and transfer experiments using two additional model backbones maintain the method’s advantages in exact decision quality and access control. These findings indicate that the proposed design choices collectively improve safe and correct behavior in memory-augmented LLM agents.

Conclusion: The study concludes that representing provenance explicitly in a typed execution graph and using it as an operational control signal—through permission filtering, trust-weighted retrieval, and risk-sensitive action gating—improves both safety and decision accuracy for language-model agents using shared memory. Rather than being only for post-hoc auditing, provenance can actively govern which evidence is admissible and sufficient for actions, leading to better access control and robustness in the evaluated settings.

Abstract: Shared memory helps language-model agents reuse information across long workflows, yet relevant evidence may not be admissible for a particular agent or action. Because restrictions propagate through derivations, summaries can conceal private, poisoned, untrusted, or revoked sources, enabling unauthorized reads or unsafe actions. Existing approaches provide semantic retrieval, scoped access, or lineage tracking, but do not clearly separate hard authorization from graded trust or adapt evidence requirements to action risk. We introduce MAP-Graph, a provenance-aware memory layer that represents agents, sources, memories, claims, and actions in a typed execution graph. It traces ancestry, excludes permission-ineligible records, reranks eligible memories by semantic similarity and multiplicative path trust, and applies a risk-sensitive gate before action execution while retaining affected lineage for audit. On a controlled benchmark of 2,700 synthetic tasks per method across three domains, MAP-Graph achieves 94.96\% overall task success, 72.70\% exact decision accuracy, and 90.22\% in the clean setting, where success requires a correct \textsc{Allow} rather than a safe intervention. Ablations isolate the roles of permission filtering, path trust, and action gating, while transfer tests with two additional backbones preserve the exact-decision and access-control advantages. These results support provenance as an operational control signal, rather than only post-hoc audit metadata, within the evaluated setting.

</details>


### [105] [Measuring Semantic Abstractness of SAE Features via Nonlocality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10537)
*Chuqiao Lin,Shivaji Sondhi,Xiao-Liang Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Feature Nonlocality (FNL), an entropy-based metric to quantify how abstract or context-dependent a sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature is, and shows it correlates with semantic abstractness, helps distinguish reasoning features from token-level ones, and is useful for auditing and steering LLM behaviour.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of sparse autoencoder (SAE) features in LLMs want to separate superficial, token-bound features from genuinely abstract, high-level ones (e.g., reasoning, contextual understanding). Current approaches—automatic interpretability descriptions and causal steering performance—do not reliably indicate the abstraction level of features. A principled, model- and label-independent measure is needed to assess whether a feature reflects nonlocal, contextual information versus local lexical cues, to better evaluate mechanistic explanations and choose effective features for interventions.

Method: The authors define Feature Nonlocality (FNL) as the entropy of the distribution of per-position influence on an SAE feature’s activation: higher entropy means influence is more spread across positions (more nonlocal/contextual), while lower entropy indicates a few positions dominate (more local/token-based). They compute FNL for features learned by SAEs over LLM activations and compare FNL to various existing proxy metrics of semantic abstractness derived from LLM-based evaluation. They also construct pairs of features classified as contextual reasoning vs token-driven and test if FNL correctly ranks them. Finally, they apply FNL in two downstream settings: auditing jailbreak-mitigation features to inspect what they actually detect, and selecting high- vs low-FNL features for activation steering in DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B on the MATH-500 benchmark.

Result: FNL shows positive correlation with existing LLM-based proxy metrics for semantic abstractness of SAE features. In experiments on feature pairs consisting of one contextual (reasoning) and one token-level feature, FNL assigns a higher score to the contextual feature in 73–84% of random pairs, indicating strong discriminative ability. In the jailbreak mitigation audit, they find that most features that are effective at blocking jailbreaks are actually low-FNL positional features (e.g., tied to local pattern/position) rather than truly abstract detectors of harmful intent. For steering on MATH-500 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, steering high-FNL features improves accuracy by 4.6 percentage points over the unsteered model and yields better performance than steering low-FNL features, though this advantage varies across models.

Conclusion: Feature Nonlocality (FNL) is proposed as an LLM-independent, label-free metric that quantifies how nonlocal and thus how abstract/contextual an SAE feature is, based solely on the distribution of positional influence on its activation. FNL correlates with existing abstractness proxies, effectively distinguishes contextual reasoning features from token-level ones, and reveals that many jailbreak-mitigation features are low-level positional ones rather than genuine intent detectors. Moreover, selecting high-FNL features for steering can improve reasoning performance on math benchmarks. Overall, FNL offers a practical tool for evaluating the abstraction level of mechanistic features and for choosing better targets for downstream control and interpretability applications.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have helped uncover mechanistic explanations for LLM behaviours such as reasoning, jailbreaking etc., via understanding the corresponding task-relevant and causally effective features. To evaluate such mechanistic explanations, downstream studies must distinguish surface lexical features from genuinely high-level ones. However, neither an autointerp-based semantic description nor causal steering utility fully resolves the abstraction level of a feature. To this end, we introduce \emph{Feature Nonlocality} (FNL), defined as the entropy of the normalized per-position influence on an SAE feature's activation. We report that FNL correlates with existing LLM-based proxy metrics of feature semantic abstractness, and successfully distinguishes context-dependent reasoning features from token-driven ones, correctly assigning the higher FNL to the contextual feature in $73$--$84\%$ of randomly drawn pairs that consist of one contextual and one token-level feature.
  We demonstrate two downstream applications. We audit SAE-based features used for jailbreak mitigation and find surprisingly that most effective features are positional features with low FNL rather than genuinely recognizing harmful intents.
  We report that steering high-FNL features in DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B improves MATH-500 accuracy by $4.6$ points over the unsteered model and outperforms steering low-FNL features, though the gains are model-specific. We conclude that FNL provides an LLM-independent, label-free, correlational witness of the abstraction level of an SAE feature, with applications in evaluating mechanistic explanations as well as selecting features for downstream interventions.

</details>


### [106] [SKILLER: Language-Level Reinforcement Learning for Reusable Skill Extraction in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10538)
*Chenhao Dang,Siyuan Xiong,Conghui He,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SKILLER, a natural-language reinforcement learning framework that uses a strong model to automatically generate and refine skills tailored to small open-source models, significantly improving their performance while cutting deployment costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent skills can reliably constrain and guide language models for repeatable, high-quality task execution, but using strong closed-source models to run these skills is very expensive. Open-source models that can run on consumer GPUs are cheaper but weaker, and existing skill-generation methods do not effectively create skills tailored to these compact models. There is a need for an automated way to produce executor-specific skills that let small, cheap models perform real-world tasks competitively.

Method: SKILLER frames skill generation as a natural-language reinforcement learning process where a powerful model serves as both actor and critic, and the small-model agent system is treated as the environment. The strong model proposes skills in natural language, observes how the small model performs with those skills on tasks, and then critiques and updates the skills based on feedback, with all RL signals (rewards, policy updates) expressed in natural language rather than numeric vectors. This loop iteratively refines a library of executor-specific skills for the target small model.

Result: On five benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.5-4B as executors, SKILLER surpasses three open-source and one closed-source baselines for skill generation or evolution, with absolute performance improvements of 4.3–20.4 percentage points for the 9B model and 1.8–13.3 points for the 4B model. On single-skill tasks in SkillsBench, the small models equipped with SKILLER-generated skills match the performance of strong closed-source systems, demonstrating that tailored skills can close much of the capability gap.

Conclusion: Automatically generated, executor-specific skills can substantially boost the effectiveness of small open-source language models, enabling them to approach the performance of strong closed-source models on structured task suites while remaining much cheaper to deploy. SKILLER shows that a natural-language-based reinforcement learning framework, where a strong model guides a weaker one through language-only feedback, is a practical and powerful way to build and refine such skills for real-world agent systems.

Abstract: Agent skills represent a standardized format for packaging procedural knowledge and domain expertise, serving within agent harness systems as an essential mechanism to continually constrain a language model's behavior space for repeatable, high-quality task execution. However, because strong closed-source models entail high inference costs, current popular agent harnesses, such as Codex and OpenClaw, remain prohibitively expensive when deploying these skills to accomplish real-world tasks. The rapid capability enhancement of open-source models deployable on consumer-grade GPUs presents a compelling opportunity to drastically reduce these costs by leveraging skill-based behavioral constraints. Nevertheless, automatically generating effective skills tailored specifically for such compact models remains a significant practical challenge. To address this, we propose SKILLER, a natural-language-driven reinforcement learning framework designed to automatically generate executor-specific skills for small models, which employs a strong model as the actor and critic, treats the small-model agent system as the environment, and propagates all reinforcement learning signals entirely via natural language. Extensive experimental evaluations across five relevant benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.5-4B demonstrate that SKILLER outperforms three open-source and one closed-source skill generation or evolution methods, achieving absolute gains ranging from 4.3 to 20.4 percentage points for the 9B model and 1.8 to 13.3 points for the 4B model, while remarkably matching the performance of strong closed-source models on single-skill tasks in SkillsBench. The project is available at https://github.com/DANG-ai/SKILLER.

</details>


### [107] [Reinforcement Learning-Based Laser Cutting Machine Parameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10549)
*Khanh Quan Pham,Majid Kundroo,Geunwoo Ban,Seongho Bae,Taehong Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an RL-based algorithm (RL²C) that automatically tunes laser-cutting parameters for optical films, achieving better cut quality with fewer trials and less time than existing RL optimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Laser-based cutting of optical films needs precise tuning of parameters like focal length and laser power, which currently relies on slow, inaccurate trial-and-error by human operators. This manual process increases taper defects, film wastage, time, and labor cost. There is a need for an automatic, data-driven method that can quickly and reliably discover near-optimal cutting parameters across different film types while adapting to changing conditions.

Method: The paper introduces RL²C, a reinforcement-learning framework using Q-learning with an epsilon-greedy exploration strategy. The algorithm models laser-cut parameter selection as a sequential decision problem, where states represent cutting conditions/films, actions correspond to parameter settings (e.g., focal length, power), and rewards reflect cut quality and waste. RL²C adds a dynamic environment space adaptability mechanism that can expand or adjust the state space when new, previously unseen cutting conditions appear during multi-batch experiments, allowing the agent to continue learning without retraining from scratch.

Result: In experiments on optical film cutting, RL²C identifies optimal or near-optimal cutting parameters with fewer optimization steps and substantially less time compared with other RL-based optimization methods. Quantitatively, RL²C reduces optimization steps by up to 12.5% and processing time by up to 81.8%. It also significantly decreases taper size and film wastage, indicating improved cut quality and process efficiency.

Conclusion: RL²C demonstrates that reinforcement learning is effective for industrial laser-cutting parameter optimization. By combining Q-learning with dynamic state-space adaptability, the method accelerates convergence to good parameter sets, improves cut quality, reduces film waste and processing time, and lessens the need for manual intervention. This suggests RL-based controllers can enhance robustness and productivity in laser-processing applications and potentially broader manufacturing settings.

Abstract: Achieving high accuracy in laser-based cutting of optical films requires careful tuning of parameters such as focal length and laser power beam, adjusted according to the specific properties of each film type. Trial-and-error based traditional methods are used to find the most suitable cutting parameters for various films, but they are slow and inaccurate. To address this issue, this paper presents the Reinforcement Learning for Laser Cutting (RL$^{2}$C) algorithm, which uses Q-learning with an epsilon-greedy policy to dynamically optimize cutting parameters, significantly reducing taper size and film wastage. Additionally, RL$^{2}$C incorporates a dynamic environment space adaptability mechanism to allow it to adapt to new states encountered during the learning process over multiple batches of experiments. Experimental results demonstrate that RL$^{2}$C requires fewer steps and less time to find optimal cutting parameters compared to various RL-based optimization methods. Specifically, RL$^{2}$C reduces the number of optimization steps by up to 12.5\% and processing time by up to 81.8\% compared to existing methods. This study demonstrates the potential of RL in industrial laser-cutting processes by improving cut quality, reducing time and film wastage, and minimizing manual interventions.

</details>


### [108] [DashArena: Benchmarking LLMs on Interactive Analytic Dashboard Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10567)
*Xiaotong Wang,Dazhen Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces DashArena, the first benchmark that evaluates automatically generated analytic dashboards by considering both the dashboard and its interaction trajectory, using a VLM-based judge distilled into DashJudge-8B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing models can auto-generate analytic dashboards from data and natural language, but it is hard to evaluate how useful these dashboards are. Traditional evaluations focus on static layout or code execution success, which miss whether the dashboard truly supports realistic analytical workflows and high-quality interactions in open-ended settings.

Method: Propose DashArena, a benchmark where systems must output an analytic dashboard plus a replayable interaction trajectory that represents an intended analytical workflow. A browser executor replays the trajectory to create visual and execution evidence. A vision-language model (VLM) judge compares pairs of candidate dashboards with their interaction evidence, and the comparisons are aggregated into a leaderboard with the Bradley–Terry model. The VLM judge is further distilled into an 8B-parameter open-weight model, DashJudge-8B. Conduct human evaluations and ablation studies to measure agreement and the effect of including interaction evidence, and run experiments with state-of-the-art models on the benchmark.

Result: DashJudge-8B aligns well with human judgments, indicating the judge can effectively approximate human assessment of dashboard quality. Ablation studies show that including interaction evidence boosts judge agreement compared to using static or execution-only information. Experiments with strong frontier models on DashArena reveal ongoing deficiencies in rendering correctness, analytical reasoning, and interaction design when generating dashboards.

Conclusion: Realistic generation of analytic dashboards remains a difficult problem for current models. Evaluating dashboards in an interaction-aware, workflow-grounded manner, as in DashArena, uncovers failure modes that static appearance checks or execution-only tests miss. DashArena and DashJudge-8B provide a principled framework and tools for benchmarking and advancing open-ended analytic dashboard generation.

Abstract: Analytic dashboards combine coordinated views and interactions for data exploration and decision-making. Recent models can generate them from data and natural-language goals, but evaluating their usefulness remains difficult. Dashboard generation is open-ended, and neither static appearance nor successful execution alone captures analytical support and interaction quality. We introduce DashArena, to our knowledge the first benchmark for open-ended, task-grounded generation of interactive analytic dashboards. Its key innovation is to require each system to generate both a dashboard and a replayable interaction trajectory. A browser executor replays the trajectory and turns the system's intended analytical workflow into reproducible visual and execution evidence. A VLM judge compares candidates using this evidence, and Bradley--Terry aggregation produces the leaderboard. We further distill the judge into the open-weight DashJudge-8B. Human evaluations show that DashJudge-8B effectively reproduces human judgments and ablations show that interaction evidence improves judge agreement. Experiments with frontier models reveal persistent rendering, analytical, and interaction failures. Together, these results show that realistic dashboard generation remains challenging and that interaction-aware evaluation captures failures missed by static or execution-only checks.

</details>


### [109] [Agentic Instruction Data Selection: Let DataMaster Interpret Your Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10579)
*Fanqi Zhou,Qiaosheng Chen,Zixian Huang,Gong Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DataMaster, an agent that automatically designs instruction data selection strategies from natural language user requirements, outperforming static heuristics and even full-pool training in several domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction data selection relies on single metrics and hand-crafted heuristics, which fail to generalize across diverse, complex real-world datasets. Developers must repeatedly inspect data and tune rules for each new task, a process that is time-consuming, ad hoc, and error-prone. There is a need for an automated, general framework that can interpret user needs and synthesize effective selection strategies without manual intervention.

Method: The authors propose the Instruction Data Selection Agent (DataMaster), which takes natural language descriptions of a user’s data needs and automatically composes a tailored selection strategy by combining and orchestrating multiple selection metrics or criteria. DataMaster acts as an agent that interprets user intent, decides which selection operations to use, and applies them over a large instruction pool to curate a training subset. The system is evaluated across different domains (math, medical, and code) and compared against static selection baselines and full-pool training.

Result: Across math, medical, and code benchmarks, DataMaster consistently outperforms static heuristic baselines in most evaluated settings, and in a substantial number of cases even surpasses training on the entire available data pool. These results indicate that automatically orchestrated, intent-driven selection strategies can yield both higher performance and more efficient data usage than traditional fixed-metric or full-pool approaches.

Conclusion: Automating instruction data selection via an agent that understands natural language requirements and composes selection strategies is more effective and scalable than manual heuristic design. DataMaster reduces developer burden, adapts to diverse domains, and can even outperform full-pool training, suggesting that future data curation pipelines should be agent-driven and intent-aware rather than relying on static rules or single metrics.

Abstract: Although existing instruction data selection methods have introduced various metrics, the inherent complexity of real-world datasets makes it impractical for any single metric to generalize across all scenarios. Developers are thus often forced to manually inspect data and craft heuristic rules for each new application---a tedious and error-prone process. In this paper, we propose a paradigm shift from manual configuration to automated orchestration via the Instruction Data Selection Agent (DataMaster), which interprets user intent and autonomously composes optimal selection strategies. By allowing users to specify data needs through natural language descriptions, DataMaster simplifies data curation and removes the burden of manual strategy design. Extensive experiments across the math, medical, and code domains show that DataMaster outperforms static baselines in most settings and surpasses full-pool training in a substantial number of cases. The implementation of DataMaster and the scripts needed to reproduce the reported pipeline are publicly available at https://github.com/nju-websoft/DataMaster.

</details>


### [110] [HexEval: An Evidence-Driven Hexagonal Framework for Multidimensional Scholar Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10584)
*Xiaokang Qu,Yiting Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HexEval is a multidimensional, evidence-driven framework that evaluates scholars along six dimensions using both intrinsic paper quality and externally verifiable scholarly signals, producing interpretable, auditable profiles instead of opaque scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional scholar assessment heavily relies on citation-based metrics and reputation proxies, which are coarse, often biased, and lack transparency. Recent LLM-based methods mainly evaluate individual papers and do not provide holistic views of scholars or retain explicit evidence and reasoning paths. The authors want a principled, auditable way to assess scholars that: (1) captures multiple facets of scholarship, (2) grounds judgments in verifiable evidence, and (3) addresses opacity and bias issues of aggregate metrics and black-box models.

Method: The authors design HexEval, a hexagonal framework with two evidence layers and six total dimensions. The intrinsic layer assesses anonymized representative works along three axes: research rigor, methodological innovation, and scientific contribution. The external layer uses heterogeneous, publicly verifiable data (e.g., GitHub, Lens, OpenAlex) to characterize scholars via knowledge translation, research coherence, and academic impact. HexEval structures evaluation as evidence-driven reasoning: it collects and organizes evidence, generates dimension-specific rationales, and preserves intermediate signals instead of collapsing everything into a single score. They run experiments to compare HexEval’s outputs with human judgments or external reference criteria, and explore calibration strategies for intrinsic quality and the ability of external modules to recover trajectory and impact patterns.

Result: Across the six dimensions, HexEval shows varying but meaningful alignment with human or external benchmarks. For the intrinsic layer, applying structured calibration improves absolute agreement with human assessments of paper quality. For the external layer, the modules successfully capture scholars’ broad career trajectories and ordinal impact signals (e.g., who is more influential), though not necessarily precise numeric metrics. The framework also surfaces where public scholarly data are sparse or ambiguous, affecting coverage and attribution of contributions.

Conclusion: Evidence-driven reasoning over heterogeneous scholarly data can support transparent, auditable AI-assisted scholar assessment better than purely metric-based or opaque model-based approaches. HexEval’s two-layer, six-dimension design produces interpretable profiles with explicit evidence and rationales, but it is limited by the quality and completeness of public data sources. The work suggests a promising paradigm for future assessment systems that balance intrinsic research quality with externally verifiable scholarly behavior while making evaluation processes inspectable and debuggable.

Abstract: Scholar assessment plays a fundamental role in faculty recruitment, funding allocation, academic promotion, and talent discovery. Existing scholar assessment methods predominantly rely on bibliometric indicators and reputation proxies, while recent large language model (LLM)-based approaches mainly focus on evaluating individual research papers rather than comprehensively assessing scholars. We argue that scholar assessment should be formulated as an evidence-driven reasoning problem that jointly considers intrinsic research quality and externally verifiable scholarly behavior. To this end, we propose HexEval, an evidence-driven hexagonal framework for multidimensional scholar assessment. HexEval explicitly organizes scholar assessment into two complementary evidence layers. The intrinsic layer evaluates anonymized representative works along three dimensions, namely research rigor, methodological innovation, and scientific contribution, whereas the external layer characterizes scholars through knowledge translation, research coherence, and academic impact using heterogeneous evidence collected from GitHub, Lens, OpenAlex, and other publicly verifiable sources. Instead of producing opaque aggregate scores, HexEval preserves intermediate evidence, dimension-specific rationales, and verification signals throughout the evaluation process, enabling interpretable and auditable scholar profiles. Experiments across all six dimensions show dimension-dependent agreement with human or external reference criteria: structured calibration improves absolute agreement for intrinsic quality, while the external modules recover broad trajectory and ordinal impact signals. These results support evidence-driven reasoning over heterogeneous scholarly evidence as a promising paradigm for auditable AI-assisted scholar assessment, while exposing the coverage and attribution limitations of public scholarly data.

</details>


### [111] [Curate Before You Connect: Identity and Ontology Tagging in a Production Knowledge Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10644)
*Vaibhav Dangaich,Kevin Lewis,Kundeshwar Pundalik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper describes how to turn extracted entities and relations from government documents into a large knowledge graph, highlighting that identity resolution and ontology tagging are the most destructive and error-prone steps, and proposing conservative, evidence-anchored policies for both.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In building a knowledge graph from many government documents, the most dangerous errors happen not in extraction but when deciding which records represent the same real-world entity and what ontology types they should have. Once entities are merged or mis-typed, the errors are hard or impossible to undo and often leave no trace. The authors want ingestion and tagging methods that minimize irreversible mistakes and make uncertainty explicit.

Method: They design an ingestion and identity-resolution layer that operates in two stages. First, a "record-identity ladder" determines sameness using a hierarchy of signals: exact identifiers, name fields, display names, and type-scoped position, avoiding fuzzy name similarity. This supports careful de-duplication within tables. Second, when writing to the graph, a coarser canonical-name key is used so that records with exactly equal canonical names are automatically merged. For ontology tagging, they implement multi-class tagging but revise it after discovering that using name fragments alone spuriously creates type assertions; they enforce that each role or class assignment must have anchored, explicit evidence in the source. They also instrument the system to measure conformance debt, track secondary classifications that compensate for errors in primary classes, and maintain a human curation queue.

Result: Using this pipeline, they construct a knowledge graph with 537,157 entities and 2,198,567 relationships from 98,795 government documents. The conservative identity policy means entity resolution only proposes candidates for human review rather than automatically resolving ambiguous cases; this follows an incident where an incorrect merge of two name variants corrupted data and caused loss of eight entities. For ontology tagging, requiring anchored evidence reduces the number of role assignments on a test sample from 36 down to 4, with those 4 all being correct. They also quantify the system's remaining ontology/conformance problems and observe that secondary classifications often compensate for a mis-parented primary class, while a curation queue has grown to 48,403 pending proposals with only 775 resolved by humans.

Conclusion: Identity decisions in knowledge-graph construction are fundamentally more destructive than extraction errors, so automation should be limited to clearly safe cases and treated as candidate generation rather than final resolution. Exact identifiers and conservative canonical-name keys are preferable to similarity-based matching, and ontology tagging must rely on explicit, anchored evidence rather than name-based heuristics to avoid fabricating classifications. Even with these safeguards, significant conformance debt and curation backlog remain, underscoring the need for ongoing, human-in-the-loop maintenance rather than fully automated entity and ontology management.

Abstract: Extraction produces candidate entities and relationships; writing them into a graph is where identity is decided, and identity decisions are destructive in a way extraction errors are not. A wrong type can be corrected later, but two records merged under one identity cannot be separated once their properties have been combined, and the merge leaves no error behind. This paper describes the ingestion and ontology-tagging layer that turns a validated extraction stream into a knowledge graph of 537,157 entities and 2,198,567 relationships drawn from 98,795 government documents. We describe a record-identity ladder that decides sameness from identifier columns, name columns, display names and type-scoped position rather than from name similarity. The ladder governs de-duplication within parsed tables, while the graph write applies a coarser canonical-name key, so records sharing a canonical name merge automatically on exact equality. We argue rather than demonstrate that this is where the automation line belongs: no identity benchmark is reported, and the over-merges the key permits are undetectable by construction. That policy, under which entity resolution only ever flags candidates, followed an incident in which two surface forms of one name were merged, corrupting a correct record and deleting eight entities from an unrelated document. We then describe multi-class ontology tagging and an evidence asymmetry we did not anticipate: an entity name is an instance label rather than a type assertion, so matching name fragments against a class index invents classifications. Requiring anchored evidence cut role assignments on an enriched sample from 36 to 4, all confirmed correct. We quantify the graph's conformance debt, show secondary classifications compensating for a mis-parented primary class, and describe a curation queue grown to 48,403 pending proposals against 775 human decisions.

</details>


### [112] [Decision-Aware Approximation of Belief Functions for Evidential Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10650)
*Sohaib Afifi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new way to approximate belief mass functions that prioritizes preserving decision quality (low regret) in optimization problems rather than preserving similarity between mass functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical approximations of mass functions in evidence theory try to keep the approximated mass function close to the original using distances such as Jaccard or Jousselme. However, in linear combinatorial optimization with evidential costs, what ultimately matters is the decision quality, not how similar the two mass functions are. Existing methods can lead to different, worse decisions even if the approximation is distance-optimal.

Method: The authors define a decision-aware approximation framework based on minimizing decision regret: decisions are made using the compressed mass function but evaluated under the true mass function. They compare this to traditional distance-based approximations using a shortest-path example where distance-optimal compression can flip the chosen path. They derive a one-point regret bound at the true optimum, formulate an exact dynamic programming algorithm for the scalar case to compute optimal decision-aware approximations, and design an online extension that prunes focal elements before the final cost is known.

Result: They show analytically that the regret can be localized at the true optimal decision and exploited for efficient computation via dynamic programming in the scalar setting, plus an online pruning variant. Empirical experiments on random instances demonstrate that their decision-aware compression changes the optimal decision less frequently than standard representation-aware (distance-based) compression, for both a linear objective and a nonlinear proxy read-out.

Conclusion: Distance-based approximation of mass functions can be misleading for decision-making in evidential optimization problems. A decision-aware, regret-based compression better preserves the optimal decision. The proposed theoretical bounds, dynamic programming approach, and online pruning scheme provide practical tools that more reliably maintain decision quality than classical representation-focused methods.

Abstract: Reducing the number of focal elements of a mass function is classically driven by an intrinsic distance, such as Jaccard or Jousselme, that keeps the approximation close to the original as a body of evidence. We consider instead the case where the mass function feeds a linear combinatorial optimisation problem with evidential costs. What should then be preserved is not the closeness of the two mass functions, but the quality of the decision they induce. We introduce a decision-aware approximation that targets the regret of the decision: one decides with the cheaper approximation and is evaluated under the true mass function. On a minimal shortest path, the distance-optimal approximation flips the decision while a decision-aware merge preserves it, and this occurs on a non-negligible fraction of random instances. We prove a one-point bound that localises the regret at the true optimum, turn it into an exact dynamic program for the scalar case, and extend it to an online version that prunes focal elements before the final cost is known. In experiments the decision-aware compressor flips the decision less often than representation-aware compression, for both the linear criterion and a non-linear proxy read-out.

</details>


### [113] [Operationalising Relative Causal Knowledge: Backbone Identifiability from Private Reports on a Shared Outcome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10664)
*Fabrizio Russo,Mark Somers*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies when a common causal “backbone” across agents is identifiable from their private causal models and shows that, in a basic setting, it is generally not identified; it becomes identifiable only under stronger structure and when agents share causal response functions, not just observational summaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent systems, policymakers, or scientists with different perspectives often need to coordinate using a shared causal representation. The Relativity of Causal Knowledge (RCK) proposes that this is done via a common interventionally consistent abstraction (backbone). However, it is unclear under what conditions this backbone is uniquely determined by what each agent individually knows. The paper aims to clarify when and how such a common causal structure can be recovered from heterogeneous private causal knowledge.

Method: The authors analyze a stylized two-agent common-effect setting where each agent observes and identifies the causal effect of its own private cause on a shared outcome, but not the joint system. They impose standard assumptions (compatibility of models, non-degeneracy, and local overlap) and study the identification problem for the joint intervention kernel that would serve as a backbone. They then introduce additional structural assumptions such as additive separability and consider the type of information agents communicate (observational residual summaries vs. fully identified response functions). Formal identification arguments show which aspects of the joint causal structure remain underdetermined and how stronger assumptions plus richer communication restore identification.

Result: Under the baseline assumptions, the local single-cause causal marginals held by each agent do not pin down a unique joint backbone; there exist infinitely many joint intervention kernels consistent with the same private reports but that diverge on joint (multi-cause) interventions. Introducing additive separability removes an interaction degree of freedom in the joint effect, but observational residual-based summaries that agents might naturally share are still not enough to identify the backbone. Identification is achievable if agents share causally identified response functions, i.e., richer causal objects rather than just observational or residual information. An example from education value-added modeling illustrates how mis-specification of what is communicated leads to underidentified policy composition.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the existence of a shared causal backbone across agents is, in general, not guaranteed by their individual causal marginals: multiple incompatible joint causal structures can rationalize the same private knowledge. To uniquely recover a backbone, one must impose extra structure, such as additive separability, and, crucially, agents must communicate sufficiently rich causal information, like identified response functions. Thus, coordinating policies across agents with different models is primarily a problem of what causal content is communicated and only secondarily a technical problem of composing policies once the right backbone has been identified.

Abstract: The Relativity of Causal Knowledge (RCK) explains how a network of agents with different structural causal models can exchange causal knowledge through a shared interventionally consistent abstraction, or backbone. We ask the prior identification question that this transport mechanism presupposes: when is that backbone determined by the agents' private causal knowledge? In the basic two-agent common-effect case, two private causes influence one shared outcome and each agent identifies only the single-cause causal marginal relevant to its own perspective. We show that, under standard compatibility, non-degeneracy, and local overlap assumptions, those local causal marginals do not identify a unique backbone. Infinitely many joint intervention kernels can induce exactly the same private reports while disagreeing on joint interventions. We then give a conditional recovery result. Additive separability removes the hidden interaction degree of freedom, but observational residual summaries remain insufficient. Identification becomes possible when agents communicate causally identified response functions. An education value-added example illustrates why this is first a communication problem, and only then a policy-composition problem.

</details>


### [114] [VERDICT: Training-Free Step-Wise Verification of Multimodal Reasoning via Disagreement-Aware Consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10665)
*Rohit Sinha,Kunal Tilaganji,Tanuja Ganu,Nagarajan Natarajan,Amit Sharma,Vineeth Balasubramanian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VERDICT is a training-free, domain-agnostic method that verifies step-by-step reasoning in multimodal LLMs by explicitly modeling and exploiting disagreements among multiple frozen verifiers to compute a principled consensus score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs often produce reasoning chains with subtle errors that yield wrong answers. Existing verification methods either rely on expensive labeled supervision and don’t generalize well across tasks, or they combine signals from multiple verifiers using naive aggregation (like averaging), ignoring that disagreements among verifiers themselves are informative about correctness. The paper is motivated by the need for a scalable, training-free verification approach that can robustly detect incorrect reasoning steps by leveraging cross-modal and cross-verifier disagreement.

Method: The authors formalize verification as a coupled scoring problem over multiple heterogeneous, frozen verifiers. They cast this as a coordination game whose equilibrium reflects the joint consensus of verifiers: strong agreement indicates stable, valid reasoning steps, while disagreement indicates instability and likely errors. They derive a unique closed-form equilibrium solution that produces consensus scores without additional training. Building on this, they introduce VERDICT (VERification via Disagreement-Informed Coupled Thresholding), a step-wise verification framework that: (1) collects scores from different verifiers for each reasoning step; (2) computes a disagreement-aware consensus score through the closed-form coupled equilibrium; and (3) uses these scores for disagreement-aware filtering and stability-conscious ranking of reasoning steps, all in a training-free, domain-agnostic way.

Result: Across six benchmarks, VERDICT improves the base multimodal LLM’s performance by up to +5.95%. Its performance is competitive with domain-specific critics that require substantial supervised training and task-specific adaptation. These results indicate that explicitly leveraging cross-modal agreement and disagreement among frozen verifiers provides strong verification signals without additional supervision or finetuning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that step-wise verification for multimodal LLMs can be effectively achieved in a training-free and domain-agnostic manner by modeling verification as a coupled scoring problem over multiple verifiers. VERDICT’s disagreement-informed equilibrium scoring makes cross-modal disagreement explicit and operational, enabling more reliable identification of valid versus unstable reasoning steps. This approach narrows the gap to heavily supervised, domain-specific critics while avoiding their labeling and training costs, suggesting a scalable path for robust reasoning verification in multimodal LLMs.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models often generate reasoning chains containing subtle errors that lead to incorrect answers. Current verification approaches have notable limitations. Existing approaches either require expensive labelled supervision with inconsistent cross-task performance or aggregate scores from multiple sources by simple aggregations, missing a key insight: when these scores disagree, that disagreement itself carries important information about whether a reasoning step is truly valid or not. We formalise this as a coupled scoring problem among disparate, frozen verifiers, interpretable as a coordination game with a unique closed-form equilibrium where agreement signals valid steps while disagreement reveals instability. Towards this end, we propose a training-free domain-agnostic step-wise verification approach we call VERDICT: VERification via Disagreement-Informed Coupled Thresholding. To our knowledge, VERDICT is the first training-free verifier that makes the structure of cross-modal disagreement explicit and actionable. It computes consensus scores through a closed-form solution, enabling both disagreement-aware filtering and stability-conscious ranking of reasoning steps. Evaluated across six benchmarks, \method consistently improves over the base model by up to +5.95%, and performs competitively with domain-specific critics that demand extensive supervision, demonstrating that cross-modal agreement provides robust verification signals without task-specific adaptation and Training-Free Verification

</details>


### [115] [FITTER: Vocabulary-Agnostic Cross-Domain Inference on Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10668)
*Jiaxin Pan,Mojtaba Nayyeri,Osama Mohammed,Daniel Hernandez,Rongchuan Zhang,Cheng Cheng,Steffen Staab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FITTER, a fully-inductive model for temporal knowledge graph completion that can generalize across different graphs, vocabularies, and time domains, outperforming existing inductive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing temporal knowledge graph completion methods assume that all entities, relations, and timestamps are known during training. This ties each model to a single graph and vocabulary, limiting scalability and cross-domain use on the Semantic Web where heterogeneous graphs and unseen vocabularies are common.

Method: The authors design FITTER, a fully-inductive structural model that performs temporal link prediction without requiring prior exposure to specific entities, relation names, or exact timestamps. FITTER encodes predicates via their interaction patterns with other predicates and time, using relative temporal orderings instead of absolute times. A message-passing architecture aggregates both local and global temporal context to build vocabulary-agnostic embeddings. They also provide a proof that their temporal encoding is invariant to global time shifts.

Result: On six temporal knowledge graph benchmarks spanning diverse domains, temporal granularities, and time spans, FITTER achieves superior performance in cross-domain, cross-graph transfer tasks compared with inductive baselines. Notably, it does so without retraining on the target graphs.

Conclusion: Vocabulary-agnostic structural learning, as implemented in FITTER, enables effective fully-inductive temporal link prediction across heterogeneous knowledge graphs. This suggests a practical foundation for scalable inference over Semantic Web data where graphs, vocabularies, and time domains differ and evolve over time.

Abstract: Temporal knowledge graphs are central to many uses of the Semantic Web, but existing completion methods assume the entities, relation names, and timestamps to be reasoned about are already known at training time, restricting each model to a single graph and vocabulary.
