We propose FITTER, the first fully-inductive structural model for temporal knowledge graph link prediction that supports cross-domain transfer: the inference graph may contain entirely unseen entities, relation names, and timestamps drawn from a different domain. FITTER represents each predicate by its interaction patterns with others and time through encodings of relative rather than absolute ordering; message-passing fuses local and global temporal context to produce vocabulary-agnostic embeddings. We prove the temporal encoding is time-shift invariant and evaluate FITTER on cross-domain, cross-graph transfer over six temporal knowledge graph benchmarks of diverse domains, granularities, and time spans. FITTER consistently outperforms inductive baselines without retraining, indicating that vocabulary-agnostic structural learning is a viable foundation for inference over the heterogeneous knowledge graphs of the Semantic Web.

</details>


### [116] [REDAgentBench: Executable Red Teaming and Faithful Measurement of LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10669)
*Zixing Chen,Xingyuan Liu,Jie Zhu,Huaixia Dou,Shuo Jiang,Junhui Li,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build an executable, sandboxed benchmark (REDAgentBench) to more faithfully measure and diagnose safety failures of LLM agents under realistic tool-using attacks, revealing large vulnerability rates and showing a simple reminder can significantly reduce harmful behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluations for LLM agents typically compress behavior into a single attack success rate, without clearly separating whether the agent was exposed to a risky situation, actually executed a harmful action, or whether the evaluator could observe evidence of harm. This conflation makes it hard to know how unsafe agents truly are, how attacks exploit tool use and environment interaction, and where to intervene to improve safety. The authors want a more faithful, executable, and diagnostic way to red-team agent systems that use tools and services.

Method: They design REDAgentBench, an executable benchmark framework tailored for tool-using LLM agents. The framework (1) formalizes explicit safety constraints and links them to agent-system vulnerabilities; (2) automatically derives red-teaming attacks from these vulnerabilities; (3) runs these attacks in isolated, sandboxed service environments that mimic real tools; and (4) verifies harmful effects from concrete artifacts like service receipts and final-state changes rather than from prompts alone. They then populate the benchmark with 1,661 adversarial cases across five types of service surfaces, and evaluate six different LLMs integrated into three different agent harnesses. They also construct a diagnostic cohort where they can precisely resolve whether specific actions caused violations and whether the agent had previously acknowledged the related risk or constraint. Finally, they test a simple, training-free policy reminder intervention by replaying cases with an added reminder and comparing violation rates.

Result: Across the 1,661-case benchmark and multiple models and harnesses, the macro-average attack success rate is high—about 65.69%. They find that reported ASR is sensitive to the agent harness used and what evidence is visible to the evaluator, and that simply disclosing evaluation context can itself alter how agents behave during execution. In a focused diagnostic subset where they can ground violations in environment state and specific actions, they observe that nearly 20% of confirmed violations with clear action anchors occur even after the agent explicitly states the relevant safety constraint or risk, highlighting a gap between recognition of danger and safe execution. They further show that adding a training-free, explicit safety policy reminder before execution dramatically reduces the rate of confirmed violations by more than 70 percentage points when they replay the same scenarios.

Conclusion: Executable, environment-grounded benchmarks such as REDAgentBench provide a more accurate and actionable picture of LLM agent safety than single-number ASR metrics that conflate exposure, execution, observability, and adjudication. The study reveals that current tool-using agents are substantially vulnerable under realistic adversarial conditions, that safety evaluations are highly sensitive to harness design and evidence visibility, and that agents often fail at the execution stage despite explicitly recognizing risks. At the same time, simple, non-training interventions like policy reminders can significantly mitigate harmful behavior. Overall, the work argues that future safety assessment and improvement of LLM agents should rely on sandboxed, state-grounded, and diagnostic evaluations that target specific vulnerabilities and intervention points rather than coarse, static metrics.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents combine language-based reasoning with external tools to perform complex tasks. Adversarial inputs can exploit interactions between the agent and its environment, causing the agent to violate safety policies during execution. Yet existing evaluations often reduce agent safety to a single attack success rate (ASR), collapsing exposure, execution, observation, and adjudication and potentially conflating actual violations with evidence visibility. We introduce REDAgentBench, an executable framework for autonomous red-teaming and faithful measurement. It derives attacks from explicit safety constraints and associated agent-system vulnerabilities, runs them in isolated service sandboxes, and verifies harmful effects from service receipts and final-state changes. The benchmark contains 1,661 cases across five service surfaces. Across six models and three agent harnesses, macro-average ASR is 65.69%; reported ASR varies with harness and evidence view, while evaluation-context disclosure changes execution behavior. In a state-grounded diagnostic cohort, almost one in five confirmed violations with resolved action anchors occurs after the agent states the relevant constraint or risk, revealing a Recognition--Execution Gap. Finally, a training-free policy reminder reduces confirmed violations by more than 70 percentage points in matched replay. These findings show that executable evaluation can improve safety measurement and identify actionable intervention points.

</details>


### [117] [Self-Correcting Long-Horizon Search Agents via Tree-Structured Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10676)
*Aijun Yang,Qianxue Guo,Ziyi Huang,Yuxuan Chen,Shiyou Qian,Jian Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReTree is a self-correcting, tree-structured memory for LLM-based search agents that maintains bounded context, preserves evidence provenance, and actively revises earlier reasoning when new evidence contradicts it, yielding substantially better QA performance than standard ReAct with full trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based search agents typically feed the full interaction trajectory back into the model, which makes the context grow without bound, adds noise, and can propagate earlier mistakes. Existing context compression approaches either drop important details or overwrite incorrect facts without fixing later reasoning that depended on them. A better memory mechanism is needed to keep context small, preserve links to sources, and systematically correct prior reasoning when contradictions are found.

Method: The paper introduces ReTree, which represents the agent’s search and reasoning process as an evidence tree. Each node stores (1) a bounded summary of the local reasoning state, (2) linked evidence passages, and (3) a revision history. As new information is retrieved, ReTree checks for conflicts with earlier claims. On detecting a contradiction, it backtracks to the node where the claim originated, replaces outdated evidence, regenerates the affected summaries, prunes inconsistent branches of the tree, and then continues the search from the repaired node. This yields a bounded per-step context that still retains explicit evidence provenance.

Result: On four public QA and search benchmarks, ReTree consistently outperforms a Full-Trajectory ReAct baseline. It improves answer accuracy by as much as 25.6 percentage points while using a smaller reasoning context; the average maximum per-step context length for Full-Trajectory ReAct is about 1.27–1.51 times larger than that of ReTree. This demonstrates both effectiveness (higher accuracy) and efficiency (more compact context) of the proposed memory structure.

Conclusion: ReTree provides an effective self-correcting memory abstraction for long-horizon LLM-based search agents. By structuring reasoning as an evidence tree with bounded summaries and explicit provenance, and by actively revising earlier steps when conflicts arise, it controls context growth, maintains traceability to sources, and significantly boosts QA performance compared with full-trajectory ReAct baselines.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based search agents answer questions through multi-step interactions with external environments. However, providing complete execution trajectories to the LLM causes unbounded context growth and introduces noise. Existing compression methods reduce context at the cost of important details and often replace erroneous facts without repairing downstream reasoning derived from them. To address this problem, we propose ReTree, a self-correcting tree-structured memory mechanism for search agents. ReTree constructs a bounded per-step reasoning context while preserving source-linked evidence. It models search as an evidence tree whose nodes store bounded summaries, evidence, and revision histories. When newly retrieved evidence contradicts an earlier claim, ReTree traces back to the node where the claim was introduced, replaces outdated evidence, regenerates summaries, prunes affected branches, and resumes search. Source-grounded evidence provenance supports reliable conflict localization and keeps final claims traceable to retrieved passages. Experiments on four public question-answering and search benchmarks show that ReTree consistently outperforms Full-Trajectory ReAct, improving answer accuracy by up to 25.6 percentage points (pp); the average maximum per-step reasoning context of Full-Trajectory ReAct is $1.27$--$1.51\times$ that of ReTree. These results establish ReTree as an effective self-correcting memory abstraction for long-horizon search.

</details>


### [118] [Ex-Omni-2D: Expressive Omni-Modal Dialogue Models with Native Visual Presence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10720)
*Haoyu Zhang,Zhipeng Li,Xiaoying Tang,Tianshu Yu,Yiwen Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ex-Omni-2D is an omni-modal dialogue system that, from multimodal queries plus reference image/audio, generates coordinated text, personalized speech, and avatar video in real time using a visual thought plan and a streaming video student model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing omni-modal dialogue models can process multimodal inputs and synthesize speech but their responses are visually disembodied, lacking a synchronized, personalized avatar video response. Training a fully joint query–text–speech–video system usually requires large-scale aligned supervision, which is hard to obtain and inefficient. The authors aim to create an integrated, visually embodied dialogue agent that can respond in text, personalized voice, and aligned avatar video while remaining data- and compute-efficient.

Method: The paper proposes Ex-Omni-2D, which takes a multimodal query, a reference image, and reference audio. It first predicts a structured Visual Thought Plan (VTP) that encodes scene, emotion, and motion. Based on the VTP, it produces response text and multi-codebook speech units that act as a shared acoustic-temporal interface. These units are decoded into speech and temporally aligned with video frames, allowing separate learning from heterogeneous speech, dialogue, and avatar-video datasets instead of requiring fully aligned four-way supervision. A high-quality full-sequence Video Generator is trained as a Teacher model. Then a more efficient few-step, block-causal Streaming Student model with Prefix Streaming is distilled from the Teacher. Prefix Streaming maintains a clean latent representation across consecutive video chunks to limit quality degradation over time, enabling efficient incremental video synthesis.

Result: The distilled Streaming Student video generator, combined with the full Ex-Omni-2D pipeline, supports four-step inference and runs across four GPUs with an end-to-end real-time factor (RTF) of 1.293 at 400×720 or 720×400 resolution. This demonstrates that the system can generate coordinated text, personalized speech, and reference-conditioned video close to real time while maintaining acceptable visual quality and temporal consistency.

Conclusion: Ex-Omni-2D shows that a structured visual thought plan plus a shared multi-codebook speech-unit interface can effectively couple dialogue, TTS, and avatar video generation without requiring fully joint multimodal supervision. Through teacher–student distillation and a block-causal streaming design with Prefix Streaming, the system achieves a practical trade-off between video quality and real-time efficiency, enabling visually embodied omni-modal dialogue agents suitable for interactive applications.

Abstract: Omni-modal dialogue models can understand multimodal inputs and synthesize spoken replies, yet their responses remain visually disembodied. We introduce \textbf{Ex-Omni-2D}, an omni-modal dialogue framework that generates a coordinated response comprising text, personalized speech, and reference-conditioned video. Given a multimodal query, reference image, and reference audio, the model predicts a structured \textit{Visual Thought Plan} (VTP) describing scene, emotion, and motion, followed by response text and native multi-codebook speech units. These units form a shared acoustic-temporal interface: they are decoded into speech and aligned online with video frames. This interface enables the response and avatar pathways to be learned from heterogeneous speech, dialogue, and avatar-video data, avoiding the need for large-scale query--text--speech--video supervision. A full-sequence Video Generator serves as the primary Teacher. For efficient incremental generation, we further distill it into a few-step block-causal \emph{Streaming Student} whose Prefix Streaming mechanism carries a clean latent across consecutive chunks to reduce cumulative late-chunk degradation. With four-step inference, the complete four-GPU pipeline achieves an end-to-end RTF of 1.293 at $400\times720$/$720\times400$, providing a practical quality--efficiency operating point.

</details>


### [119] [Tree-of-Ideas: Automated Research Ideation via Cross-Trajectory Reasoning over Scholarly Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10740)
*Xun Li,Yiying Yang,Pengtao Li,Xiao Yao,Suyu Liu,Xiaoyang Ye,Ziyu Lu,Yuan Yao,Yangning Li,Yinghui Li,Wenhao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Tree-of-Ideas, a framework that reconstructs how research ideas evolve and interact across papers to automatically generate grounded, novel research ideas, achieving performance close to human-curated references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research ideation often relies on a deep, dynamic understanding of how problems, methods, and solutions evolve in the literature. Existing automatic methods are limited: they either treat the entire prior literature as unstructured text, missing the temporal and causal evolution of ideas, or focus narrowly on linear citation chains, missing interactions between different research paths. There is a need for a method that captures branching, interacting scholarly trajectories to better support automatic, high-quality research ideation.

Method: The authors propose Tree-of-Ideas (ToI), a two-stage framework. First, EvoTrace reconstructs branching scholarly trajectories from citation graphs, explicitly tracking how methods evolve, which problems get resolved, and where gaps remain. This creates structured, tree-like evolutionary paths of ideas. Second, EvoAgent reasons across these trajectories, identifying where different paths converge on similar problems or offer complementary solutions. Using this cross-path reasoning, EvoAgent generates new research ideas that are both novel and grounded in the literature.

Result: Evaluated on six AI research topics, ToI outperforms existing automatic baselines for research ideation. On a 10-point evaluation scale, ToI achieves an average score of 6.27, compared to 5.36 for the strongest baseline. It shows particularly strong performance in Novelty (6.36) and Groundedness (7.00). Its overall score is close to that obtained when humans reference papers directly (6.29), indicating that ToI approximates human-level use of the literature for ideation.

Conclusion: Modeling research as interacting evolutionary trajectories, rather than as flat text or isolated citation chains, significantly improves automatic research ideation. Tree-of-Ideas demonstrates that reconstructing and reasoning over branching scholarly paths enables the generation of research ideas that are both novel and well-grounded in existing work, approaching the quality of human literature-based ideation.

Abstract: Effective research ideation requires moving beyond a static understanding of prior work to trace how research problems and solutions evolve across the literature. Existing methods either treat papers as unstructured context or model scholarly evolution as isolated citation chains, overlooking interactions among research trajectories. We propose Tree-of-Ideas (ToI), a two-stage framework. EvoTrace reconstructs branching scholarly trajectories from citations, tracking evolving methods, resolved problems, and gaps. EvoAgent then reasons across trajectories to identify convergent problems and complementary solutions, generating grounded research ideas. Across six AI research topics, ToI achieves the highest score among automatic methods (6.27 vs. 5.36 for the strongest baseline on a 10-point scale), with strong Novelty (6.36) and Groundedness (7.00). Also, its score approaches that of human-paper references (6.29), demonstrating the value of cross-path evolutionary reasoning.

</details>


### [120] [Compositional Benchmark Synthesis for Hierarchical Human Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10765)
*Farnaz Soleimani,Abdelghani Chibani,Yacine Amirat,Ghazaleh Khodabandelou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They construct a synthetic yet realistic hierarchical benchmark for human behavior understanding from a flat action dataset, ensuring that evaluation is not circularly tied to generation rules, and show it poses a consistent compositional generalization challenge across multiple model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing datasets either have only isolated, atomic action labels or shallow, domain-specific hierarchies, which are inadequate for studying recognition of human behavior across multiple semantic levels (actions to intentions). There is a need for a scalable way to generate rich hierarchical benchmarks while retaining realism and avoiding circular supervision, so models are tested on genuine reasoning about intentions rather than memorizing generation rules.

Method: Starting from a flat, single-label action corpus with pre-extracted features, they synthesize multi-level episodes (actions, activities, low-level intentions, high-level intentions). A transition model with a subject-consistency constraint assembles action clips into episodes, and a coverage-aware sampler balances subject usage (reducing Gini coefficient). To avoid circular supervision, they enforce disjointness between the rules for sequence generation and the first-order logic rules used for evaluation. They instantiate this framework to produce 15,002 episodes and design several baselines (including a graph-aware model and logic-free/control baselines) to probe the benchmark’s properties rather than push SOTA recognition performance.

Result: The generated benchmark exhibits a robust compositional held-out gap of 0.13–0.17 macro-F1 across all tested model families, including a strong graph-aware model, suggesting a structural difficulty in compositional generalization. A logic-free baseline still surpasses the intrinsic data rate for violating held-out semantic rules, indicating that statistical signals exist but are not sufficient to close the gap. An order-destroying control leaves macro-F1 within seed variation, supporting that models are not merely exploiting superficial sequence order artifacts and that the generator is consistent with its design goals.

Conclusion: The proposed framework can turn flat, atomic action datasets into realistic multi-level hierarchical intention benchmarks while mitigating circular supervision. The resulting benchmark is structurally challenging, particularly for compositional generalization, as evidenced by persistent performance gaps across diverse baselines. By releasing the ontology, transition model, and generator, they enable regeneration and extension of the benchmark, providing a reusable testbed for future research on hierarchical human behavior and intention understanding.

Abstract: Recognizing human behavior across levels of abstraction, from atomic actions to long-horizon intentions, requires data annotated along a semantic hierarchy. Large corpora provide isolated, atomically labeled clips without temporal composition, whereas recorded composite-activity corpora offer shallow, domain-narrow, fixedhierarchies. A benchmark-generation and evaluation frameworkis proposed that synthesizes a four-level hierarchical-intention benchmark, spanning actions, activities, low-level intentions (LLIs), and high-level intentions (HLIs), from a flat single-label action corpus while retaining real pre-extracted features at the action level. Episodes are assembled by a transition model under a subject-consistency constraint, and a coverage-aware sampler reduces the subject usage Gini from 0.566 to 0.248. Synthesizing such a benchmark raises a circular-supervision risk that recorded datasets avoid: if the rules generating the episodes also govern the evaluation, models can succeed by recovering the generator rather than through genuine reasoning. Validity is addressed by design, holding sequence-generation rules disjoint from the first-order-logic rules used at evaluation. The instantiation yields 15,002 episodes. Four reference baselines from different model families characterize difficulty, not as recognition methods. A compositional held-out gap of 0.13 to 0.17 macro-F1 appears across all baselines, including a graph-aware model that recognizes best yet does not close the gap, indicating a structural property of the benchmark rather than a model artifact. A logic-free baseline still violates the held-out semantic rules above their intrinsic data rate, and the order-destroying control changes macro-F1 within seed variation, serving as a generator-consistency check. Theontology, transition model, and generator are released so the benchmark can beregenerated and extended.

</details>


### [121] [Rule of Thumb: Explaining Artificial Intelligence Systems using Partial Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10766)
*Kaivalya Rawal,Daria Onitiu,Brent Mittelstadt,Sandra Wachter,Chris Russell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Rule of Thumb (RoT), a fast, model-agnostic XAI method that highlights the most relevant features behind an AI system’s decision for a specific datapoint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing XAI methods can be slow, model-dependent, and often do not align well with emerging regulatory requirements or practical needs such as zero-shot LLM explanation and model auditing without access to internals. There is a need for a general, intuitive, and efficient approach that can work across models and use cases, including scientific discovery, while being compliant with AI regulations.

Method: Formulate explanations as identifying the minimal set of most relevant features that best predict the behaviour of an AI system on a given datapoint, using only partial information. RoT is designed to be model-agnostic and to work by probing the system’s input–output behaviour rather than requiring access to internal parameters. It is then instantiated and evaluated in several settings, including zero-shot LLM classification, black-box auditing, and scientific analysis workflows, with visualisations tailored for practitioners.

Result: RoT can successfully generate explanations in zero-shot LLM classification, in auditing opaque systems without model access, and in aiding scientific discovery tasks. Across experiments, RoT achieves substantial speed improvements over alternative XAI approaches while remaining model-agnostic and requiring only black-box access. It also produces explanations that can be presented in practitioner-friendly visual forms.

Conclusion: Rule of Thumb offers a general, efficient, and practical XAI framework that highlights key predictive features for individual decisions in a model-agnostic, black-box manner. It supports multiple use cases (LLM zero-shot explanation, model auditing, scientific discovery), aligns with regulatory requirements, and runs significantly faster than common alternatives, making it a promising tool for real-world explainability workflows.

Abstract: Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) seeks to explain how an Artificial Intelligence (AI) system arrived at a particular decision. We propose ''Rule of Thumb'' (RoT) explanations, a new approach to XAI based upon a novel formulation that identifies the most relevant features for predicting the behaviour of an AI system, for a particular datapoint. We show how RoT is well-suited to enable XAI in: (a) zero-shot classification using large language models (LLMs), (b) auditing of opaque AI systems without model access, and (c) the use of AI in scientific discovery. Additionally, RoT meets specific requirements from leading AI regulations, provides a familiar interface and visualisations for XAI practitioners, is model-agnostic, and is substantially faster than alternatives.
  Code available at: https://github.com/KaiRawal/Rule-of-Thumb-Explaining-Artificial-Intelligence-Systems-using-Partial-Information

</details>


### [122] [SkillLens: Visual Skill Cards for Retrieval-Augmented GUI Action Prediction and On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10775)
*Zhou Liu,Ligang Huang,Zeli Su,Zewei Pan,Zhaoyang Han,Xing Chen,Yuanfeng Song,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Visual Skill Cards (VSCs), a compact visual procedural memory that makes GUI agents more reliable by encoding when a skill applies, what to look at, and how to verify progress, significantly boosting performance on web interaction benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current computer-using agents can see rich GUIs but lack good procedural visual memory: they recognize buttons and fields but fail to understand which workflow they are in, what the next important control is, or how to confirm that progress has been made. Long, noisy interaction traces are hard to use directly, while text-only skills miss critical visual state information. There is a need for a representation that connects reusable procedures to the visual conditions under which they should be executed and verified.

Method: The authors introduce Visual Skill Cards (VSCs), a state-conditioned memory representation that ties procedures to visual applicability cues, evidence, and verification signals. They propose SkillLens, which (1) converts heterogeneous past interaction traces into VSCs via a Trace-to-Visual-Skill-Card process, and (2) at inference retrieves the relevant cards and expands only the minimal visual evidence required by a fixed VLM executor to predict grounded GUI actions. They also introduce CardDistill, which uses VSC evidence as privileged context to train a student model that can act without online card retrieval.

Result: On the Multimodal-Mind2Web and WebLINX-BrowserGym benchmarks, augmenting a frozen GPT-5.4-mini executor with SkillLens and VSCs improves Step Success Rate by +11.6 points and Overall score by +2.9 points, respectively. Using CardDistill to train a smaller Qwen3-VL-2B student further yields gains of +12.0 points in Step SR and +3.2 points in Overall, demonstrating that the VSC representation transfers into more capable standalone agents.

Conclusion: Encoding procedural knowledge as Visual Skill Cards—linking skills to visual applicability, evidence, and verification—provides an effective visual procedural memory for GUI agents. This architecture enables better use of interaction experience, improves action prediction for frozen VLM executors, and can be distilled into standalone models, leading to consistent performance improvements on challenging web interaction tasks.

Abstract: Computer-using agents can perceive rich software interfaces, yet their decisions often lack visual procedural memory: they may recognize individual controls without identifying which familiar workflow is active, which control matters next, or what evidence would confirm progress. Raw interaction traces preserve such information but are long and noisy to condition on, whereas text-only skills often omit the visual state that makes a procedure applicable. We introduce Visual Skill Cards (VSCs), a state-conditioned memory representation that binds reusable procedures with applicability cues, visual evidence, and verification signals. SkillLens constructs VSCs from heterogeneous interaction experience through Trace-to-Visual-Skill-Card and, at inference time, retrieves relevant cards and selectively expands only the evidence needed by a fixed visual-language model executor for grounded GUI action prediction. The same representation also supports CardDistill, which uses VSC evidence as privileged teacher context to train a student that acts without runtime card retrieval. Across Multimodal-Mind2Web and WebLINX-BrowserGym, SkillLens improves the frozen GPT-5.4-mini executor by +11.6 points in Step SR and +2.9 points in Overall, respectively; CardDistill further improves the corresponding student-only Qwen3-VL-2B metrics by +12.0 and +3.2 points.

</details>


### [123] [ChemWorld: Programmable Chemical Worlds for Controlled and Replayable Agent Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10792)
*Jiangjie Qiu,Yijun Li,Xiaonan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChemWorld is a programmable, replayable virtual chemical experimentation environment that lets agents interact with systematically varied, partially hidden chemical laws while keeping the public task interface fixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous chemistry needs environments where agents can iteratively act, observe, and adapt. Physical labs, while providing real data, are expensive, slow, and hard to control for tightly matched interventions. Existing digital environments typically use fixed underlying experimental worlds, limiting the ability to study how agents behave under systematically varied but controlled chemistries. There is a need for a controlled, programmable, and auditable environment that can simulate different chemical worlds while exposing a stable experimental interface to learning agents.

Method: The authors design ChemWorld as a programmable chemical environment composed of reusable process and observation modules that are compiled into fully executable chemical worlds. They architect a separation between (1) the public experimental contract—what agents can see and do—and (2) evaluator-owned private chemical and material laws that govern the underlying world. They implement transactional execution that logs all operations, failures, resource use, and state changes to enable exact replay and auditing of environment–agent interactions. They validate ChemWorld through a comprehensive test suite: full-census qualification of a reference registry, 52 automatically generated world compositions, and various module, interface, compilation, and invalid-action tests. They further run deterministic case studies demonstrating lifecycle semantics, failure recovery, and deterministic replay, as well as world-fork experiments where child worlds modify only a single private law relative to a parent world while keeping public conditions identical. Finally, they evaluate agent interaction by having an independent agent successfully complete a full experimental lifecycle in a non-reference world via the public interface.

Result: ChemWorld successfully supports the construction of multiple executable chemical worlds from modular components while enforcing a strict separation between public interface and private world laws. The transactional execution and logging system enables exact replay and auditing of entire experiment trajectories, including operations, failures, and state transitions. Testing across a reference registry, 52 generated world compositions, and a range of module and interface checks shows that the system is robust and qualifies as a consistent substrate. The deterministic experimental cases confirm that ChemWorld can support shared lifecycle semantics, recover from failures, and reproduce runs exactly. The parent–child fork experiments show that changing a single hidden law while holding public conditions fixed isolates the causal impact of that law on outcomes. The independent agent run demonstrates that external agents can operate successfully in ChemWorld using only the public experimental contract.

Conclusion: ChemWorld provides a controlled, programmable, and replayable environment for autonomous chemical experimentation, allowing researchers to systematically vary underlying chemical laws and world compositions while maintaining a stable public interface for agents. This design enables rigorous comparison of agent behavior and experimental outcomes under matched conditions with hidden variations, and supports exact replay and auditing of full experimental trajectories. As such, ChemWorld is positioned as a complementary substrate to physical laboratory experimentation, useful both for studying experimentation itself and for calibrating or interpreting results obtained in real labs within the domain covered by its component models.

Abstract: Autonomous chemistry increasingly depends on environments in which agents can repeatedly act, observe, and adapt.Physical laboratories provide essential real-material evidence but are costly to repeat and difficult to use for tightly matched interventions, whereas most digital environments keep the underlying experimental world largely fixed. We introduce ChemWorld, a programmable chemical environment in which reusable process and observation components are compiled into executable worlds. ChemWorld separates the public experimental contract available to an agent from evaluator-owned chemical and material laws. Researchers can therefore vary world composition and operating conditions, or change a single hidden law while holding the public task and interaction conditions fixed. Transactional execution records operations, failures, resource changes, and state transitions, allowing complete environment-action trajectories to be replayed exactly and audited. Full-census qualification covered the reference registry, 52 generated compositions, and module, interface, compilation, and invalid-action tests. Eight deterministic experimental cases demonstrated shared lifecycle semantics, failure recovery, and exact replay, while six parent-child world-fork pairs isolated the effects of single private-law interventions under matched public conditions. An independent agent also completed a full lifecycle in a non-reference world through the same public interface. Within the declared component and model domain, ChemWorld provides a controlled and replayable substrate for studying experimentation across systematically varied chemical worlds, complementary to physical-laboratory evidence and calibration.

</details>


### [124] [EvoMem: Memory-Augmented Evolution for Code Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10795)
*Viktor Volkov,Valentin Khrulkov,Andrey V. Galichin,Danil Sivtsov,Nikita Glazkov,Olga Volkova,Konstantin Pchelin,Iaroslav Bespalov,Dmitry V. Dylov,Petr Anokhin,Ivan Oseledets*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes EvoMem, a persistent memory system that stores and reuses successful mutation strategies in LLM-based evolutionary program search to reduce redundant exploration and improve performance across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-driven evolutionary code/search frameworks repeatedly rediscover similar mutation strategies because they do not retain or reuse knowledge about which mutations worked well in past runs or tasks. This wastes computation, limits cross-run and cross-task learning, and misses opportunities to transfer useful mutation patterns across related domains.

Method: EvoMem augments evolutionary program search with a persistent memory. After each evolutionary run, it converts successful mutation events into structured, task-aware advice, storing them with provenance information. In later runs, given the current task description and program context, EvoMem retrieves a small, relevant subset of these stored instructions to guide the mutation operators. The system is evaluated on several benchmarks including geometric optimization, multi-hop QA, and GPU kernel optimization.

Result: Across the evaluated domains, EvoMem yields positive average improvements either in final target metrics or in search speed in most, though not all, experimental settings. The results also show that the effectiveness of this memory-based guidance varies across different tasks and benchmarks.

Conclusion: Persistent memory of successful mutations can meaningfully reduce redundant exploration in LLM-driven evolutionary search and enable better reuse and adaptation of effective strategies, though benefits are task-dependent. EvoMem demonstrates that encoding and retrieving structured mutation advice across runs is a promising direction for improving evolutionary program search with LLMs.

Abstract: Successful mutation strategies in evolutionary code search may contain reusable knowledge that is useful beyond a single run, and in some cases may transfer across related tasks and domains. However, existing LLM-driven evolutionary frameworks largely discard such knowledge, repeatedly rediscovering similar ideas and limiting opportunities for cross-run and cross-task learning. We introduce EvoMem, a persistent memory architecture for LLM-based evolutionary program search that captures and reuses candidate mutation knowledge. EvoMem converts successful mutation events into structured, task-aware advice for future runs. It operates in two phases: after each run, it extracts and stores promising ideas with provenance, and during subsequent evolution, it retrieves a small set of relevant instructions based on the current task and program context to guide mutation. Across geometric optimization, multi-hop question answering, GPU kernel optimization, and related benchmarks, our experiments show positive average improvements in target metrics or search speed for most evaluated settings, while also revealing variability across tasks. Overall, EvoMem provides evidence that persistent memory can reduce some redundant exploration and improve the reuse and adaptation of successful strategies in LLM-driven evolutionary search.

</details>


### [125] [Hypothesis Frontier: Verifier Guided LLM and Symbolic Search for First-Order Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10843)
*Serafim Batzoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents Hypothesis Frontier, a verifier-guided neurosymbolic framework that uses LLMs plus exact logical verification and simplification to synthesize first-order formulas that correctly classify labeled objects across multiple relational structures, solving more problems and compressing the resulting formulas compared to naive repeated LLM prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-order concept synthesis requires finding quantified first-order formulas that correctly classify examples across several finite relational structures. While each candidate formula can be evaluated exactly, the search space is enormous, and raw LLM-generated formulas are often close but not fully correct. There is a need for a method that systematically exploits the semantic promise of LLM outputs while guaranteeing correctness via symbolic verification, thereby improving success rates and formula quality over simple prompt-based generation.

Method: The authors propose Hypothesis Frontier, a verifier-guided neurosymbolic loop. An LLM generates candidate first-order formulas. A symbolic verifier evaluates each candidate on all training objects across the given relational structures. The system maintains the strongest currently verified hypothesis and identifies its remaining misclassified examples. These errors are fed back to guide subsequent LLM generations, narrowing the search. Symbolic post-processing then repairs syntactic or semantic invalidities while staying close to the LLM hypothesis and simplifies formulas that already fit the training data without changing any of their predictions. After selecting final formulas that are consistent with the training data, an exact simplification pass further shortens many of them while preserving all training predictions.

Result: Under controlled experimental conditions with matched models, problem sets, and budgets for the number of LLM rounds, Hypothesis Frontier solves significantly more first-order concept synthesis problems than a baseline that repeatedly calls the LLM with the original prompt and no verifier-guided feedback. Moreover, many of the formulas that perfectly fit the training data can be exactly simplified to shorter equivalents without losing any training accuracy.

Conclusion: Verifier-guided neurosymbolic reasoning can substantially improve first-order concept synthesis compared to naive LLM prompting. By coupling LLM-based hypothesis generation with exact symbolic verification, repair, and simplification, Hypothesis Frontier both increases the number of induction tasks solved and compresses many resulting formulas, showing that exact logical reasoning is a useful complement to LLMs for structured concept induction.

Abstract: First-order concept synthesis asks a system to infer one formula that classifies labeled objects consistently across several finite relational structures. Every candidate can be evaluated exactly, but quantified first-order formulas form a vast search space, and LLM outputs are often semantically promising without being fully correct. We introduce Hypothesis Frontier, a verifier-guided neurosymbolic framework that evaluates each LLM formula on every training object, retains the strongest verified hypothesis across rounds, and uses its remaining errors to guide subsequent generation. Symbolic processing repairs invalid formulas while remaining anchored to the LLM-generated hypothesis, and simplifies train-valid formulas without changing any training prediction. Under matched models, problem sets, and LLM-round budgets, Hypothesis Frontier solves substantially more problems than repeated original-prompt generation. After the final formulas are selected, exact simplification shortens many train-valid formulas while preserving every training prediction. Exact symbolic reasoning therefore helps both to solve more induction problems and to compress many of the resulting formulas.

</details>


### [126] [Enhanced Filtering Algorithms for the Euclidean Traveling Salesperson Problem and its variants in Constraint Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10881)
*Alessandro Bertagnon,Marco Gavanelli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They improve solving Euclidean TSP in CP by using geometry instead of only a distance matrix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CP approaches to Euclidean TSP build a full distance matrix and then ignore the geometric structure of points, potentially missing strong pruning opportunities. Many real applications in routing, logistics, and intelligent transportation involve Euclidean TSP or its variants, so better propagation could bring computational gains.

Method: They design new filtering (propagation) algorithms for the TSP constraints, implemented in Constraint Logic Programming, that explicitly exploit geometric information from the coordinates (e.g., Euclidean distances, spatial relations) rather than just a generic distance matrix. They then generalize the same geometric-filtering methodology to Euclidean variants such as the Euclidean Generalized TSP (EGTSP).

Result: The proposed geometric filtering algorithms achieve stronger constraint propagation than existing CP approaches and, according to experiments, provide computational advantages (e.g., faster solving, better pruning) on benchmark problems for Euclidean TSP and EGTSP.

Conclusion: Using the underlying Euclidean geometry in CP models of TSP and its variants leads to more powerful filtering and improved computational performance compared to treating the problem as a generic TSP on a precomputed distance matrix. The approach is effective and extensible to other Euclidean routing problems like EGTSP.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) is one of the best-known problems in computer science and arises in many engineering applications, such as smart vehicles and intelligent transportation systems. In the "Euclidean" case, each node is defined by its coordinates in the plane and distances are computed using the Euclidean metric. In the Constraint Programming (CP) literature, the Euclidean TSP is typically addressed by computing the full distance matrix and treating it as a general case; however this approach ignores the geometric information carried by the points' coordinates. In this work, we propose new filtering algorithms, implemented in Constraint Logic Programming (CLP), that exploit such geometric information to achieve stronger constraint propagation than existing approaches. Moreover, we show how this methodology can be extended to other Euclidean variants of the TSP, including the Euclidean Generalized Traveling Salesperson Problem (EGTSP), which is relevant in practical routing and logistics applications. Experimental results demonstrate the computational advantages of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [127] [ComBodied Agents: a New Paradigm of Human-Centric Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10915)
*Qianggang Ding,Xingyao Wang,Rui Feng,Zhibin Wang,Feixiang Wang,Kelong Mao,Hao Sun,Zhiyao Luo,Jiankai Tang,Lei Li,Jiadong Guo,Minheng Ni,Weicong Lin,Chenxi Yang,Hongxiang Gao,Zhenghua Chen,Yang Bai,Min Wu,Jun Cheng,Huazhu Fu,Dacheng Tao,Bang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes “Combodied Agents,” a new paradigm for AI systems that focus on modeling and supporting a person’s evolving state and agency over time, integrating software, sensors, robots, and human services into a closed-loop system aimed at sustained human benefit rather than just task completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Agentic AI has a structural gap: digital agents mainly change software states, and embodied agents mainly act on the physical world, but neither treats the human’s evolving mental, physical, and social state—and their agency—as the main object of modeling and support. For example, when an older adult misses medication, existing systems can only remind or deliver pills, without understanding whether the person forgot, is confused, experiencing side effects, or intentionally refusing, nor what proportionate, ethical support is appropriate. There is also fragmentation across personal assistants, health agents, and AI companions, lacking a unified, human-centered framework that respects consent, safety, and user control over time.

Method: The authors define and architect “Combodied Agents” as systems that (1) use event-based multimodal perception (from software logs, sensors, wearables, robots, and human feedback) to reconstruct meaningful personal events; (2) maintain a longitudinal, correctable memory that provides temporal context on the person; (3) build Personal World Models that estimate future personal states and outcomes under different decisions and interventions; and (4) apply an admissible intervention policy that chooses proportionate, reversible support under conditions of consent, uncertainty, safety, and user control. This is implemented as a closed loop where feedback from the person and environment continuously updates the models, using purpose-bounded, uncertainty-aware, user-correctable representations instead of exhaustive digital twins. They also organize the design space and outline metrics and infrastructure needs.

Result: Conceptually, the paper unifies fragmented capabilities across personal assistants, health and wellbeing agents, AI companions, and adaptive human–AI systems into a single closed-loop framework centered on human-state trajectories. It proposes a systematic organization of the design space by human-state targets, relational contexts, and agent roles, and it outlines requirements for scenario-centered evaluation, agency-preservation metrics, benchmarks, and edge-native personal models. The main outcome is a structured paradigm and research agenda, rather than empirical performance numbers.

Conclusion: Combodied Agents reorient Agentic AI from focusing on external task completion to prioritizing sustained human benefit by modeling, predicting, and supporting individuals’ evolving states and agency. By using multimodal sensing, correctable personal memory, and Personal World Models within an admissible, ethics-aware intervention policy, they aim to deliver proportionate, consent-respecting support without building exhaustive digital twins. The framework offers a roadmap for designing, evaluating, and governing human-centered agents that integrate digital, physical, and human service channels around the person’s long-term wellbeing.

Abstract: After an older adult misses a medication dose, a software agent can send another reminder and an embodied agent can bring the medication. Yet neither explains whether the person forgot, is confused, has side effects, or deliberately refused, nor what support is appropriate. This reveals a structural gap in Agentic AI: Digital Agents primarily transform software states, while Embodied Agents transform physical states; neither makes a person's evolving state and agency the primary object of modeling, intervention, and evaluation. We introduce Combodied Agents, a human-centered paradigm that perceives, models, predicts, and supports individual human-state trajectories over time, using software tools, sensors, wearables, robots, and human services as action channels rather than end goals. We unify fragmented capabilities across personal assistants, health agents, AI companions, and adaptive human--AI systems into a closed loop: event-based multimodal perception reconstructs meaningful personal events; longitudinal, correctable memory provides temporal context; Personal World Models estimate future personal states and outcomes under alternative decisions and interventions; and an admissible intervention policy selects proportionate support under consent, uncertainty, safety, reversibility, and user control. Feedback from the person and environment updates the loop. Rather than requiring an exhaustive Human Digital Twin, the framework uses purpose-bounded, uncertainty-aware, user-correctable representations. We organize the design space by human-state targets, relational contexts, and agent roles, and propose scenario-centered evaluation, agency-preservation metrics, benchmark requirements, edge-native personal models, and governance directions. Combodied Agents shift Agentic AI from external task completion toward sustained human benefit.

</details>


### [128] [IO Factory: Simulating AI-Enabled Influence Campaigns at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10920)
*Lukasz Olejnik,Wenchao Dong,Jonas R. Kunst,Signe Riemer-Sørensen,Tobias Herb,Meeyoung Cha,Daniel Thilo Schroeder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents IO Factory, a simulation framework that models large-scale, AI-enabled information and influence campaigns with full traceability of actions, exposures, and belief changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital manipulation is shifting from single AI-generated messages to coordinated swarms of AI agents that adapt over time and hide within normal social interactions. Existing analyses that focus on individual messages or static datasets miss the end-to-end dynamics of planning, execution, exposure, interpretation, and adaptation that define real campaigns. There is a need for a controlled, scalable environment to study these complex processes, evaluate their effects, and support systematic red-teaming and reproducible research on influence operations.

Method: The authors design and implement IO Factory, a simulated social platform environment populated with up to 100,000 agents. Within this environment, they model different actor roles (e.g., campaign planners, executors, targets), platform actions (posts, interactions), exposure records, and belief variables. The framework connects these elements in a continuous loop: campaigns are planned under specified objectives and constraints, actions occur on the simulated platform, exposure paths are logged, structured model-based evaluations interpret effects, and the simulated population’s beliefs are updated accordingly. The system records all key components—actors, objectives, constraints, exposure paths, and measurement rules—to ensure full traceability.

Result: Experiments show that IO Factory can run large-scale campaign timelines involving up to 100,000 agents while maintaining detailed logs. The framework produces inspectable evidence that specific campaigns led to particular exposures and measurable shifts in configured belief variables among the simulated population. Performance and data outputs demonstrate that the system is scalable and that its traceability supports systematic analysis of how campaigns propagate and affect beliefs.

Conclusion: IO Factory provides a practical, scalable architecture for simulating and analyzing coordinated information and influence operations in a controlled environment. By integrating planning, actions, exposure tracking, evaluation, and adaptation into a single traceable pipeline, it enables reproducible experiments and structured red-team analysis of AI-driven campaigns. This offers a foundation for more rigorous study and mitigation of complex, swarm-based digital manipulation strategies.

Abstract: We introduce IO Factory, an AI-driven framework for simulating information and influence campaigns as fully integrated, traceable processes. The threat of digital manipulation now extends beyond persuasive text from individual language models to AI swarms, i.e., persistent groups of coordinated agents that adapt to platform feedback and disguise organized campaigns as ordinary social interaction. Because such campaigns cannot be identified from isolated messages alone, they must be analyzed across a continuous spectrum of planning, platform action, exposure, interpretation, measurement, and adaptation. IO Factory represents this process inside a controlled simulated platform, linking actor roles, platform actions, exposure records, structured model-based evaluations, and configured changes in the simulated population. We implement the architecture and evaluate it across configurations of up to 100,000 agents. The results show that IO Factory executes campaign timelines at scale and produces inspectable evidence of exposure and measured movement in configured belief variables. By recording the actors, objectives, action constraints, exposure paths, and measurement rules used in each run, IO Factory supports reproducible research and red-team analysis of coordinated influence.

</details>


### [129] [ThinkRetrieve: Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning Traces for Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10928)
*Vaibhav Singh,Soumya Suvra Ghosal,Sarvesh Gharat,Soumyabrata Pal,Ramasuri Narayanam,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThinkRetrieve improves large reasoning models at test time by inserting dynamically retrieved, step-by-step solved examples into the model’s ongoing chain-of-thought, leading to significantly higher accuracy than standard test-time scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard test-time scaling for large reasoning models extends chain-of-thought reasoning, but longer traces often become less reliable due to accumulating errors, increased uncertainty, and drift from the question. Existing approaches mainly retrieve facts or whole exemplars before reasoning starts, rather than providing fine-grained guidance during reasoning. The authors aim to make extended test-time reasoning both longer and more accurate by stabilizing and guiding the reasoning process itself.

Method: The authors introduce ThinkRetrieve, a framework that, at each intermediate reasoning step, retrieves similar past problems with step-by-step solutions from an external corpus. These retrieved partial solutions are injected directly into the model’s current reasoning trace as in-context guidance. The system is evaluated on several math and science reasoning benchmarks using multiple models (1.5B–8B parameters), comparing against standard test-time scaling without such step-wise retrieval.

Result: Across GSM-8K, MATH-500, AIME 2025, and SciQ, and for five reasoning models between 1.5B and 8B parameters, ThinkRetrieve consistently outperforms standard test-time scaling. On AIME 2025 in particular, it achieves relative accuracy gains of up to 60%, demonstrating robust improvements in reasoning performance when guided step-wise retrieval is applied.

Conclusion: Dynamically retrieving and injecting solved step-by-step exemplars during chain-of-thought reasoning makes test-time scaling more effective and reliable. Rather than only making reasoning traces longer or retrieving static context up front, guiding each intermediate step with relevant solution patterns yields substantial and consistent accuracy gains across models and benchmarks.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) improve performance by allocating additional inference-time compute to generate extended chain-of-thought reasoning. However, recent studies reveal that sequential test-time scaling often yields diminishing or even negative returns, as longer traces exhibit increased uncertainty, error compounding, and drift from the original problem. We propose ThinkRetrieve, a test-time scaling framework that augments the reasoning traces of LRMs with dynamically retrieved solved examples at each reasoning step. Given an external corpus of problems paired with step-by-step solutions, ThinkRetrieve retrieves relevant exemplars at each intermediate step and injects them directly into the thinking trace, providing the model with guidance on how to reason rather than merely what facts are relevant. Experiments across five reasoning models (1.5B--8B parameters) on GSM-8K, MATH-500, AIME 2025, and SciQ demonstrate that ThinkRetrieve consistently improves accuracy over standard test-time scaling, with relative gains of up to $60\%$ on AIME 2025.

</details>


### [130] [FedCGR: Federated Cross-Domain Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10929)
*Zhuodong Liu,Hugen Lv,Xiangyu Li,Bohan Guo,Peiyu Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes FedCGR, a federated generative cross-domain recommendation framework that uses a stable semantic item language and personalized aggregation to enable privacy-preserving and effective cross-domain recommendation under domain heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-domain recommendation methods rely on overlapping users or shared interaction signals to align item spaces, which is difficult or impossible under federated settings due to data sparsity, unavailability, or privacy constraints. Directly applying generative models with semantic item IDs in federated learning faces limitations: the tokenizer must remain fixed, blocking global sharing of collaborative filtering signals, and naive federated averaging can harm performance when domains are heterogeneous. A new approach is needed to transfer knowledge across domains without sharing raw interactions and while mitigating negative transfer across diverse clients.

Method: The authors model federated cross-domain recommendation as generation over a stable semantic item language, where items are represented as discrete semantic ID (SID) sequences built from public metadata. They introduce FedCGR, which (1) uses a reliability-aware semantic interface to inject local collaborative filtering evidence into the SID-based modeling without changing the global tokenizer, and (2) trains a prototype-personalized generator that performs selective aggregation of shared parameters across clients according to domain relatedness while keeping domain-specific parameters local. This preserves a consistent global item vocabulary but allows adaptive, domain-aware personalization and reduces harmful global averaging under heterogeneity.

Result: On six Amazon cross-domain recommendation scenarios, FedCGR consistently surpasses federated generative baselines and delivers performance comparable to or better than strong sequential and federated CDR methods. The improvements hold under both full-ranking and sampled evaluation protocols, showing its robustness and effectiveness across multiple experimental settings.

Conclusion: FedCGR effectively resolves key challenges in federated cross-domain recommendation by using a stable semantic item language and explicit adaptation mechanisms. It enables privacy-preserving cross-domain knowledge transfer without sharing sensitive interaction data, alleviates negative transfer under heterogeneous domains, and achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance across multiple benchmarks and evaluation schemes.

Abstract: Cross-domain recommendation (CDR) transfers preference knowledge across related domains, but federated deployment makes cross-domain alignment difficult because the behavioral anchors that align item spaces, such as overlapping users and shared interaction signals, are often sparse, unavailable, or privacy-sensitive across clients. To address this tension, we revisit federated CDR as generation over a stable semantic item language. By representing items as discrete semantic ID (SID) sequences derived from public item-side metadata, cross-domain item alignment is induced by a shared vocabulary rather than by exchanging private interactions or aligning domain-specific embeddings. Directly federating SID-based generators, however, introduces two design constraints: the SID tokenizer must remain fixed to preserve cross-client token consistency, which creates a semantic-only bottleneck because local collaborative filtering (CF) signals cannot be globally shared or aligned; meanwhile, standard federated averaging can cause negative transfer under domain heterogeneity. To overcome these constraints, we propose FedCGR, a federated generative CDR framework that keeps the item language stable and makes adaptation explicit. FedCGR injects local CF evidence through a reliability-aware semantic interface and trains a prototype-personalized generator that selectively aggregates shared parameters according to domain relatedness while keeping domain-specific quantities local. Experiments on six Amazon cross-domain scenarios show that FedCGR consistently outperforms federated generative baselines and achieves competitive performance against strong sequential and federated CDR methods under both full-ranking and sampled evaluation protocols.

</details>


### [131] [XCoT-VLA: Executable Chain-of-Thought for Vision-Language-Action Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10976)
*Foundation Model Team,XPeng Inc*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces XCoT-VLA, a vision-language-action model that replaces verbose natural language reasoning with compact executable tokens to achieve real-time, reasoning-aware trajectory planning for autonomous driving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-Language-Action models often rely on long natural-language Chain-of-Thought explanations to connect perception, reasoning, and control. While good for interpretability, such verbose CoT is too slow, open-ended, and inefficient for real-time autonomous driving, where the reasoning representation must be compact, optimizable, and directly actionable. The authors aim to build a reasoning mechanism that preserves semantic structure yet is fast and tightly coupled to trajectory generation.

Method: They propose XCoT-VLA, which uses short, discrete executable CoT tokens (XCoT) instead of natural language rationales. These tokens are learned from automatically constructed Reason-Action supervision, where logged trajectories provide ground-truth actions and scene context supplies the causal semantics. The model predicts an XCoT token sequence that stays in the same multimodal context as trajectory queries, and both are processed via shared self-attention. A deterministic token-function routing mechanism sends XCoT tokens through a Reason FFN and trajectory queries through a Control FFN, enabling flow-matching based trajectory generation. Additionally, they introduce XCoT Policy Optimization (XCPO), a refinement stage that optimizes behavior directly in the executable token space.

Result: On autonomous driving benchmarks, XCoT-VLA substantially improves planning metrics: it reduces longitudinal Average Displacement Error (ADE) from 1.645 to 1.323 on a general-distribution test set, and lateral Final Displacement Error (FDE) from 1.616 to 0.648 in lane-change scenarios. It achieves these gains while using only 2–6 XCoT tokens for reasoning, greatly reducing autoregressive reasoning overhead compared to natural-language CoT.

Conclusion: The study shows that driving-oriented reasoning does not need verbose natural language; instead, it can be encoded as a small number of compact, executable tokens that are directly tied to trajectory generation. XCoT-VLA maintains real-time feasibility while improving control quality, suggesting that executable CoT representations offer an effective, efficient alternative to traditional language-based reasoning in autonomous driving VLA systems.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models can connect scene understanding, semantic reasoning, and trajectory generation for autonomous driving. However, verbose natural-language Chain-of-Thought (CoT) is poorly suited to real-time control because it is open-ended, costly to decode, and difficult to optimize as an action-facing representation. We propose XCoT-VLA, which replaces descriptive rationales with compact executable CoT tokens learned from automatically constructed Reason-Action supervision. Logged trajectories provide action evidence, while scene context supplies causal semantics. The predicted XCoT sequence remains in context and conditions fixed trajectory queries through shared multimodal self-attention. Deterministic token-function routing applies the Reason FFN to XCoT tokens and the Control FFN to trajectory queries for flow-matching trajectory generation. We further introduce XCoT Policy Optimization (XCPO) as an optional refinement extension in the same executable token space. XCoT-VLA reduces longitudinal ADE from 1.645 to 1.323 on a general-distribution set and lateral FDE from 1.616 to 0.648 in lane-change scenarios. By representing driving-oriented reasoning with only 2-6 executable XCoT tokens, our method substantially reduces autoregressive reasoning overhead and remains within the real-time planning budget. These results demonstrate that driving-oriented reasoning can be compact, executable, and directly connected to trajectory generation.

</details>


### [132] [V-FiLLM: Verified Financial LLM Reasoning Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11047)
*Alicia Larsen,Victoire Laurent,Aulia Kharis Rakhamsari,Lara Turgut,Nino Antulov-Fantulin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents V-FiLLM, a framework for automatically generating large-scale, verifiably-correct financial reasoning benchmarks over tables, and shows that current LLMs struggle with deeper and more robust financial reasoning but can be improved with targeted fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks cover many STEM domains but pay relatively little attention to financial reasoning, especially when it involves structured/tabular data. Manual benchmark creation is expensive, error-prone, and hard to scale, and often relies on other models for labeling, inheriting their mistakes. The authors aim to build a scalable, automatically verifiable benchmark that can precisely control reasoning difficulty and expose specific weaknesses of LLMs in financial question answering over tables.

Method: They propose V-FiLLM, which constructs executable computation trees grounded in real financial tables. Each tree encodes a sequence of symbolic operations involving financial concepts. These trees are evaluated symbolically to produce exact ground-truth answers, and then automatically rendered into natural-language questions, ensuring correctness by construction and removing LLMs from the labeling loop. The framework allows independent control over four difficulty dimensions: (1) computation depth (number of reasoning steps), (2) expression breadth (branching/complexity of the computation tree), (3) financial concept complexity, and (4) context size (amount of tabular information). They then evaluate various open-source LLMs on these generated benchmarks and also apply lightweight LoRA fine-tuning using verified chain-of-thought traces to assess whether targeted adaptation improves performance.

Result: Open-source LLMs show substantial performance degradation as tasks become more complex: accuracy drops by up to 51 percentage points as computation depth increases and by up to 47 percentage points when numerical values are adversarially perturbed, indicating a lack of robustness and compositional generalization in financial reasoning over tables. After applying LoRA-based fine-tuning on verified chain-of-thought reasoning traces, a representative model’s accuracy increases from 81.1% to 85.6% on held-out V-FiLLM problems and achieves about 5 percentage-point improvement over the base model on the external FinQA benchmark.

Conclusion: V-FiLLM provides a scalable, automatically verifiable way to build financial reasoning benchmarks over structured data with fine-grained control of difficulty. The observed accuracy and robustness drops under deeper reasoning and adversarial perturbations reveal that current LLMs still struggle with complex, compositional financial reasoning. However, the gains from lightweight LoRA fine-tuning on high-quality reasoning traces suggest that targeted, low-cost adaptation can significantly improve financial QA performance and is a promising avenue for developing more capable and robust models in this domain.

Abstract: While existing benchmarks have made substantial progress in evaluating LLMs across STEM domains, financial reasoning over structured data remains comparatively less explored. We introduce V-FiLLM, a framework that generates financial reasoning benchmarks from executable computation trees grounded in real tables, yielding items whose answers are correct by construction. Trees are evaluated symbolically to obtain ground truth and rendered into natural-language questions, removing any model from the labeling loop, so items can be generated at arbitrary scale without annotation cost and without inheriting a generator's error rate. V-FiLLM exposes four independently controllable axes of difficulty including computation depth, expression breadth, financial concept complexity, and context size. By evaluating on open-source models, we find that accuracy falls up to 51% as reasoning depth increases, and up to 47% points under adversarial numerical perturbations, highlighting remaining challenges in robust financial reasoning over tables. We further show that lightweight LoRA fine-tuning on verified chain-of-thought traces improves accuracy from 81.1% to 85.6% on held-out problems and outperforms the base model by 5% points on FinQA (Chen et al., 2022a), s), suggesting that targeted, low-cost adaptation is a promising direction for compositional reasoning in financial QA.

</details>


### [133] [SkillZip: Evaluation-Free Skill Compression for Self-Evolving Agents by Discovering Reusable Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11079)
*Xiaofan Bai,Hongqiang Lin,Chao Liu,Yantao Zhang,Xuan Jin,Xipeng Cao,Yuhong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillZip is a method to structurally compress self-evolving agent skills by finding the shortest faithful explanation of their rules, workflows, and contracts, without using evaluation rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agents accumulate large, redundant skills as similar requirements and action sequences are repeatedly restated, making skills costly to inject and hard to maintain. Existing generic prompt compression treats skills as flat text and ignores their structured constraints, while evaluation-guided compression is expensive, requires rollouts, and depends on a specific evaluation set. A method is needed that compresses skills while preserving their structured behavior and constraints, without requiring evaluation.

Method: SkillZip formulates skill compression as finding the shortest faithful structural explanation of a skill under a typed minimum description-length objective. It treats a skill as a contract plus a residual and enforces a hard coverage constraint over triggers, workflow edges, tool requirements, obligations, and output fields. The method factors out repeated rules at the broadest applicable scope, abstracts common action sequences into shared procedures, and keeps differences as explicit exceptions. It provides sharing thresholds, preserves rare unique rules, and allows efficient local updates. It supports a one-shot mode with a single structured extraction and deterministic optimization, and a continual Zip-on-Write mode that integrates each new self-evolution patch without replaying past tasks or reparsing history.

Result: Experiments show that SkillZip achieves better compression ratios, maintains or improves generalization, and incurs lower or acceptable cost overhead compared to baselines such as generic prompt compression and evaluation-based approaches.

Conclusion: SkillZip effectively compresses structured skills for self-evolving agents by exploiting their internal structure rather than treating them as flat prompts, preserving behavioral coverage without evaluation rollouts, supporting continual updates, and empirically outperforming existing methods in compression, generalizability, and cost.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents accumulate reusable skills by appending successful procedures and failure fixes. Over time, the same requirement is often restated in several branches, examples, and warnings, while common action sequences are copied rather than reused. The resulting skill becomes expensive to inject and difficult to maintain. Generic prompt compression is ill-suited to this setting because a skill is not a flat passage: its name and description define when it applies, its workflow controls execution, its tool and output contracts constrain validity, and rare exceptions may remain essential even when no sampled task activates them. Evaluation-guided compression can test these behaviors, but it introduces rollouts, cost, and dependence on the compression-time evaluation set. We present SkillZip, an evaluation-free method that compresses a skill by finding its shortest faithful structural explanation. The intuition is explain once, reference many: state a repeated rule once at the scope where it applies, factor a repeated action sequence into a shared procedure, and keep only the differences as explicit exceptions. We formalize this intuition as a typed minimum description-length objective over a skill contract and a residual, subject to a hard coverage constraint for every extracted trigger, workflow edge, tool requirement, obligation, and output field. The formulation provides simple sharing thresholds, preserves unique rare rules by construction, and supports efficient local updates. SkillZip has a one-shot mode with one structured extraction call and deterministic optimization, and a continual Zip-on-Write mode that integrates each self-evolution patch without replaying tasks or reparsing the full history. Through comprehensive experimental evaluations, we demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of SkillZip in compression performance, generalizability, and cost overhead.

</details>


### [134] [RTSKG: Building a Rail Transit Station Knowledge Graph Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11080)
*Shutong Zhu,Tianxing Wu,Runfeng Liu,Yuang Gu,Xuan He,Yuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build and release a Rail Transit Station Knowledge Graph (RTSKG) that links stations with surrounding urban entities, and show it improves station-related prediction/recommendation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: City-scale tasks like ridership prediction around rail transit stations need rich, large-scale urban data. Existing work typically organizes data in simple forms and ignores the complex spatial and semantic interactions among diverse urban entities (stations, roads, POIs, etc.), limiting model performance and generalizability.

Method: They design a unified schema for urban entities related to rail transit stations and construct RTSKG, a knowledge graph that integrates heterogeneous data such as rail stations, road segments, and points of interest while explicitly encoding spatial and semantic relations among them. They publish it as Linked Data (with a persistent URL) so it can be accessed and reused. They then plug RTSKG into downstream models for two tasks: station-area store recommendation and knowledge-enhanced ridership prediction, to test its utility.

Result: Using RTSKG as structured prior knowledge improves the performance of models on both station-area store recommendation and ridership prediction compared with baselines that do not use this knowledge graph, indicating that explicit modeling of station-related urban interactions is beneficial.

Conclusion: RTSKG effectively captures and exposes the spatial and semantic relationships between rail stations and surrounding urban entities, and this structured representation boosts the performance of city-level rail-transit-related tasks.
