The dataset, released as Linked Data, has the potential to serve as a reusable infrastructure for broader analyses and applications involving rail transit stations.

Abstract: Rail transit systems play a vital role in urban mobility and economic development. As key components of such systems, rail transit stations function as critical transport hubs that enhance urban accessibility and stimulate development in surrounding areas. City-level rail transit station related tasks (e.g., ridership prediction) require large-scale urban data, but current studies often neglect complex interactions among various urban entities in terms of data organization. In this paper, to address the above issue, we build a Rail Transit Station Knowledge Graph (RTSKG) dataset which explicitly models the spatial and semantic interactions among different kinds of urban entities, to benefit city-level rail transit station related tasks. RTSKG integrates heterogeneous urban entities, such as rail transit stations, road segments, and points of interest, with a specially designed unified schema, and is accessible as Linked Data at https://w3id.org/rtskg/. Evaluations on station-area store recommendation and knowledge-enhanced ridership prediction demonstrate the effectiveness of RTSKG, highlighting its potential to support city-level rail transit station analysis.

</details>


### [135] [Why Does CLAUDE.md Keep Growing? Catastrophic Remembering in Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11095)
*Kushal Chakrabarti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates why agentic coding README prompts (like CLAUDE.md) grow without bound, identifying a phenomenon called 'catastrophic remembering', and shows that adding explanatory prompt comments can drastically reduce redundant instructions and improve instruction-following performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic code repositories use growing instruction files (e.g., CLAUDE.md) to guide LLM-based agents, but these files tend to expand endlessly, becoming cluttered and inefficient. The authors observe that instructions are easy to append but hard to safely delete once their rationale is forgotten, causing unbounded prompt growth. They aim to formally characterize this growth, understand its dynamics, and find mechanisms to control it without sacrificing correctness or performance.

Method: 1) Empirical analysis: They study 247,694 instruction lifetimes across 1,867 real repositories, tracking how many instructions are added/removed and modeling deletion likelihood over time (via a hazard/log-hazard analysis). 2) Theoretical framing: They formalize the cost of safely deleting instructions under imperfect recall as exponential in the number of instructions, and define the concept of 'catastrophic remembering' as a mirror to catastrophic forgetting in continual learning. 3) Synthetic benchmark via IFEval inversion: They invert IFEval to create verifiable environments where the optimal prompt is known, allowing them to test how adding comments encoding latent reasoning affects prompt size and redundancy. 4) Real-world evaluation via WildIFEval inversion: They apply the same commenting strategy to more realistic agentic instruction-following tasks to measure performance gains.

Result: 1) Prompt growth characterization: Agentic prompts more than triple in size over their lifetime (+226%), with a net gain of about +4.9 instructions per commit. Older instructions are increasingly unlikely to be removed, with a deletion log-hazard of -0.032 per commit, consistent with 'catastrophic remembering'. 2) Synthetic-world intervention: In inverted IFEval settings where the optimal prompt is known, adding comments that encode the underlying reasoning reduces excess instructions by 99.3%, shrinking growth from +211.3% to +1.4%. 3) Real-world intervention: Applying the same commenting strategy in WildIFEval improves real-world agentic instruction-following performance by up to 23.1%.

Conclusion: Unbounded growth of agentic README prompts is driven by an asymmetry between cheap insertion and expensive, risky deletion under imperfect recall, leading to 'catastrophic remembering'. This makes prompts increasingly bloated and hard to manage over time. Introducing structured comments that capture the latent reasoning and rationale behind instructions serves as an effective control mechanism: it enables safe pruning of redundant or obsolete instructions, nearly eliminating excess growth in controlled settings and significantly improving performance in realistic tasks. The paper argues that if natural language prompts are treated like code, they should also be documented with comments to maintain clarity, correctness, and sustainability over the lifecycle of agentic systems.

Abstract: Agentic coding READMEs like CLAUDE.md grow without bound in real repositories, stopping only when the repository retires or someone rewrites the file wholesale. We trace this to imperfect recall: appending an instruction is always cheap, but once an instruction's rationale is gone, deleting it without risking a correctness regression costs O(2^|D|) in a prompt of |D| instructions. We name the resulting divergence catastrophic remembering, the inverse of catastrophic forgetting around which continual learning is organized. First, we characterize this phenomenon across 247,694 instruction lifetimes in 1,867 repositories: agentic prompts grow without bound, more than tripling over their lifetime (+226%), gaining +4.9 net instructions every commit; further, the older an instruction gets, the less likely it is to be deleted (log-hazard -0.032/commit). Then, we show that prompt comments can halt the growth: inverting IFEval yields verifiable worlds whose optimal prompts are known, and there comments encoding latent reasoning remove 99.3% of excess instructions (+211.3% to +1.4%). Finally, applying the same inversion to WildIFEval, we show that prompt comments can improve real-world agentic instruction-following by up to 23.1%. If English is the new code, why don't we have comments yet?

</details>


### [136] [sLTN: Structural Logic Tensor Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11136)
*Davide Rinaldi,Luciano Serafini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends Logic Tensor Networks (LTN) with explicit structural dimensions (sLTN) so that temporal, sequential, and graph-structured constraints can be expressed and learned within the same neurosymbolic framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Original LTNs can integrate first-order logic with differentiable learning, but they mainly handle flat sets of individuals and do not natively encode structural organization such as time, sequence, or graph relations. Many real-world reasoning tasks require such structure, so there is a need to generalize LTNs to explicitly model and reason about these dimensions within the logic itself.

Method: The authors introduce sLTN, which augments the LTN language with structural dimensions—named tensor axes that correspond to domain-specific organizational structures (e.g., time steps, sequence positions, graph nodes). These dimensions can be quantified over, linked via structural relations, and used in logical formulas. They define the syntax and fuzzy tensor semantics of sLTN, prove that it collapses to standard LTN when no structural dimensions are used, and implement the framework in PyTorch with a declarative signature, formula parser, and tensor interpreter.

Result: The paper provides a formal semantics for sLTN, a proof that it generalizes classical LTN, and an operational PyTorch implementation. Using illustrative temporal and sequential reasoning examples, they demonstrate how sLTN can directly express and enforce structural constraints that would be cumbersome or impossible in the original LTN formalism.

Conclusion: sLTN generalizes Logic Tensor Networks by treating structural organization as first-class logical entities, enabling more natural and expressive neurosymbolic reasoning over temporal, sequential, and relational data. The released sltn library makes this framework practically usable for tasks that require structured reasoning within a differentiable environment.

Abstract: Logic Tensor Networks (LTN) provide a neurosymbolic framework in which first-order logic is interpreted through tensor operations, enabling logical constraints to be integrated with differentiable learning. However, the original formulation of LTN is primarily suited to data represented as flat collections of individuals, and does not explicitly capture structural organization such as temporal order, sequential position, or graph connectivity. We introduce sLTN, an extension of LTN that makes structural dimensions first-class elements of the language. Structural dimensions represent named tensor axes associated with domain-specific organization, such as time steps, sequence positions, or graph nodes. They can be quantified explicitly, related through structural relations, and used to express temporal, sequential, and relational constraints directly at the logical level. We formalize the syntax and fuzzy tensor semantics of sLTN and show that, in the absence of structural dimensions, the framework recovers the original LTN semantics as a special case. We further describe a PyTorch implementation based on a declarative signature, formula parsing, and tensorial interpretation. The framework is illustrated on representative temporal and sequential reasoning examples. This paper serves as a companion to the sltn library, available at https://github.com/logictensornetworks/sltn.

</details>


### [137] [Long-Horizon AI Research for Grothendieck Constant: A Case Study in Human-AI Mathematical Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11195)
*Alan Li,Rahul Saha,Anton Xue,Swarat Chaudhuri,Adam Klivans,Pravesh K Kothari,Raghu Meka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper is a case study on how an AI research system helped improve the best known bounds on the Grothendieck constant, illustrating concrete ways AI can contribute novel insights in advanced mathematics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although AI agents are increasingly applied to mathematical research, there is little concrete understanding of how to use them effectively, especially for deep theoretical problems. The Grothendieck constant is a central quantity capturing the gap between discrete optimization problems and their continuous relaxations, but its exact value is unknown and prior bounds left a significant uncertainty. The authors are motivated both by the mathematical challenge of tightening these bounds and by the meta‑scientific goal of documenting how AI can be productively integrated into serious mathematical discovery.

Method: The authors employ an AI research system as a collaborator in a research program targeting better bounds on the Grothendieck constant. The system is used iteratively to explore ideas, suggest candidate approaches, refine arguments, and help search the space of possible proof techniques, with human experts critically evaluating, guiding, and formalizing the AI’s proposals. The paper documents this process qualitatively, identifying patterns of interaction, types of prompts, and working conditions that made AI contributions most fruitful, as well as where it fell short.

Result: Using this human–AI collaborative workflow, the authors obtain improved bounds on the Grothendieck constant: they show that it lies between 6π/11 and π / (2 log(1+√2)) − 10^{-4}. These bounds strictly tighten previously known estimates, and domain experts judge some of the AI-suggested ideas that led to them as genuinely novel. The paper additionally distills a set of observed strengths and weaknesses of the AI system in this context.

Conclusion: The case study demonstrates that, under carefully designed conditions and with strong human oversight, AI agents can make nontrivial, sometimes novel contributions to cutting-edge mathematical research. The improved bounds on the Grothendieck constant serve as a concrete mathematical outcome, while the documented workflow offers guidance on how to structure future collaborations between mathematicians and AI systems to maximize the chance of breakthrough insights and to mitigate current limitations.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly used in mathematics research, but it is often unclear how to use them effectively. Towards this, we present an extensive case study of how AI was used to improve bounds on the Grothendieck constant $K_G$, which captures the hardness between combinatorial problems and their continuous relaxations. Specifically, while the precise value of $K_G$ is not known, we recently tightened the best known bounds to \[
  \frac{6π}{11}
  \;\le\;
  K_G
  \;\le\;
  \fracπ{2\log(1+\sqrt2)} - 10^{-4}. \] Crucially, these improvements were achieved using an AI research system that could arrive at insights deemed novel by domain experts. We give a detailed discussion of our experience using AI for mathematics research, particularly touching upon its strengths and weaknesses, as well as our experience with creating ideal conditions for AI to arrive at breakthrough insights.

</details>
